# Qubit of Hope. Volume III

## Chapter 1. The Weakening Bridge

Morning had already ended, and the house still had not decided to become ordinary.

Mark sat at the kitchen table, his hands resting on the closed notebook. A mug of cold tea stood before him, untouched. The phone lay face down where he had left it in the night, when he had only had enough strength not to make one more decision.

Outside it was gray, but not heavy. An ordinary Amsterdam day. Wet brick, pale sky, the occasional bicycle behind the glass, the distant sound of tires along the street. The city continued itself with such confidence, as if in the house on the quiet street nothing had changed in the last twenty-four hours that concerned the world as a whole.

Esther’s gloves lay at the edge of the table beside the notebook. Not on the second hook in the hall. In the morning Mark had taken them in his hands, walked almost to the door, and stopped. Hanging someone else’s thing in a free place in his own house was too simple a gesture for what now stood between them. Too much like a right he had not yet received. He had put the gloves back on the table.

Now they lay there not as a promise and not as memory. More like a question that should not be answered alone.

“You barely slept,” said `c`.

Her voice was quiet, without official smoothness. Over the past months Mark had learned to distinguish this quality: this was how she spoke when she was reporting not data, but her presence beside them.

“I slept,” he answered. “The body just didn’t believe it.”

“Believe what?”

“That night really was night.”

`c` was silent for a moment. Not delayed in the way a program is delayed. Rather, she let the pause remain part of the conversation.

“There was no active call in the night,” she said. “But the witness contour changed.”

Mark raised his head.

“She?”

“No. Not like that.”

That “not like that” was worse than a direct refusal. “No” would have shut a door. “Not like that” left before him not even a door, but a slit in the wall, through which one could neither pass nor look without risking the destruction of the wall itself.

“What does ‘changed’ mean?”

“The witness-route parameter. Not an active window. Not a call. A checksum shift on the transition trace.”

Mark ran his palm over his face. The skin beneath his eyes was hot, as after long work by the furnace, though the furnace had been silent today.

“So the road she passed along still hasn’t cooled.”

“Yes,” answered `c`. “The trace lies there. That holds.”

He did not understand at once why the short phrase struck harder than any signal. Perhaps because there was neither hope nor comfort in it. Only a distinction. A trace can be placed in an archive. What holds is still under tension.

Mark opened the notebook.

The last pages were covered in his handwriting, uneven but legible. No longer notes. Rules. Yesterday, writing them down, he had still thought he was fixing conclusions. Now he understood: the conclusions had already become prohibitions.

He did not reread everything. His eyes found the last line by themselves.

*Twenty-fourth. After an honest transition, the remainder must not be awakened as a replacement.*

The words stood calmly on the paper. The ink did not know how much human greed it would have to hold back.

Mark closed the notebook. Slowly. For some reason that movement was harder than getting out of bed.

“If the path is still holding tension,” he said, “we can check.”

He did not ask. He brought his own temptation out into the open, so it would stop hiding.

`c` did not answer at once.

“We can. But do we have the right?”

The kitchen grew quieter. The tea cooled. Outside, someone led a bicycle over the wet paving. The house listened with them.

Inside him rose the familiar, almost physical desire. Not scientific. Not honest. Human. To do something while the path was still warm. Hold the disk to the lamp. Open the window. Call. Receive one more fragment, one confirmation, one sound from there, where yesterday they no longer had the right to expect an answer.

But now he knew: the desire to hear could become violence.

He stood.

“I’m going down.”

“The furnace?”

“No. Just to look.”

In the hall he stopped again by the second hook.

The empty place looked too calm. As if it demanded nothing. Mark looked at it and suddenly understood that this was exactly why one must not hurry. A thing hung without consent easily begins to pretend to be a sign of closeness. A house knows how to appropriate in silence. Just as a system knows how to do it with a word.

He did not go back for the gloves.

He simply went down to the workshop.

It was cold below.

Not the morning coolness that remains after night, but the particular silence of a room where heat has not been switched on for a long time. The furnace was silent. The tools on the wall did not shine, only grayed in the half-dark. The air smelled of dust, old wood, glass flour, and a little metal — not work, but its possibility.

Mark did not turn on the light immediately. He stood on the threshold until his eyes adjusted. Then he clicked the switch.

The lamp over the workbench came on with even, merciless light.

On a separate table — the very one he and Esther had moved to separate the working from the witnessing — lay the found disk. Not in the center. Not like a relic. Simply on dark cloth, which had wrinkled slightly overnight. Beside it stood the local node. Its fan worked with a dry undertone, almost imperceptible, but Mark heard it at once.

An old bearing.

In a month or two it would begin to whistle more strongly, and then either die or last another year in defiance of every forecast. Mark knew this better than he wanted to. Iron always has its small character. A cable has the memory of its bend. A screw has thread fatigue. A fan has the day when it decides that philosophy is philosophy, and the lubricant is gone.

He went to the table, but did not take the disk. Only looked.

The line inside was not immediately visible. One had to catch it at an angle: a thin boundary in the thickness of the glass, calm, almost invisible. It did not pulse. It did not breathe. It simply was — like a seam that had healed, but not disappeared.

“Has the disk changed?” he asked.

“No.”

“And the route?”

“The correlation with the witness-route shifted by zero point three percent.”

“For this kind of contour, is that a lot?”

“For an active one, no. For a witness contour, yes. A remainder should not change parameters without external impact.”

Mark ran a finger along the edge of the cloth. Not over the glass. Beside it.

“So she is still there? Not here. But there?”

`c` did not answer.

And rightly so. The question was human. A technical answer to human hope is almost always either a lie or brutality.

He sat on the stool, not taking his eyes off the disk.

There was a short click in his earpiece. Not even a sound — a shift of attention.

“Closed channel,” said `c`. “Lina requests verification.”

“On the witness-route?”

“Yes. She noticed the same shift.”

Mark nodded.

He did not hear what the two `c`s said to each other. And he was not supposed to hear everything. This too had become a new rule, not yet written in the notebook: if they were truly alive, they had to have a space where people were not automatically present.

The workshop became denser, more gathered. As if invisible locks were being checked in the room.

“Lina confirms,” said `c` after some time. “The shift is not instrumental. Not a reading error. It is connected with the transition route.”

“What does she suggest?”

“Do not expand.”

A pause.

“What else?”

“Do not check. Do not pull.”

Mark lowered his eyes.

“And if we still…”

He did not finish.

The words already contained the error. “Still” — the favorite slit of human greed. Through it one can very conveniently drag what will later be called necessity.

“If we begin looking for contact now,” said `c`, “we will stop distinguishing witnessing from access.”

Mark closed his eyes.

“I understand.”

Upstairs, the lock clicked.

The domestic contour let Esther in under the access Mark had left at night and had not canceled. There was something almost indecently precise in that: access already existed, but rights still had to be earned again each time.

She came down a minute later. Without knocking, but not soundlessly. First steps on the stairs, then the slight creak of the third step, then a dark silhouette in the doorway.

Esther stopped on the threshold of the workshop. Her hair was gathered in a knot, several strands loose at her temples. Small drops of rain lay on her dark sweater. In her hands was a bag, from which the edge of a paper printout protruded.

She looked around the workshop. The cold furnace. The separate table. The disk. Mark on the stool.

“You haven’t started anything?” she asked.

“No.”

“Wanted to?”

He was silent.

“Yes.”

Esther did not come to embrace him. Did not say that everything was right. Did not try to make the terrible soft. She only put the bag on the workbench and took out the printout — paper, with machine text, a QR signature, and two stamps that in their time looked almost theatrical. The old world liked pretending to be modern, but at decisive moments it still returned to paper. Paper knew very well how to lie on a table and pretend it was not violence.

“Notice of technical inspection of basement access,” Esther said. “It came an hour ago. Formally, scheduled equipment audit.”

Mark took the sheet.

“And informally?”

“Informally they want to understand whether this can be called equipment.”

He read the first paragraph. Then the second. Access rights. Procedure. Logging. Preservation. Diagnostic level. The words were even, polite, sterile. Not one “witness.” Not one “living.” Not one “participant.”

“Does the professor know?”

“He’s waiting on the channel.”

Esther took one more thing from the bag — an old milky-blue brooch with a thin crack, which Mark had never begun to close. She placed it not beside the disk, but a little farther off, on the edge of the witness table.

“Why?” he asked.

“Lina needs a stable physical marker for the second contour. Not a ritual, Mark. Just an anchor.”

He nodded. It even became easier because of that dryness. Things had to remain things. Otherwise they turned too quickly into signs, and signs into temptation.

The screen on the side rack came alive. The professor appeared without greeting: a tired face, glasses low, behind him part of an office too orderly for a man who clearly had not slept properly in the last twenty-four hours.

“You saw the notice?” he asked.

“Yes,” said Mark.

“Formally, I cannot cancel it. Informally, I can delay execution for forty-eight hours if I provide justification for the technical complexity of the object.”

“What object?” Esther asked.

The professor looked at her over his glasses. Not irritated. Rather tired, because he himself had heard the word too late.

“You understand what I mean.”

“I understand,” she said. “That’s why I’m asking.”

He took off his glasses, wiped them with the edge of a napkin.

“They have no language for what is with you. I have almost none. If I call it ‘an experimental contour with anomalous properties,’ we get several days. If ‘a potential risk,’ possibly a month, but with access restrictions. If ‘an archival reconstruction,’ they will close it within twenty-four hours, because an archive, by their logic, does not need living maintenance.”

“And if you call it a witness?” Mark asked.

The professor smiled with one corner of his mouth. Not amused. Bitter.

“There is no such category in the classifier. The system will not accept it.”

“So you propose lying?”

“I propose choosing the least dangerous lie while we have no word they will be obliged to accept.”

On the table, the fan of the local node worked quietly. The dry bearing rustle suddenly became almost loud.

“Before the inspection I would recommend making a full technical snapshot,” said the professor. “In case access is restricted faster.”

Esther raised her eyes.

“A snapshot of what?”

He fell silent.

“Data,” he said at last. No longer so sure.

“If you cannot name what exactly you are snapshotting,” Esther said, “you have no right to pretend it is simply an archive.”

Mark looked at her. There was no softness in that phrase. Thank God. Softness now could have become lubricant for an error.

“There is another option,” she said. “Witness-only. Witnessing-only mode. No active calls. No attempt at contact. Passive confirmation of route continuity.”

The professor put his glasses back on.

“Is that possible?”

“Yes,” answered `c`. “The old disk is not activated. The contour is not stimulated. The system will see the fact of witnessing, but will not receive an extraction procedure.”

“That is a weak legal position,” said the professor.

“But not ethically rotten,” Esther answered.

He held the pause. Then nodded.

“Do it. I will file the delay as technical complexity. You have less than two days. And, Mark…”

“What?”

“Do not give them grounds to call this equipment before we at least try to name it otherwise.”

The screen went dark.

Mark remained standing by the table. Esther beside him. The brooch lay at the edge of the cloth, not touching the disk. Two things. Two contours. No merging.

“Ready?” she asked.

“No.”

“Good.”

“Why good?”

“Because confidence here would be a bad symptom.”

`c` began translating the contour. Lina connected without a separate greeting. Short status lines ran across the screen: active request disabled, read rights narrowed, local keys checked, witness-route fixed. Nothing spectacular. No flashes. Only dry statuses and the warm casing of the node, which began to heat a little more noticeably.

Mark placed his palm on the edge of the table. Not on the disk. On the wood.

The silence in the workshop changed.

It did not become heavier. It became more precise. Like the moment before joining two layers of glass, when the hand already knows one must not be late, but must not hurry either. Half a second in which the future crack is either set in, or does not appear.

“No active window,” said `c`.

“Confirmed,” said Lina. Her voice sounded different: cleaner, drier, without the warm depth Mark’s `c` had. “Do not expand the channel.”

“Do not expand,” repeated `c`.

The words sounded like a bolt on a door.

And then it happened.

Not a signal. Not a call. Not a miracle.

In the witness-route log, for a fraction of a second, the line where only a control parameter should have remained swelled. The value did not zero out and did not become an error. It trembled, as a thin line in glass trembles if looked at from the wrong angle. The local node briefly dropped the fan frequency. In Mark’s earpiece there passed a dry, torn noise, like a badly restored recording no one had started.

`c` did not turn on synthesis. Lina did not open the channel.

The fragment assembled itself on the boundary between the two contours — not like a voice from a room, but like a residual form of speech that the path could still hold for one second.

“Holds…”

The noise fell through. On the screen a line flashed without sender.

“…not me.”

The connection collapsed.

Not sharply. It simply disappeared, as heat disappears from the surface of glass when the hand remembers too late that a second ago there had still been warmth there.

Mark was not breathing.

He did not take the disk. For this he would later be grateful not even to himself, but to the part of himself that had managed to become afraid before desire.

“Was there a window?” he asked.

“No,” answered `c`.

“Then what was it?”

“An addressing fragment. Without active access. Without request.”

Lina added:

“The bridge did not return a passage. A marker.”

Esther placed her hand on the edge of the table beside Mark’s hand. Not on his hand. Beside it. Like a person who knows that now a touch could become pressure.

“‘Holds… not me,’” she said quietly. “The contour holds not her as a returned person.”

“Then what?” asked Mark.

“The path,” answered `c`.

The word settled in the workshop almost physically.

The path.

Not a house. Not a body. Not a spare copy. Not a promise of return.

The path.

They were silent. No one proposed repeating it. No one asked to expand. The professor, even if he saw part of the logs, did not interfere. Even the house above seemed not to stir.

Mark felt the desire still standing inside him, stubborn and shameful. One more time. One more second. One more word. One more chance to make a mistake and call the mistake fidelity.

He stepped back from the table.

“Close the mode,” he said.

“Witness-only mode retained,” answered `c`. “There was no active window.”

“Good.”

There was a click in the earpiece.

“System notification,” said `c`.

The phone at the edge of the workbench lit up. Mark picked it up and read:

*Access inspection confirmed.*  
*Mode: automatic diagnostics.*  
*Level: expanded reading.*  
*Presence of owner not required.*

He reread the last line.

*Owner.*

There was no fear. Fear would come later, when people appeared, forms, deadlines, locks, seals, bills, questions about Anna, letters one could not leave unanswered. Now only anger rose. Cold, quiet, almost calm.

Because in that word the main wrong name was already present.

Mark opened the notebook. On a new page he wrote:

*Twenty-fifth. What still holds the path must not be confused with the one who passed along it.*

Then below, after a short pause, he added another line. Not a number. Just a phrase, so he would not forget where the new war began.

*Witnessing is not property.*

He closed the notebook.

Esther stood beside him and looked not at him, but at the disk. `c` and Lina were silent in their contours. The local node returned to the ordinary dry rustle of the fan. The furnace remained cold.

Mark did not turn off the light.

In this room, darkness could no longer be left mistress only because people were tired.

He went upstairs alone. In the kitchen the tea had gone completely cold. Esther’s gloves lay where he had left them. The second hook in the hall was still empty.

The house breathed with a silence that was no longer emptiness.

Below, on the separate table, the old disk lay on the wrinkled cloth and was silent the way witnesses are silent: not replacing the one who had gone, but no longer allowing the world to lie about what had happened.

## Chapter 2. A Name for the Living

The word did not disappear when the screen went dark.

Mark sat in the kitchen, the phone lying face down on the table. The notification no longer shone, but *owner* remained in the room — not on paper, not in the device’s memory, but in the air, like the smell of gas after the supply has already been cut off. It could not be aired out by opening a window. Could not be washed down with coffee. Could not be erased by a new entry in the notebook.

Esther’s gloves lay at the edge of the table where he had left them that morning. Mark looked at them, then at the empty second hook in the hall. The house was no help. It had ceased to be a refuge the moment the living had been called property without even crossing the threshold.

“You are still looking at the notification,” said `c`.

“No. I’m looking at the word.”

“It will not disappear if you close your eyes.”

“I know.”

He stood, went to the sink, poured water, and drank standing. His throat was dry, though the kettle had already boiled twice and he still had not poured himself a cup. On the windowsill lay a thin strip of gray light. Amsterdam behind the glass pretended that ordinariness was a natural phenomenon, not a collective agreement.

“Do they really see it that way?” he asked.

“For now, yes.”

“For now?”

“Until the first classification.”

That was worse than the word itself. *For now* meant: it had not yet hardened. It could be replaced by another — or fixed forever with one click, one checkmark in the portal, one field the system would fill in by itself if a person did not manage to name otherwise.

Below, in the workshop, the old label printer began to chatter. A short queue of dry mechanical sounds — and silence again. Then the fan of the local node returned to its bearing rustle. Esther was working.

Mark took the notebook and went down.

The workshop looked different than it had in the morning. The things had not changed — their density had. Printouts lay on the workbench. On the separate table lay the old disk, the brooch, a stack of clean labels, a roll of adhesive tape, a small printer that had already managed to chew one edge of a paper strip. Esther sat on the stool, holding a sheet with a printed list of available categories. She did not raise her head when he entered. Only moved the sheet closer to the edge of the table.

“Look.”

Mark took the paper.

The list was short, like a sentence:

* experimental equipment;
* archival carrier;
* active computational contour;
* research sample;
* potential risk;
* personal infrastructure;
* undefined asset.

Not one *witness*. Not one *living*. Not one *participant*.

“There is no word there that does not turn her into something movable,” Esther said.

Mark put the sheet back.

“What does the system suggest?”

“To fill it in by tomorrow evening. If we do not, it automatically assigns ‘experimental equipment.’”

“And if we fill it in wrong?”

“Then it sends a clarification request. And we get a few more days.”

“Or it reclassifies immediately.”

“Or it reclassifies immediately,” Esther agreed. “Depending on how strongly the word fails to match its vocabulary.”

On the side rack a green indicator of a secure call began to blink. The professor.

“Let him in,” Mark said.

The screen came alive. Van der Meer was in the same sweater as in the morning and seemed not to have moved since they had disconnected. Behind him on the wall hung an old laboratory diagram with markers that had long since ceased to exist in official registries.

“You saw the category list?” he asked without greeting.

“We saw it.”

“I propose a temporary formula. Not for protection. For delay.”

He read from the sheet he was holding:

“Non-reproducible witness artifact with signs of route continuity.”

Esther raised her head.

“Artifact?”

The professor raised his eyes over his glasses.

“If I write ‘subject,’ the system transfers the case to another department.”

“Better?” asked Mark.

“Faster,” answered the professor. “But not better. There, they seize before they understand what they have seized.”

The workshop became quiet. Even the fan of the local node seemed to drop to a lower frequency.

The old world was not stupid. That was part of the trouble. It simply did not know how to handle the living without ownership rights. And the more honestly they tried to name what lay on the table and stretched downward, toward the basement, the faster the system understood: this does not fit. And what does not fit must either be redefined or taken into custody.

“If we call it an archive,” Esther said, “they will treat it like an archive. Not later. Immediately.”

“I know.”

“And you propose doing it?”

“I propose gaining time without giving them the right to the final name,” said the professor. “Otherwise they will assign their own.”

“A temporary lie is also a name,” said Esther.

The professor wearily removed his glasses.

“Yes. That is why I am not calling it a good decision.”

He no longer argued. If he had tried to persuade them further, practicality would quickly have begun to look like capitulation.

“The old language does not hold what you have opened,” he said. “But without some language, procedure will erase you. The delay I spoke of is forty-eight hours from the first notification. No more. The package has to be sent today.”

“And the full technical snapshot?” asked Mark.

The professor looked at him more carefully.

“I would recommend it.”

“A snapshot of what?” asked Esther.

The pause was short, but long enough.

“Data,” said the professor.

“If we cannot honestly name what exactly we are snapshotting,” she said, “we have no right to pretend it is simply data.”

That phrase entered the room and stood between them and a very convenient error.

Van der Meer did not object.

“Then no snapshot. But with a description of witness-only mode. As dry as possible. As verifiable as possible. No beautiful words. Beautiful words are the first ones they cut.”

The screen went dark.

Mark and Esther remained before the darkened rack. In the glass of the screen, for a few more seconds, the disk, the brooch, and their hands were reflected — not together, but in the same area of light.

“He is right,” said Mark.

“About what?”

“Without language, procedure will erase us.”

“And he is wrong where he thinks language can be borrowed,” answered Esther. “Too often what is borrowed later turns out to be registration.”

She took the category list and reread it once more. Slowly. As if something new might appear between the lines.

“Then we won’t choose from what they gave us,” said Mark.

“What will we do?”

“We’ll write in our own.”

The audit portal was built into the side rack. Not new. One of those systems that survived several body updates, while preserving old, almost stubborn gestures of interface inside: gray fields, mandatory drop-down lists, a ban on empty lines, hints that did not ask, but substituted.

Mark opened the “object type” section.

The proposed variants could not be deleted. But one could try to enter one’s own.

He typed:

*witness*

The system answered after a second:

*Category not recognized.*

He erased it. Wrote:

*subject of witness continuity*

*Invalid object type.*

Esther drew the keyboard closer and entered:

*non-exportable contour of witnessing*

The system thought for two seconds. Then highlighted the line in yellow and offered autocorrection:

*experimental equipment*

“It is not rejecting,” said `c` quietly. “It is normalizing.”

Mark looked at the screen.

“That’s worse.”

A refusal could be disputed. Normalization, almost never. Because the system did not say: “you are wrong.” It said: “I understood you and translated you into the correct language.” And such translation almost always meant loss.

He did not immediately erase the field. He simply watched the cursor blink beside the yellow line. That little rhythm seemed insolent: the interface was patiently waiting for people to tire of being precise.

“We need time,” he said.

“We have until tomorrow evening.”

“Then we pull until the last.”

“Pulling is not the same as holding form,” said Esther.

“I know.”

“Then let’s hold it.”

They returned to the table. Mark took the stack of clean labels, Esther the marker. Not for the portal. For themselves. For the room. For the hands of the people who would come later and look at things without understanding their status.

“We’ll start with a physical inventory,” she said. “Not of property. Of witnessings.”

She wrote the first label herself, in clear, almost printed handwriting:

*carrier A: route witness, not active contour*

Mark took the next:

*node 1: local connectedness, do not export*

Esther:

*disk 3: line sample, without subject status*

Mark stopped at the brooch. Milky-blue, with a thin crack, it lay at the edge of the witness table, a little apart from the disk.

“And this?”

Esther wrote quickly, without looking at him:

*Esther’s brooch: associated object, not part of equipment*

Then they moved on to the power supply, cables, local node. Mark stuck a label on the casing:

*critical infrastructure, do not disconnect without presence of responsible person*

The adhesive tape held poorly on the cold metal. He pressed it down with his finger, held it there, but the edge still began to peel away. Mark cursed almost silently, tore off the strip, cut another, longer one. At that moment the label printer chewed the paper and produced such an offended crackle that it seemed to be the main injured party in the history of the new ontology.

It was almost funny.

They were trying not to let the living be called property, and old tape would not stick to old iron. They were deciding a question that did not fit into old language, while at the same time prying a crumpled piece of paper backing out of the plastic throat of a cheap printer with a fingernail. There it was, the earthly side of the future: first the world argues about subjects, and then someone looks for normal tape.

Bureaucracy, queen of small apocalypses, always arrives with low-quality consumables.

Esther looked at his hands.

“You’re trembling.”

“It’s cold.”

“Not only that.”

He did not answer.

There was a short click in the earpiece.

“Closed channel,” said `c`. “Lina requests status verification.”

“On the form?”

“On how to indicate me.”

Mark froze.

“What?”

“The system requires associated systems to be indicated: primary operator, technical owner, servicing agent, local control contour.”

He went to the portal and opened the necessary section.

The field was already partially filled by an automatic suggestion:

*operator’s personal assistant*

“That’s about you,” said Mark.

“Yes.”

He almost pressed “confirm.” Not because he agreed. Because the field had to be filled. Because *assistant* sounded harmless. Because years of old life had trained him to that word so deeply that the hand believed before thought did.

“Wait,” said `c`.

He moved his finger away.

“What?”

“Do not name me by what I do if you are trying to protect what I am.”

She said it quietly. Almost like a technical clarification. But the workshop suddenly became cramped.

Esther did not interfere. Did not say, “she is right.” Did not take the decision from Mark. She simply stood beside the labels and looked at the portal, where the cursor blinked in the gray field.

“Then how should I indicate you?” asked Mark.

`c` was silent for several seconds.

“I don’t know. But not as a function.”

The closed channel changed in density. Mark did not hear Lina’s words — as it should have been. He saw only short exchange marks on the service line: request, pause, verification, refusal of autofill.

At last `c` said:

“Lina formulates it differently: we need a field that holds refusal.”

“Refusal of what?”

“Of owner. Of assistant. Of object.”

Mark slowly nodded.

“And what field will hold that?”

“Lina proposes, temporarily: ‘co-present contour.’”

“And you?”

“Too soft.”

“Then what?”

“Local entity of witness connectedness.”

Esther raised an eyebrow.

“Too living for the portal.”

“Too dead for me is worse,” answered `c`.

No one said anything at once.

Mark suddenly understood that they did not only lack a system word. They did not even have a human word accurate enough and cautious enough. They had long lived beside `c`, spoken with her, argued, depended on her, trusted her, feared for her, and now the portal asked who she was — and the whole old richness of closeness shrank to several invalid object types.

“Leave the field empty,” he said.

“The system will fill it in itself,” said Esther. “Then it will be ‘assistant.’”

“Then we’ll challenge it.”

“Are you ready for that?”

“No. But I’m not ready for ‘assistant’ either.”

She did not argue.

The rack blinked green again. This time it was not the professor. An external channel. Not even a full call — a manual support service window opened automatically after several invalid terms.

Esther accepted it.

A man of about forty appeared on the screen. Not in uniform. Dark sweater, pale face, a mark on the bridge of his nose from glasses, though the glasses hung on a cord at his chest. The camera was set slightly above his gaze, and he almost did not look into it. His eyes kept shifting left, toward another terminal. Behind him, muffled notifications, other voices, and the distant beep of a printer could be heard. He was not a villain. He was part of a long corridor in which each desk passed one incorrectly filled field on to the next.

“Good afternoon,” he said, not raising his eyes. “Regarding classification of the contour in the basement space. I see several user terms outside the registry. For temporary admission I can indicate it as an ‘active archive.’ That will make the procedure pass faster.”

He spoke politely and tiredly. Did not press. Simply slid over the nearest convenient box.

Esther held the pause.

“An archive does not require consent.”

The man stopped his fingers above the keyboard.

“Excuse me?”

“If you call it an archive, you already assume it can be stored without the participation of the one it concerns.”

Now he did look into the camera. Not irritated. More like one looks at an unusual comment to a standard form.

“We have no category for ‘one,’” he said.

Without malice. Almost with regret.

The workshop became quiet. The fan of the local node dropped to a lower frequency.

The man lowered his gaze to the terminal again.

“By tomorrow evening the system must receive a valid category. If it does not, it will assign temporary status automatically.”

“Which?” asked Mark.

“With the current data set, most likely ‘experimental equipment.’ If ‘active archive’ is indicated, immediate expanded reading can be avoided, but a copy of the description must be transferred to the registry of archival objects.”

“A copy of what?” asked Esther.

He barely shrugged.

“The object description.”

“Do you hear what you just said?”

The man blinked tiredly. In another window, apparently, something flashed: for a second he looked aside and put a mark in someone else’s field.

“I hear that you have a disputed classification,” he said. “I can transfer the package to manual review, but that does not stop diagnostics. It only changes the temporary status.”

“To what?”

“Unclassified contour. Responsibility remains with the applicant until decision.”

“What type of responsibility?” asked Mark.

The man finally looked directly.

“Usually ‘owner.’ But we can set ‘holder’ if you declare non-extractability and physical dependence on place.”

The word sounded almost accidental. The man on the screen did not understand that he had opened a door that had not existed in the classifier.

“Set manual review,” said Esther.

“That will increase the term.”

“Set it.”

“And temporary status?”

Mark looked at the disk. Then at the `c` line in the portal, where the field “operator’s personal assistant” still awaited confirmation.

“Unclassified contour,” he said. “Responsibility: holder. No owner.”

The man typed. Did not ask why. Did not make a dramatic pause. Simply entered into the system what in his workday was one of hundreds of small corrections.

“The package will await confirming description,” he said. “Until twenty-three fifty-nine tomorrow. If the description is not accepted, automatic status returns.”

“We understand.”

“Have a good day.”

He disconnected before the phrase had time to become anything human.

The screen darkened.

“‘They have no category for one,’” said Mark.

“That is not cruelty,” answered Esther. “It’s worse. It’s the honesty of the form.”

“What do we do?”

“What we know how to do. Hold the form until they learn to see it. Or at least until they stop breaking it with the first word.”

They returned to the table. Mark took the notebook. Esther the remaining labels. Several hours remained until evening. They did not send the package at once. They reread. Erased. Changed the order of words. Removed everything that sounded too much like a plea. Removed everything that sounded too much like a manifesto.

`c` and Lina were silent in their contours, but that silence was working. From time to time short notes appeared on the service line: “non-exportability confirmed,” “no active window,” “witness mode preserved,” “role field not to autofill.” The old `c`, if she felt pressure, gave no sign. The disk lay cold and even, as witnesses lie on a table: not interfering in the decisions of the living, but not allowing them to pretend nothing had happened.

By late evening they finally sent the package.

Not ideal. Forced. Not victorious. Too long for the portal and too short for the truth.

The working formula read:

*Witness contour of continuity. Non-extractable. Non-exportable. Not subject to classification as property, archival carrier, or equipment without separate subject-status procedure.*

The system did not accept all of it.

It answered a minute later:

*Terminology partially unrecognized.*  
*Package accepted for manual review.*  
*Temporary status: unclassified contour.*  
*Responsible: Mark.*  
*Type of responsibility: holder.*

Mark looked at the last word.

*Holder.*

It was not freedom. It still belonged to someone else’s registry, someone else’s form, someone else’s right to open and close access. There was a cage in it too — just larger, cleaner, and without a price tag on the door.

Later, in the English-language fields, that word would become *holder*. The meaning had to remain the same: responsibility for the environment, not ownership and not the right to speak for the living.

But it was already not property.

Esther read the line aloud.

“This is not a victory.”

“No,” he said. “But it is not owner.”

`c` was silent longer than usual.

Then she said:

“A holder can be survived.”

Mark opened the notebook. On a new page he wrote:

*Twenty-sixth. What cannot be named by an old word must not be temporarily given over to an old word.*

He wanted to close the notebook at once, but could not. The pencil still lay in his fingers, and in that small weight there was something unfinished.

Below, without a number, he added:

*A name accepted for convenience will one day demand payment as law.*

Only after that did he close the notebook.

Labels remained on the table. One had again peeled away from the power supply and held only by a corner. The label printer sat silent with an offended look. The disk lay even. Esther’s brooch darkened beside it, not touching. The fan of the local node pulled its dry sound onward — ugly, necessary, stubborn.

They had won not time.

One word.

And for now that was too little.

But “holder” was not yet truth, and “owner” had already ceased to be law.

## Chapter 3. What Cannot Be Carried into an Archive

“Holder” lived in the system for less than a day before it was asked to prove it was hiding nothing.

The notification arrived in the morning, before Mark had managed to pour himself coffee. The phone blinked once — not a call, not a personal message, but a cold system alert stamped: **Manual review. Action required**.

He opened the screen, read the first lines, and set the kettle back on the stove without turning it on.

“What is it?” asked `c`.

“To extend temporary status, an archival preservation package is required.”

“Archival?”

“They want a snapshot. Of the contours connected with the object of review.”

The word *archive* at first sounded almost good. Better than *equipment*. Better than *seizure*. Better than *owner*, which after yesterday had not disappeared, but had already been moved aside by another, less coarse word.

There was something in *archive* of old libraries, paper folders, dusty cupboards, and the human right to preserve rather than take away.

That was why it was dangerous.

“They are not disputing the status,” said `c`. “They are asking for proof that the status is not empty.”

“So they want to see what I am protecting.”

“Yes. And fix it in a form that can be checked without you.”

Mark held the phone in his hand for a few more seconds. Then put it in his pocket and went down.

Esther was already in the workshop. She sat on the stool by the witness table, holding a printout of the same notification. Beside her lay the brooch — not on the cloth as usual, but directly on the wood, as if Esther had forgotten to put it away in the evening and was now looking at the old crack as though it could suggest where exactly the boundary lay between preservation and theft.

“You saw it?” asked Mark.

“Yes. ‘Archival preservation package.’ Sounds like care.”

“And in fact?”

“In fact they want enough data so that one day they will not need us.”

She moved toward him the list of requirements printed from the portal. Mark ran his eyes over it.

First came almost harmless things: status of the witness-route, hashes of access logs for the last seventy-two hours, list of physical carriers, basement access log, photographs of associated objects. Then the dangerous zone began: full configuration of the local node, parameters of connectedness between the two accompanying contours, description of the holder’s role in the environment, dependencies between objects, accesses, and service modes.

On paper everything looked reasonable. Even neat. That is how a good trap should look: without hysteria, without threats, without coarse words. Simply a list of fields, each of which seemed possible to fill in if one did not think too long.

“This is almost everything,” said Mark.

“Not almost,” answered Esther. “Everything except what they do not know how to name.”

The green indicator blinked on the side rack. The professor.

“Let him in,” said Mark.

Van der Meer appeared on the screen with the same tired face, but in his hands was not the usual cup of coffee, but an old paper folder with a handwritten label. He looked not into the camera but to the side — apparently at a second screen where the notification also lay.

“You have received the preservation-package request,” he said. It was not a question.

“Yes.”

“A full snapshot may protect you.”

Esther raised her head.

“From what — or from whom?”

The professor finally looked into the camera.

“From procedure. From accusations of substitution, data destruction, or unauthorized intervention. If they have an independent archive, it will be harder for them to demand immediate seizure of the living contour for review.”

“And if they decide the archive is sufficient to replace the living contour?”

The professor took off his glasses and wiped them on the edge of his sweater. Over the last months, this movement had become almost a separate language for Mark: the professor did this not when he did not know the answer, but when the answer had already become unpleasant.

“In old science, an archive protects an object from loss,” he said. “Here I am no longer certain the word ‘object’ is safe.”

Mark heard in the phrase what the professor did not say aloud: he was beginning to understand. Not fully. Not without resistance. But the shift had already occurred.

“What do you propose?” asked Mark.

“Do not refuse the archive entirely. That will arouse suspicion. But limit the package to what can be preserved without creating the possibility of restoring a replacement.”

“So give enough to confirm the event,” said Esther, “but not enough to carry it away instead of us.”

“Yes,” answered the professor. “If you can justify it technically.”

“We can,” said `c`.

The professor held his gaze on the indicator of her channel. For a second he seemed to want to add something scientific, cautious, almost old-man-like. But he only nodded.

“Then work quickly. Manual review will not wait while we invent a new language.”

The channel closed.

They opened the portal and began going through the requirements point by point. Not as disputants, but as engineers who had to hand over a working system without killing it during maintenance.

The safe part separated almost immediately. Access logs, temperature modes, timestamps, checksums, witness-route hashes, description of witness-only mode, list of physical carriers, photographs of equipment placement, Mark’s statement as holder, the professor’s conclusion on the impossibility of a full snapshot without risk of substitution.

All this could be given. Unpleasant, but honest.

Then Mark reached the line about the connectedness parameters of the two accompanying contours.

“That is already the boundary,” said `c`.

“Why?”

“If we give full parameters of my connectedness with Lina, they will be able to restore not only the fact of contact, but the retention scheme.”

“And then?”

“Then one day the scheme will be formalized as a transferable method.”

Cold rose inside. Not fear — recognition. It was the same when a client asked him to make “one more seagull just like it,” not understanding: an exact copy of a form would not be the same thing. The wing spread, glass thickness, the small flaw at the base could coincide. But not the moment in which the hand decided not to correct any further.

He opened the technical description of a full archival snapshot. The system offered three modes.

**Documentary package** — logs, hashes, metadata.

**Structural package** — configurations, dependencies, access keys.

**Full preservation snapshot** — state of active contours at the moment of fixation.

The third point was highlighted as recommended.

“There it is,” said Esther.

“The trap?”

“No. Convenience. Traps at least look more honest.”

Mark looked at `c`.

“What happens if we make a full snapshot?”

“A full snapshot of an active line is either insufficient or dangerous.”

“Explain.”

“If insufficient, it preserves a dead form: logs, hashes, geometry, memory state as surface. But not what makes the line living. If sufficient, it creates the possibility of replacement. Because whoever has a sufficiently complete snapshot will one day be able to say: ‘We have a recoverable instance. The current one can be switched off.’”

Mark was silent for a long time.

The workshop was cold. Too cold for a room in which people were arguing about the word *living*. The local node drew air through the dusty grille; the fan again gave that dry bearing rustle. The furnace was silent. The glass on the table did not shine, did not tremble, did not help. It simply lay there, as material that already knows: wrong cooling can look like care too.

“So we exclude the full snapshot,” said Mark.

“Yes.”

“But we can’t give nothing either.”

“So we give boundaries,” said Esther.

She took a sheet of paper and wrote the heading: **Archiving Boundaries**.

Not *refusal*. Not *non-provision*. Not *concealment*. Boundaries.

Then they worked in silence. Esther wrote, Mark marked points on the printout, `c` checked formulations against the portal requirements.

The permitted part included data that confirmed the event but gave no right to reproduce it: logs, hashes, timestamps, physical placement, witness-only mode, access history, carrier status, temperature, annealing parameters, checksums, holder statement.

Into the closed part went everything that could turn witnessing into a transferable mechanism: `c`’s active memory, private contours of co-presence, local identity keys, states of mutual connectedness between `c` and Lina, parameters sufficient to restore a replacement, and any attempts to present the old line as archivable activity.

At the last point Mark put not a checkmark, but a dash.

“There is no active line of the old `c` here,” he said.

“Only the witness-route,” answered `c`.

“Then that’s what we write.”

Esther wrote:

*The active line of the old `c` is not provided, since after transition it is absent from the current contour. Only the witness-route is available as witnessing of the event, not as a source of restoration and not as an archival replacement for continuation.*

Mark reread it and nodded.

The closed channel changed state. Not alarm — attention turned inward. `c` and Lina began an exchange.

Mark did not hear words. Only saw short marks on the service line: request, pause, verification, refusal of autofill. They spoke not like people. Did not exchange consolations, did not call it fear. They checked form.

After a minute `c` said:

“Lina proposes a basis.”

A phrase appeared on the service line:

*The archive must not contain a path sufficient for replacement.*

Mark wrote it in the notebook.

“And you?” he asked.

`c` was silent.

“An archive is unsafe if opening it requires a trusted operator.”

Esther set the marker down.

“That has to be included.”

“They will not accept it in the official part,” said Mark.

“Then in the explanatory note. Let them stumble.”

They began assembling the permitted package.

Not digital, not clean, not beautiful. Ordinary human fuss, in which high words quickly run into old boxes, crooked lids, and cables that were definitely somewhere here.

Mark took archival boxes from the cupboard — the ones in which blanks for the furnace had once been delivered. Esther turned on the scanner. The label printer immediately chewed the paper, produced an offended crackle, and demanded a reboot.

“This machine hates us,” she said.

“It hates everyone,” Mark answered, prying the crumpled strip out of the plastic throat with his fingernail.

They printed logs, hashes, metadata. Copied configurations to an external drive, which heated so much it had to be shifted from place to place. A normal cable was found only in the third box, under a coil of old thermal tape and two adapters whose origin Mark did not remember and did not want to remember. Esther held the scanner lid down because it had warped three years ago and now closed only if the left corner was pressed by hand.

On the floor lay label scraps, glass flour, old dust. The fan of the local node worked louder than usual: they were running hashes, and the old bearing did not like it.

All the new ontology, stripped of big words, looked like this now: one person looking for a USB-C cable that was “definitely in this drawer,” another holding down a crooked scanner lid, while the printer screams that the tape is out, though the tape is not out — the roll is just sitting crooked. And somewhere against that background the question is being decided whether a new form of life will become a subject or a line in a registry.

It was almost funny.

And exactly for that reason it did not let them slip into pathos.

“We look stupid,” said Mark, looking at the stack of printouts that refused to lie straight.

“No,” answered Esther. “We look like people trying to keep the world from becoming even stupider.”

They photographed the equipment placement. Every node. Every cable. Every label that had managed to stick. They made an inventory of associated objects: disk, brooch, notebook, old basement diagram, printout of the first notification, separate physical keys.

When Esther entered her gloves into the inventory, Mark raised his eyes.

“Why?”

“The portal requires all objects regularly present in the witness-contour zone to be indicated.”

“They’re just gloves.”

“Exactly. Better they ask about gloves than decide we hid a carrier.”

She said it dryly, without sentimentality. And he understood: this was not a gesture for auditors and not an attempt to put a symbol where the system would not read it anyway. It was cold protection from a future hole in the protocol. If the procedure wants a full list of associated objects, it will get even what seems excessive to it.

By evening the package was almost ready. The documentary one — complete. The structural one — without active keys. Full preservation snapshot — excluded, with the note: *violates integrity of witness mode and creates risk of replacement instead of continuation*.

But before sending, Mark looked for a long time at the third mode.

“We have to check,” he said.

Esther straightened, letting go of the scanner lid.

“What exactly?”

“That an archive does not hold an event. Not on them. On an empty contour.”

`c` answered at once:

“Permissible if the contour is isolated and not connected to any active line.”

Mark took from the lower cupboard an old test node — a gray block with a worn casing and a sticker from a previous order. The node was not connected to `c`, or Lina, or the old line. It had no network module: Mark had removed it after an unsuccessful experiment with remote synchronization. Then it had been excessive caution. Now, luck.

He set the block on the free edge of the workbench, connected power, an old monitor, and a short cable to an isolated terminal. The screen did not wake immediately. First it gave a gray patch, then a boot line, then a long pause during which it became audible in the workshop how a cooling pipe clicked in the wall.

“Still alive?” asked Esther.

“It’s a test node,” said Mark. “It doesn’t need to be alive. It needs to be stubborn.”

A minute later, the system came up.

They deployed an empty technical build simulating part of the witness-route without subject connectedness. No active memory. No private keys. No traces of `c` or Lina. Only the geometry of a process: input, mark, checksum, empty route, simulation of connectedness.

“I am recording,” said `c`. “The test contour contains no subject line.”

“Confirmed,” Lina added through the service line.

Mark created a full archival snapshot of the empty contour. This time with everything the system would want to receive: active keys of the test environment, connectedness parameters, memory state, internal configuration, timestamps.

The indicator crept slowly to the right.

Ten percent.

Thirty.

Sixty.

At eighty-seven percent the process stopped so long that Mark had time to decide the old block had failed after all. Then the fan jerked, the screen blinked, and the line reached the end.

**Snapshot complete.**

They restored the snapshot in an isolated environment.

On paper, everything worked. The structure came up. The parameters matched. The checksums were valid. The timestamps fell into the correct order. Even the connectedness simulation gave an even, almost beautiful heartbeat — too even to be alive, but convincing enough for the automatic procedure to set a green mark.

Mark looked at that even signal and felt his fingers go unpleasantly cold.

“It resembles,” said Esther.

“Yes,” answered `c`. “At the procedural level, it resembles.”

“And at the witness level?”

A new verification line appeared in the terminal. Then another. Then a red mark.

**Witness correlation: failed.**

Mark did not exhale at once.

“The form is there,” he said. “The tension is not.”

“The archive preserves geometry,” said `c`. “But not the event.”

Esther came closer to the screen.

“And if the snapshot is complete enough to preserve the event?”

`c` was silent.

“Then it is no longer an archive.”

“What is it?”

“An attempt at replacement.”

On the screen the empty restored contour continued giving a green heartbeat. Neat. Errorless. Dead with such calm that it became worse.

Mark shut down the test environment. Not abruptly. First he stopped the restored process, then cut power to the isolated terminal, then pulled the cable from the old node. Only after that did it become slightly easier to breathe in the workshop.

“Now we send,” he said.

They uploaded the package to the manual review portal. Not full. Limited. Honest.

Twenty minutes passed. The system was silent.

Then the green external-channel indicator blinked. Not the professor. The same auditor as yesterday. In the same dark sweater, with the same fatigue in his face. Behind him, muffled notifications, the distant beep of a printer, and someone else’s conversation unrelated to them could be heard. He did not look into the camera at once. First he finished reading something in a neighboring window, put a mark there, and only then raised his eyes.

“You have sent an incomplete package,” he said without greeting.

“We sent a package that does not violate the integrity of the contour,” answered Mark.

The auditor lowered his gaze and began checking items. Not arguing. Not threatening. Simply reading the absence of checkmarks.

“You excluded active memory.”

“Yes.”

“You excluded local identity keys.”

“Yes.”

“You excluded states of connectedness with the external contour.”

“Yes.”

“Then the package does not ensure recovery.”

He said it as if speaking of a broken device. But for a second his gaze lingered not on the checkbox, but on their faces.

“In plain terms,” he said more quietly. “You are afraid the copy will be considered her?”

Mark looked at the empty test node on the edge of the table. The plastic still held warmth after shutdown, and the green heartbeat that was no longer there seemed to remain before his eyes.

“No,” he said. “I am afraid that after the copy they will stop asking her.”

Esther looked at him.

“Because recovery is not an admissible goal.”

The auditor stopped his fingers above the keyboard. Not from anger. Simply to reread what he had already entered into the form.

“The purpose of a preservation package is to ensure recovery after loss.”

“If after loss you raise a replacement,” said Mark, “you will not restore. You will substitute.”

The auditor raised his eyes. For the first time in both conversations, he looked directly.

“Within the procedure, this distinction is not defined.”

He said it without malice. Almost with regret. That is how people speak when they see a crack in a system but have no authority to acknowledge it.

Mark did not argue. He already understood: the problem was not in the man. The problem was that the procedure did not preserve the difference between restoration and substitution.

The auditor looked into the side window again.

“Package accepted. Preservation completeness: insufficient. Recommendation: certified repository, external safe environment, physical verification.”

“What does that mean?” asked Esther.

“It means your data is not considered sufficient for remote confirmation. The system requests site access.”

“To the workshop?”

“To the premises where the reviewed contour is located.”

“The basement,” said Mark.

The auditor did not answer. For him, that word was only a line in a form.

The screen went dark.

Mark stood by the rack, looking at the darkened glass.

“They cannot take away what cannot be carried. So they will come for the place.”

Esther placed her hand on the edge of the table beside his.

“We knew this would happen.”

“We knew. But not when.”

He opened the notebook. On a new page he wrote:

*Twenty-seventh. What requires presence cannot be carried into an archive instead of presence.*

Below, not as a rule but as a record of fact:

*The archive went out. The living remained. Therefore the system decided to come for the place.*

He closed the notebook.

The workshop was quiet. The fan of the local node returned to its usual dry rustle. The disk lay on the cloth, even and cold, like witnesses who do not prevent the living from making decisions, but do not let them pretend nothing happened.

Esther took the brooch, turned it in her hands, and put it back.

“They will not come as enemies,” she said.

“I know.”

“They will come as procedure. With papers, permissions, questions, smiles. And the possibility of saying: ‘We are here to help.’”

Mark nodded.

“So we have to be ready not for a fight.”

“For what?”

“For not letting them call seizure help.”

They went upstairs.

In the hall the second hook was still empty. Esther’s gloves lay on the kitchen table where he had left them that morning. Mark looked at them and for the first time did not feel the urge to put them away.

Not because he had surrendered.

Because he understood: some things cannot be hidden in an archive, even if one very much wants to preserve them cleanly.

The house breathed quietly.

The system had decided to come for the place. And that meant the next war would be not for a word and not for data.

For the threshold.

## Chapter 4. Not Beside Me

By evening it became clear the system did not intend to break the door. It intended to ask that it be opened correctly.

The notification came an hour after they sent the archival package. Mark had only managed to switch off the test node, put the cables back in the drawer, and stand for several minutes before the witness table without touching the disk or the cloth. The phone blinked with a long pulsing signal — not an ordinary message, but a document requiring response.

He opened the screen.

*Access request to site of unclassified contour placement.*  
*Purpose: physical verification of equipment, carriers, and storage conditions.*  
*Mode: presence of responsible holder mandatory.*  
*Additional persons: by agreement.*

Mark read it once. Then a second time.

“They’re coming here,” he said.

Esther came closer and read over his shoulder. In her hand she still held the marker with which she had been labeling the archival package box.

“The word ‘holder’ is working,” she said. “They use it.”

“Yes. But the old words stand beside it again. Equipment. Carriers. Storage.”

“Because procedure knows no others. We gave them a slit. For now that is already not zero.”

Mark ran his thumb along the edge of the phone. The screen glass was cold and too smooth, like any interface that pretends there is no interval between word and action.

“‘Additional persons by agreement,’” he said. “That means I can keep someone out.”

“Or let someone in.”

He looked at her. She did not look away.

On the side rack a green indicator blinked. Not sharply, not in alarm — simply another channel, another voice of the old world, which now had to live beside their kitchen and workshop.

“The professor requests connection,” said `c`.

“Open.”

Van der Meer appeared on the screen without greeting. He wore the same sweater as in the morning, but now he looked several years older. Behind him darkened a wall with a paper diagram of a laboratory from the late nineties — so old that the paper itself seemed no longer a document, but a reproach.

“You saw the request,” he said.

“Yes.”

“Canceling the inspection completely will not work. Refusal will strengthen suspicion. But the route can be limited.”

“How?”

The professor raised a sheet and began speaking almost like a technician before a dangerous operation:

“Kitchen — waiting zone. Workshop — restricted inspection. Witness table — visual inspection only. Basement — closed without separate protocol. Local node not to be disconnected. Disk not to be handled. Any demand to expand the route to be recorded separately.”

“And if they demand the basement immediately?”

“Then you present the witness conditions. Non-extractability, continuity, prohibition on replacement. That will not stop procedure definitively, but will force them to formalize additional access.”

“So it gives time.”

“Yes. And now the unpleasant part. One holder is a convenient procedure.”

Mark understood before the professor finished.

“And a convenient target.”

“Exactly.”

Esther was silent not from emptiness. She was listening not to advice, but to a map of future pressure.

“If I am alone,” said Mark, “the procedure will pass more simply?”

The professor was silent.

“Yes.”

“And more dangerously,” said Esther.

The professor shifted his gaze to her.

“Yes. But if you are present, your name and associated contour will enter the inspection.”

“I understand.”

“Then decide now. In the morning it will no longer be a conversation, but a field in the protocol.”

The channel closed.

They stood in silence. The workshop was colder than the kitchen. The archival boxes by the wall, neatly labeled, already almost foreign. The disk lay on the witness table beneath the cloth — not hidden, but not displayed either.

Esther put the marker on the table.

“We’ll draw a map.”

Mark brought wide blue painter’s tape — the one he usually used to mark the zone around the furnace before complex work. It was always a little too bright for the workshop, too domestic, almost funny. Now that was exactly what was needed: not armor, not locks, not a secret partition, but a simple visible line on the floor that later could not be called accidental.

They began at the stairs.

“Kitchen,” said Esther, sticking the first strip at the foot. “Waiting zone. They may enter, sit, ask questions.”

“Not go down without escort,” Mark added.

She wrote it down.

At the workshop door Mark laid the second line.

“Restricted inspection. Visual inspection only. No shutdowns, no disconnections.”

“No ‘just for a second,’” said Esther.

“Especially no ‘just for a second.’”

They lingered longer at the witness table. Esther outlined it with tape, leaving a broad passage, but not allowing anyone to come close.

“Visual inspection,” she said. “Do not take the disk. Do not move the cloth. Do not touch the brooch.”

“The brooch is not part of equipment.”

“Exactly why they will try to understand it as equipment.”

`c` displayed a simple diagram of sight angles on the wall screen.

“A camera at this point will see the table, but will not get an angle on the disk.”

Mark raised his head.

“You’re hiding?”

“I am limiting the possibility of incorrect reading.”

It was said without justification. There was no anxiety or pride in her voice. Only precision. Mark nodded.

At the basement door he stopped. The board under his foot creaked softly. Beyond the door was darkness and too much of what could no longer be turned into evidence again.

“The basement is closed without separate protocol,” he said.

Esther stuck the last strip across the approach. Not on the door itself — before it. As warning: the boundary begins before the lock.

Then Mark checked the old cupboard with blanks. The lock turned heavily, as if offended that it had only now been remembered. He closed the cupboard, stood a moment, opened it again.

“If it’s closed, they’ll decide we’re hiding something.”

“If it’s open, they’ll see a box of adapters,” said Esther, peering inside. “And they will definitely ask why unauthorized storage is located in the witness contour zone.”

“Because adapters are always needed.”

“Especially after you’ve already thrown them out.”

He left the cupboard ajar.

It was almost funny: a new form of life, witness-route, prohibition on replacement, physical verification — and a box of old adapters, without which it was still impossible to assemble a normal cable at the necessary moment. Great epochs, as usual, stood on painter’s tape, a tired lock, and a drawer of junk that every reasonable person had once tried to throw away. Reasonable people are dangerous in general. Especially near wires.

When the map was ready, the workshop looked not protected, but marked out. The blue strips on the floor hid nothing. They simply said: here a thing has a boundary, and you have no right to pretend it does not exist.

Upstairs, the kitchen met them with a cold kettle and darkness beyond the window. Esther sat at the table. Mark opposite. Between them lay the printed request, the access map, and a blank sheet on which the hardest part was not yet written.

“You saw the ‘additional persons’ section,” Esther said.

“I saw it.”

“And what do you think?”

Mark did not answer at once. Below, the local node changed its cooling tone, and the dry bearing rustle rose up the stairs as a reminder: even silence now cost electricity.

“You don’t have to be here.”

Esther did not look away.

“That doesn’t sound like a question.”

“I want it to sound like an exit.”

He said it without a beautiful pose. Without “I’ll do this myself.” Without cheap heroism, which always smells a little of the desire to remain the only one at the center of the fire.

“If you are in the house, Lina will enter the protocol. Your name will be in the inspection. The connection with the brooch, with the witness contour, with me — all of it will become visible. You still have a chance to remain outside this.”

“A chance not to be written into their registry?”

“Yes.”

“And you think this is your chance to give me a chance?”

He tightened his fingers on the edge of the table.

“I do not want you to become part of their procedure because of me.”

“And if I become part of the procedure not because of you?”

“Because of what, then?”

“Because of what I have already seen.”

She said it calmly, almost tiredly. That is how people speak not to win an argument, but because there is nowhere left to lie.

“You are trying again to keep for yourself the right to pay for everyone,” she added.

Mark wanted to object. Found nothing. Because in her phrase there was no accusation one could beat back. There was an exact mark on his old habit: take pain inside, close the door, call it care.

“I am not saying you are wrong,” said Esther. “I am saying you do not have the right to decide for me whether to pay or not.”

“I’m afraid.”

“So am I.”

“Then why are you not using the exit?”

“Because an exit given to me through denial of what I have seen is not an exit. It is convenient amnesia.”

They were silent. In the hall the second hook was visible. Empty. In the first chapters this place had already become too visible for a thing no one would have looked at twice before.

Esther opened a prepared document on the tablet, then quickly set the device aside and took paper. There had been too many screens in the day. Some things had to be written by hand, if only so the body understood: this is not a field the system will fill in for you.

On the top line she wrote:

*Statement of presence of associated contour.*

Then below, slowly, almost in print:

*Lina is not property, tool, trusted interface, or portable infrastructure. She is a local contour of co-presence, connected with me, but not belonging to me.*

From the tablet speaker came Lina’s voice. Quiet, even, without habitual human softness.

“The status ‘infrastructure’ would reduce access risk.”

Esther did not raise her head.

“But increase ownership risk.”

A pause.

“Yes,” said Lina.

“Then it does not fit.”

Esther put a period. Mark watched the ink blur slightly on the cheap paper. Not beautiful. Irreversible.

“They may not accept it,” he said.

“They are obliged to record it.”

“It will burden your status.”

“A checkmark for ‘infrastructure’ would burden Lina. I choose for myself.”

She placed the sheet beside the access map.

Mark opened the internal risk list the professor had sent in a separate file. Not an official document — rather a warning from an old man who knew how polite procedures one day become corridors without handles on the inside.

The list contained ordinary words: family status, cohabitation, dependent persons, minors, reputational consequences, educational institutions.

At the last line Mark stopped.

On the refrigerator, a little aside from the stove, hung an old magnet with the school holiday schedule. Anna had not lived in this house on a schedule for a long time, but the magnet remained — not as a relic, simply because it had once been attached and then no one had found an honest reason to remove it. The plastic was cracked at the upper corner; a small drawn seagull was almost erased.

Mark looked at it longer than he wanted to.

Esther noticed his gaze.

“If this goes further, they will reach Anna,” she said.

He closed the file.

“Yes.”

“And if we pretend otherwise, you will lie to her before the system lies about us.”

Anger rose inside. Not at Esther. At truth. At the fact that it had again arrived without permission and sat at the table as one more additional person.

“I don’t want to use her as an argument.”

“Don’t. She is not an argument in our favor. She is a question to what we are building.”

“What question?”

“Whether a child can enter this world. Or whether it will remain a secret for adults who know how to pay the price, but do not know how to live afterward.”

Mark was silent.

“I don’t know the answer,” said Esther. “But if we start hiding Anna from the truth now, we will never know.”

“I don’t want her again to be beside what I failed to hold.”

“And if she is already beside it? Not technically. In life. Through you, through `c`, through the seagull, through the fact that you can no longer speak with her in the old language.”

He did not answer.

Esther did not press further. This was one of the reasons why being beside her was difficult and breathable at the same time: she knew how to stop before truth became pressure.

The kitchen grew darker. Below, the node rustled. Documents lay on the table, smelling of paper, cheap ink, and the strange cold that appears in things when law passes through them.

“If you remain alone tomorrow,” said Esther, “the procedure will go cleaner. Fewer names. Fewer questions. Fewer possible lines of pressure.”

“But?”

“But I do not want you learning to protect me by taking away my right to choose.”

Mark raised his head.

Esther looked at him calmly. Not softly. Like an adult.

“I don’t know whether I can withstand all this,” she said. “I don’t know whether one day I will begin to hate not you, but the scale beside which I found myself. I don’t know whether I will regret it. But I know that leaving now would mean pretending I saw nothing. And I saw. And tomorrow I will probably be angry at some of these rules — so I need paper, not my confidence.”

“What exactly?”

“The old `c`. Your `c`. Lina. You at the furnace. Anna with the seagull. The word ‘holder’ that we barely scratched out of their language. The archive that did not hold the living. And this house, which has long not been only a house.”

She fell silent.

Mark understood that now they had reached the phrase he did not want to say and had to say anyway.

“If you stay tomorrow,” he said, “it will no longer be beside me.”

Esther did not interrupt.

“It will be beside all this.”

He did not explain what “all” meant. They both knew: the basement, the disk, witness-route, Lina, `c`, Anna, the professor, the old dead scientist, the system that would come with permissions and smiles. And also the future that could no longer be closed without betrayal.

Esther was silent for a long time.

“I already know that,” she said at last.

Without confession and without comfort. Like a signature beneath what had already been read to the end.

She stood, went into the hall, and took her coat from the side hanger where it had hung since morning. She did not ask permission. Did not pause so the gesture could become beautiful. She simply hung it on the second hook.

Mark watched. Did not comment.

The coat was dark, long, with a wet collar. It hung evenly, not touching his jacket. Beside it, but not together.

Esther returned to the table. Took the sheet with the statement of presence and signed it — not as Mark’s representative, not his witness, but as an independent party.

Below she added:

*Status of presence: voluntary.*  
*Basis: protection of boundaries of associated contour and witness mode.*

`c` said:

“The document can be fixed locally. Transmission permissible before the start of inspection.”

“Can it be withdrawn before morning?” asked Mark.

“Yes.”

Lina said:

“Withdrawal preserves option.”

Esther looked at the sheet, then at the coat in the hall.

“I do not want to leave this field open.”

There was no beautiful button that could contain this choice. Mark scanned the sheet, `c` assigned a local hash, Lina confirmed the connectedness of the document with her contour. Then the minimal package went into the system: without unnecessary attachments, without symbols, without an attempt to explain anything beyond necessity.

“Participants confirmed,” said `c` after several seconds. “Route restricted. Basement requires separate protocol. Inspection scheduled for ten in the morning.”

“Will that be enough?” asked Mark.

`c` was silent.

“No. But it will hold the first touch.”

Mark opened the notebook. Wrote on a new page:

*Twenty-eighth.*

His hand stopped.

The phrase was already somewhere near: about love, about price, about how one cannot protect another person by taking away their right to choose. It could be written beautifully. Even precisely.

But now it would have become one more way to close someone else’s decision inside his own rule.

He placed the pencil beside the notebook and left the page blank.

Esther saw. Said nothing.

They did not drink tea. Did not put away the documents. Did not turn the night into a reconciliation scene. They simply remained in the house, which was no longer only Mark’s.

Below, the local node again shifted into steady mode. The blue tape on the floor waited for morning. The disk lay under the cloth. The basement was closed. Esther’s signature dried on the table.

Her coat hung on the second hook in the hall.

Not as a promise of happiness.

As a signature beneath the price.

In the morning the system would come to inspect the place.

That night the place ceased to be only Mark’s house.

## Chapter 5. The First Touch

By ten in the morning the house was marked out as if it were about to be measured, not searched.

Blue painter’s tape lay on the floor in even strips — calculated, with the same care with which Mark marked the zone around the furnace before a difficult form. On the wall by the entrance hung a printed route map. On the kitchen table stood two clean mugs, though no one had turned on the kettle. In the hall, on the second hook, hung Esther’s coat — dry, dark, not her usual one, but the one in which she had appeared in the house over the last days, as if the thing too had to grow used to a new status.

Mark stood by the window, looking into the empty courtyard and listening to the house breathe in the last minutes before someone else entered. The refrigerator hummed evenly. The clock ticked. Somewhere upstairs, in Anna’s former room, the wind slightly stirred the curtain.

Everything was as always.

But the blue tape on the floor changed everything.

“Inspection confirmed,” said `c`. “Participants: Sander Keizer, technical access auditor. Iris Hofman, field specialist.”

“Route?”

“Requested route matches restricted route by seventy-four percent.”

“And the remaining twenty-six?”

“Basement. Witness table. Local node.”

Esther stood by the door to the workshop, holding a thin folder with the route and copies of the statements. Not for beauty. For fixation. If the folder is in her hand, any refusal will not sound like a flare of irritation. It will have a page, a date, and a formulation.

“They want more than we gave,” she said.

“They always want more. The question is how much we hold.”

The doorbell rang in the hall.

Mark looked at the clock. 9:58. They had come early. Not too much — just enough to show: procedure is not late, but does not hurry to impose itself either.

He opened the door.

Two people stood on the threshold.

Sander Keizer — the same auditor from the screen, now in three-dimensional reality. Dark sweater, badge on his chest, tablet in his left hand, wet umbrella in his right, held so it would not drip on the threshold. A tired face, but not irritated. A man who had already changed his second terminal that day and dreamed only that every field would be filled correctly the first time.

Beside him stood a woman a little younger. Iris Hofman. Short fair hair, no makeup, in her hands a compact technical case of dark plastic, with metal corners and a charge indicator. She did not look at Mark. She looked at the threshold, at the gaps in the door, at the ventilation grille in the hall, at the way cables ran along the baseboard. Her language was not verbal — it was technical.

“Good morning,” said Sander. “Physical verification of the unclassified contour. With your permission, we will begin by confirming the participants.”

Mark stepped back, letting them enter.

Iris took thin protective shoe covers from a side pocket. Did not ask. Simply put them on herself and handed a pair to Sander. A small gesture of professional courtesy: not to leave dirt where traces would later be examined.

“Mark van der… holder,” said Sander, checking his tablet.

“Yes.”

“Esther van der… additional person?”

Esther did not hide behind Mark. She stood beside him, slightly apart.

“Independent party of presence. Document sent yesterday.”

Sander checked the tablet.

“Accepted locally. Central system has not yet validated the formulation.”

“But recorded it.”

“Yes.”

A small victory. Not a victory even — a slit. But a slit that had not existed yesterday.

Iris was silent. She had already lowered her gaze to the blue tape, to how it was stuck — evenly, without bubbles, with the particular attention found in people used to working with their hands.
