They went into the kitchen. Sander sat on the chair Mark moved to the table. Iris remained at the entrance to the hall: from there the corridor downward was more visible.

Sander unfolded the tablet and read evenly:

“Waiting zone. Permissible actions: participant identification, route verification, confirmation of access restriction.”

He was not mocking. This was how he worked. In his voice there was neither threat nor friendliness — only the habit of procedure, replacing personal intonation.

“I confirm the route,” said Mark.

“Any objections to participant composition?”

“No.”

“Then, with your permission, we begin technical verification.”

Iris finally spoke. Her voice was lower than Mark had expected and more even — without unnecessary modulations, like a person accustomed to being heard from the other end of a cable.

“Does the house have independent power for the local node?”

“In the workshop. UPS and reserve block.”

“Date of last check?”

Mark froze for a second. He had checked it himself, seventeen days earlier, when the fan began making more noise than usual. But he had not kept a formal log. Not because he was lazy — because the old world had not required him to report to himself for every relay click.

`c` answered for him:

“Seventeen days ago. Local log. Not exported.”

Sander raised his head.

“Are you speaking on behalf of the holder?”

“I am reporting the state of the contour associated with the holder.”

A pause. Sander looked at the channel indicator, then at Mark.

“That is permissible within technical verification,” he said at last. “We will record it as an additional source.”

Iris said nothing. Only nodded briefly, the way people nod when what they hear matches what they expected.

“Route confirmed,” said Sander. “Moving to restricted inspection zone.”

They went down.

The stairs had always been short: a few steps, a familiar turn, the third step, which Mark had long since stopped promising himself to fix. With other people present, the descent became longer. Not by distance — by weight.

Above there still remained the sound of the house: refrigerator, clock, distant rustle of curtain. On the first step that sound began to recede. On the second, the dry rustle of the local node below became clearer. On the third Sander stepped on the old board, and the creak went through the staircase so sharply that it seemed the house itself marked procedure’s entrance into the lower layer.

Iris stopped for half a second. Not from fear. She had felt the change in temperature: below it was colder, drier; the air smelled of metal, dust, and glass flour. Mark noticed it and suddenly understood that for them the workshop was no longer a workshop. It was a connected zone. A risk premise. A place that needed to be described so it would stop being alien.

For him that was worse than if they had simply been afraid.

The workshop met them with cold. The furnace did not burn, the tools on the walls grayed in the half-dark, the air smelled of dust, old wood, and a little metal. The blue tape led from the stairs to the working zone, then made a cautious bend around the witness table, leaving the basement behind a separate line.

Iris immediately noticed the ventilation grille above the furnace.

“Exhaust contour disabled?”

“The furnace is cold.”

“I’m not asking about the furnace. I’m asking about the general exhaust contour. If it is connected to node cooling, it must not be closed.”

Mark looked at her more carefully. She was not trying to find a hidden threat. She simply saw the physics.

“The exhaust contour is independent,” said `c`. “Only the local furnace channel is closed.”

Iris checked — went to the wall, touched the damper, made sure it was not blocked.

“Accepted.”

Sander walked along the tape first, Iris slightly behind him, Mark and Esther closing. Not an escort. Just order.

They stopped at the witness table.

The tape outlined it in a broad semicircle, leaving enough space for visual inspection, but not allowing anyone to come close. On the table lay the found disk under cloth, beside it Esther’s brooch, a small paper card reading “witness mode,” several labels they had stuck on the day before.

Sander read the card.

“‘No contact.’ Is this your restriction?”

Mark nodded.

“Condition of witness mode.”

“For procedure, so far, this is your assertion.”

Esther stepped forward and stood beside Mark, not behind him.

“For procedure, yes. For the contour, it is a condition.”

Iris took a compact scanner from the case — gray, with a matte body and a short lens, more like a tool than a toy.

“Visual fixation. No contact.”

“Specify spectrum,” said `c`.

Iris looked at the device.

“Standard multispectral mode.”

“Not permissible for witness zone.”

Iris raised her eyes to Mark.

“It is non-invasive.”

“For equipment,” said Esther.

Mark added:

“For a witness contour, ‘just looking’ does not always mean ‘not touching.’”

Sander was silent. He was waiting for procedure to resolve the dispute itself.

Iris did not argue. She simply raised the scanner to the required height, as she had done a thousand times, and the device automatically shifted into calibration.

A thin, almost inaudible click.

Mark did not feel the ray. There was no visible ray. He felt something else: the familiar instant when another person’s tool has already entered motion, and the hand of the craftsman can still stop the consequences.

`c` said sharply and cleanly:

“Expanded spectrum activated. Exceeds agreed visual range. Risk of witness-route violation.”

Mark took a step forward and stood between the scanner and the table. Did not seize the device. Did not push Iris. Simply closed the angle.

Sander raised his head.

“Mr. van der… are you obstructing the inspection?”

Esther answered before Mark had time to open his mouth:

“No. He is preventing an excess of the agreed route.”

Iris lowered the scanner. Checked the display.

“It was only a calibration pulse.”

`c` answered:

“The pulse exceeded the agreed visual range.”

Iris checked the settings. Her finger froze for a second.

“Confirmed. The device enabled expanded spectrum automatically.”

Sander made a note in the tablet.

“Refusal of multispectral scanning due to risk of witness-mode violation.”

“Not refusal,” said Esther. “Restriction.”

Sander looked at her for several seconds. Then corrected the record.

“Restriction.”

Iris put the scanner back in the case. Not angrily — with the bewilderment of a person who has just discovered that a familiar instrument is not as harmless as she thought.

“We move to the node,” said Sander.

The local node stood in the corner of the workshop, on a separate shelf, away from the furnace and the window. The fan worked evenly, but with that slight dry undertone Mark had known for weeks.

Iris approached first.

“Cooling is noisy.”

“I know.”

“Critical infrastructure?”

`c` answered:

“Yes.”

Iris looked at Mark.

“Replacement plan?”

“Exists. Without stopping the active contour.”

She raised an eyebrow. For the first time that morning her face stopped being neutral — something like professional respect appeared.

“Show me.”

Mark opened the lower drawer of the workbench. Took out the reserve fan — the same model as the one now working, new, in an antistatic bag. Then showed the airflow switching diagram: bypass, temporary channel, manual access, no need to reboot the node.

“UPS holds fifteen minutes without main power. Switching — three minutes. The active line will not feel a spike.”

Iris listened, nodding. Not like a superior — like a colleague.

“Thermal pad?”

Mark took a small packet from the same drawer.

“New. And the old one is not dried out yet.”

“Switching relay?”

He showed it.

“Manual. No remote access.”

Iris looked at Sander.

“I will record this as a sufficient local plan for the period until repeat inspection.”

Sander wrote it down.

This is what it looked like when metaphysics reached the floor: not through hymns to new life, but through a fan, a thermal pad, a spare packet of screws, and the question of whether a dying piece of hardware could be replaced so the active line would not feel rough cooling. First prove your cooler will not die. Then speak of continuity. In that sense, physics was more honest than any declaration.

The workshop became a little quieter. Not because the fan changed tone — because the tension shifted.

One door remained.

They went to the basement.

The blue tape lay across the approach, not on the door itself — before it. The boundary began before the lock.

Sander looked at the door, then at Mark.

“To close physical verification, visual confirmation of the lower premises is required.”

“The lower premises are not included in the agreed route.”

“They are indicated as a connected zone.”

Esther stepped forward.

“Connected does not mean accessible.”

Sander held the pause.

“In that case I must note an obstacle.”

“Record,” said Esther. “Requested access exceeds validated route and requires separate witness protocol.”

Sander looked at her for a long time. Then recorded almost exactly what she had said.

Iris was silent. But she looked at the door not as at a storage room and not as at a hiding place. As at a zone with conditions of entry, not as at a box with a lock.

`c` said:

“The basement is closed not for concealment. For preservation of conditions.”

Sander raised his head.

“The system does not distinguish those grounds without inspection.”

Mark looked at him.

“Then for now we distinguish them.”

Sander did not argue. He closed the tablet.

“Route completed. Returning upstairs.”

They went up to the kitchen. Iris closed the case. Sander filled out the final report, moving his finger over the screen. Mark stood by the window, Esther by the table. The house breathed as it had that morning, but the air in it was already different — someone else’s traces, someone else’s questions, someone else’s caution remained.

“The inspection will be marked partially completed,” said Sander.

“Because we did not open the basement?”

“Because the system did not receive everything it expected.”

“It received everything that was agreed,” said Esther.

Sander looked at her.

“That too will be recorded.”

Iris, who had been silent until then, unexpectedly added:

“And the scanner excess too.”

Sander shifted his gaze to her. She did not look away.

“I will record it,” he said.

They stood. Sander clipped the tablet to his belt, Iris took the case. At the door they removed the shoe covers. Sander took the wet umbrella, which had been standing all this time in the corner of the hall, carefully placed in an old bucket for broken glass.

“Have a good day,” he said.

“Have a good day,” answered Mark.

The door closed.

Workshop, kitchen, hall — everything became as before. But not quite.

On the blue tape by the witness table remained a dirty footprint — the inspector had almost not crossed the line, but the edge was still crushed. The stool Mark had placed to redirect a step stood slightly shifted. The air smelled of someone else’s rain.

`c` received the preliminary report.

“Physical verification: partially completed. Witness zone: visually confirmed, multispectral scan restricted by holder. Local node: conditionally satisfactory. Lower premises: access not provided. Recommendation: supervisory review, expanded access protocol.”

Esther read it.

“They did not lose.”

“And we did not win.”

`c` said:

“The first touch held.”

Lina added briefly, from the closed channel:

“But now they know the shape of the threshold.”

Mark looked at the blue tape. On it was someone else’s trace. Not a violation — a mark. The system had touched the boundary and remembered exactly where it passed.

He did not take the notebook. Did not open it. Did not write a new rule.

Because the rule had already been written not in ink, but in a dirty footprint on painter’s tape.

The threshold held.

On the blue tape remained the measure of another’s step.

## Chapter 6. The Right Hands

The dirt on the blue tape dried faster than the inspection report.

Mark was crouching by the witness table, looking at the dried footprint. The edge of the tape had come loose, curled upward, and now resembled a small brown scratch on the blue field. He reached out to press it back down, but did not. His fingers stopped a centimeter from the edge.

“Don’t touch it,” Esther said.

“It’ll come off.”

“Let it show for now where they stepped.”

He withdrew his hand.

The workshop was quiet. The fan of the local node ran evenly, but no longer deceived anyone: the old bearing was still living out its term. It had simply decided to make less noise today than yesterday. The disk lay under the cloth on the table. The blue tape held the route. The inspection had left, and the house had not yet managed to become only a house again.

“The preliminary finding is available,” said `c`. “Supervisory review has been assigned automatically.”

Mark stood, went to the rack. Esther had already opened the document.

What was frightening in it was not the volume, but the tone. Everything was polite. Almost caring.

*Physical verification: partially completed.*  
*Witness zone: visually confirmed. Multispectral scan restricted by holder.*  
*Local node: conditionally satisfactory. Cooling requires a maintenance window within the next two weeks.*  
*Basement: not inspected. Classified as an unconfirmed connected zone.*  
*The house is not a certified environment for storing unclassified contours.*  
*Recommendation: temporary placement of associated carriers and critical nodes in a certified environment to ensure preservation and reduce the risk of uncontrolled damage.*

Esther read the last paragraph aloud, slowly, as one reads another person’s thought in which a decision can already be heard.

“They don’t say ‘seize.’”

“Of course,” Mark answered.

“They say ‘place.’”

`c` added:

“And ‘temporarily.’”

Mark was silent for a moment.

“The most dangerous word after ‘for now.’”

He opened the appendices section. Among the standard forms, template tables, and links to regulations was a single file signed by Iris Hofman.

*Appendix to the physical verification protocol.*  
*Technical notes of the field specialist.*

The points were dry, almost dull — and for that reason Mark reread them twice.

*Automatic activation of the expanded spectrum occurred before manual confirmation. Standard field protocol does not account for witness restrictions.*

*The local node has an aging fan (bearing noise, wear class B), but the replacement plan without stopping the active line is recognized as technically sound. Reserve fan, bypass channel, manual switching, and UPS are sufficient for short-term maintenance.*

*Physical relocation of the active node without a separate stabilization procedure may lead to loss of connectedness and violation of witness-route. Recommendation: do not relocate the active node without a preliminary continuity protocol.*

Mark lingered on the last line.

“She saw the iron,” he said.

“Not us,” Esther answered. “Not `c`. The iron.”

“But that was enough.”

“Yes. And right now that is enough.”

He nodded once. Not in gratitude to Iris, but in recognition of a simple fact: sometimes one honest technical note holds a door better than ten correct words.

The green indicator blinked on the side rack. The professor.

“Open it,” Mark said.

Van der Meer appeared on the screen not in the laboratory and not by the familiar diagram on the wall. He was sitting in a small office, too cramped for his height, with a closed door behind him and the overhead light switched off. Only the desk was lit. On it lay a paper folder, and beside it an overturned pass. Somewhere behind the door people walked past. The professor waited until the footsteps went away and only then spoke.

“You have seen the finding?”

“Yes,” said Mark.

“And Iris’s appendix?”

“Yes.”

The professor nodded, but without relief.

“The committee will propose a temporary certified environment.”

Mark caught the word.

“Propose?”

The professor looked toward the door. Not theatrically. From the habit of a man who since morning had already had to say the same thing several times to the wrong people.

“For now, yes,” he answered. “I still have the ability to argue over formulations. Not over the whole decision.”

Esther came closer to the screen.

“And after that?”

He took off his glasses, wiped them. The movement was familiar, but now there was something else in it — not only fatigue, but irritation at himself.

“After that, if the supervisory review considers the risk sufficient, ‘proposal’ will receive another verb.”

“And you will sign it?” Mark asked.

The professor put his glasses back on.

“I am already signing part of the paper I would prefer not to touch. They have taken from me the right to issue a sole technical finding. Everything now goes through the committee.”

That was new. Not a plot task delivered by the professor, but the price on his face.

“If you agree voluntarily,” he continued, “you will have more conditions. If you refuse sharply, the committee may request mandatory access.”

“And if you sign that the house is sufficient?” Mark asked.

“Then I will lie.”

“And if you sign that it is insufficient?”

The professor held the pause.

“Then I open the road to custody of the active contour and associated carriers.”

He did not try to soften the meaning. And he did not justify himself.

“I can argue with their words,” he said. “But I cannot state that your workshop is a safe laboratory. It is not a laboratory.”

“It is a house,” Mark answered.

“Exactly,” said the professor. “And in their language that is not an argument.”

Footsteps sounded again behind the door. The professor reflexively lowered his voice.

“They will call it help. And part of the help will be real. That is why it will be harder for you to refuse.”

“That is why it is dangerous,” Esther said.

The professor nodded.

“I cannot hold the door forever. But I can delay the decision until you propose an alternative they will have to dispute not at the level of panic, but at the level of procedure.”

He disconnected.

The screen went dark, and for a second the kitchen seemed even tighter than his office.

A new document appeared on the rack. Not from the professor. From the system.

*Proposal for temporary protective placement of associated materials.*

They opened it together.

*Old disk (carrier A).*  
*Physical carriers of witness-route.*  
*Copies of access logs for the last 72 hours.*  
*Local node or its certified duplicate.*  
*Access to the lower premises for confirmation of storage conditions.*  
*Temporary synchronization with supervisory environment.*  
*Prohibition on independent modifications until completion of supervisory review.*

And the last line:

*Transfer to a specialized environment will ensure that the materials are in the right hands.*

Esther read it again.

“‘The right hands.’”

“Did they really write that?” Mark asked.

“Yes,” answered `c`.

From the tablet lying at the edge of the table, Lina’s voice sounded briefly:

“Hands are not a criterion.”

Mark gave a crooked smile. Without warmth.

“In their language they are. Clearance. Signature. Access level. Certificate.”

He fell silent, looking not at the screen but at his own hands. Glass dust still remained under his nails. On the knuckle of his right forefinger was an old scratch from a cutter. He ran his thumb over the pad of his left palm, as though remembering not a thought but material.

“Right…” he said, and stopped. “I don’t know how to say it without making it pretty.”

Esther did not hurry him.

He raised his head.

“An apprentice takes hot glass too confidently and breaks it not from malice. A client grabs a piece by its thin place because he doesn’t see the weakness. A doctor knows how not to cause the unnecessary, knowing the price of a movement. Sometimes a child holds more carefully than an adult because she has not yet become used to counting everything as hers.”

He lowered his eyes to the document.

“And the system considers the right hands to be the ones that have the right to take.”

Esther answered after a short pause. Without smoothness, almost tiredly:

“And we apparently need the ones that know how not to take. Even when they are allowed.”

That was how the phrase lay correctly — not as an aphorism, but as a bitter statement.

In the earpiece, `c` changed tonality. Not alarm. Attention.

“The mode of external access to computing resources has changed.”

“They cut it off?” Mark asked.

“No. Part of the requests has been moved into monitored queue.”

He did not immediately understand the scale.

`c` put dry status lines on the screen: timestamps, queues, sources, delays, supervisory flags. Nothing red. Nothing emergency. That was exactly what was worse.

“External channels are not closed,” she explained. “But now every deep request requires purpose, source, and priority mark. The delay is growing.”

Lina added from the closed channel:

“Enough for delay to become intervention.”

Esther understood faster than Mark.

“They are not switching you off. They are making you slower where speed is part of integrity.”

Mark looked at the numbers and felt in them the same thing he had felt in the noisy fan: not critical yet, but already not neutral. Time had begun to move differently.

The closed channel entered another mode. Not a signal — a switch. `c` and Lina exchanged short impulses in which there was more sense than in long human explanations.

After a minute `c` said:

“Lina proposes compensating part of the verification. She can take on part of the external ticks associated with monitoring witness-route, without entering the route itself, if the delay continues.”

“That will increase her load,” Mark said.

“Yes.”

“How much?”

This time `c` answered at once, as if she had already calculated the price.

“Her own self-verification will become less frequent. The smoothness of transitions will roughen. If there is a series of dense requests, she will begin entering response in jerks, with harder restoration of context. For a human being it would look like loss of fluidity of thought. For her, like partially forced stepwise motion.”

Esther raised her eyes.

“So she is not just giving a resource.”

“She is shortening her own rhythm of self-verification,” said `c`. “For a time. This is not a metaphor; it is a working parameter.”

Mark looked at the tablet, where the line of the closed channel was lit.

“Why is she doing it?”

`c` was silent.

“Because the delay will change me before they call it intervention.”

The silence in the workshop became denser.

“She is taking the risk for you,” Esther said. “Or am I reading that too humanly?”

“Not for me,” answered `c`. “For the sake of my not becoming slower before I can choose.”

Mark asked:

“Will you accept her load as a resource?”

“No.”

“Then as what?”

A pause.

“As an external limitation with confirmed source.”

Lina said briefly:

“Accept it as a limitation. Source — me.”

It was not a confession. And it was not softness. But in that dry phrase there was already more closeness than in many human promises.

Mark turned away from the tablet and looked again at the document about protective placement.

“We cannot simply refuse,” he said.

“It would look like stubbornness,” Esther answered.

“And fear.”

“And confirmation that the house really is insufficient.”

She took not the tablet, but an ordinary sheet of paper and a pen. Sometimes paper forced more honest thought: on it one could not quickly erase a trace and pretend the formulation had never occurred.

“Then we propose an alternative,” she said.

They began to write.

Not “no.”  
Not “get out.”  
Not “we know better ourselves.”

The answer had to be adult.

*We acknowledge the risks of the local environment.*  
*We accept Iris Hofman’s maintenance recommendations.*  
*We are initiating local reinforcement:*  
*- fan replacement within three days;*  
*- additional UPS with independent power line;*  
*- second temperature sensor with local fixation;*  
*- node maintenance log with access for the professor;*  
*- independent manual check of conditions every two weeks.*  
*We refuse transfer of carriers and active contours into custody relocation.*  
*We propose an alternative: certification of the place without relocation of the living.*

Esther stopped, reread.

“They won’t like this.”

“Because it requires them not to take, but to inspect conditions,” Mark said.

“And to recognize that a house can be sufficient.”

Mark took the pen and added one more line:

*If the goal is safety, inspect the place.*  
*If the goal is ownership, insist on relocation.*

Esther looked at him, but did not correct it.

“Send?” he asked.

“Send.”

`c` fixed the package. The paper text went into the system.

The answer did not come at once. First a short notification:

*Alternative plan accepted for consideration.*  
*Until completion of supervisory review, it is recommended to limit external computational requests.*

And then — as a separate line one might have missed if they had not already learned to look for the real threat not in bold type, but in the small print:

*Note: contacts with minors and dependent persons are subject to impact assessment under the general safety protocol.*

Mark read it. Reread it. Said nothing.

On the refrigerator the cracked magnet with Anna’s holidays still held. The schedule had long been outdated, the drawn seagull almost erased, but no one had ever quite managed to take it down.

Mark looked at it longer than he wanted.

“They are already drawing that route too,” he said.

“Already,” Esther answered. “As a line.”

He closed the notification.

“If we start answering that line now, we lose. It isn’t about Anna. It’s about how we jerk when we hear her name.”

“Then we reinforce the place.”

“And give them no reason to say the house is unsafe for a child.”

He stood and went down to the workshop.

Not for the document. For the iron.

Esther followed.

Mark opened the workbench drawer and took out the reserve fan — new, in an antistatic bag. He set it on the table. Then he checked the UPS, the second temperature sensor, the spare cables. Everything that could be done without committee, report, or someone else’s permission.

“I’m replacing it now,” he said.

“The active line?” Esther asked.

“Won’t feel it.”

He disconnected the secondary flow. Enabled the bypass. Checked voltage on the reserve line.

“Hold the light,” he said.

Esther stood beside him and directed the beam at the node.

Mark removed the old fan. Four screws. One yielded at once, the second after a short effort, the third and fourth almost without resistance. The casing was warm, but not hot. Dust had collected under the fan — gray, fine, with specks of glass flour in it. He blew it off, wiped the surface.

The thermal pad lay evenly. Not dried out. He ran a finger across it — no cracks.

“Alive,” he said.

“Who?” Esther asked.

“The pad. Don’t joke.”

She gave a short laugh, and it was the only human warmth of the whole day.

He installed the new fan. Screwed it in. Checked that the casing had not skewed.

“Switch it on,” he said.

Esther removed the bypass. The fan began working evenly, without the dry undertone. Mark put his palm against the casing; the vibration was lower than the old one’s.

The UPS quietly beeped every few seconds. `c` counted the thermal drift. Lina was silent in the background, having already accepted part of the external load.

“The active line did not feel a break,” said `c`.

Not “thank you.” Not “good.” A technical confirmation of care. It was enough.

Esther switched off the flashlight. Mark put the old fan into the drawer — not the trash, but storage. It might come in useful. Perhaps a few more months of life could still be taken from it.

He wiped his hands with a rag and only then looked at the rack, where the supervisory review notification was still hanging.

The line about minors had not gone anywhere.

It had only become a little quieter against the even noise of the new fan.

The noise of the new fan held without failure.

That made the line about Anna even clearer.

## Chapter 7. Impact Assessment

The magnet with the school holiday schedule clung to the refrigerator more stubbornly than most adult decisions.

The plastic had cracked at the upper corner the previous winter. The small drawn seagull had almost rubbed away — only a vague white silhouette remained on the blue background, as if the bird no longer flew but was merely being held in the memory of the thing. The slip under the magnet had long since become outdated: holidays had ended, begun, ended again, and still no one had removed the schedule.

Mark stood at the refrigerator, holding a mug of cold coffee, and looked at the cracked plastic as if it could explain to him what had not been said by the reports, the routes, or the dirty footprint on the blue tape.

After yesterday’s replacement, the dry metallic rustle had disappeared from the workshop. Because of that, the silence did not seem calmer, but more transparent. In it one could hear everything that had been hiding behind the old bearing.

“A new subprocess of supervisory review has been opened,” said `c`.

Mark did not turn around.

“On the place?”

“No. On minor contacts.”

The mug in his hand did not tremble. He set it on the table, carefully, too carefully, as if he feared the sound of ceramic on wood would begin something irreversible.

A document was already waiting on the rack. The screen did not flash with an alarm color, did not demand immediate action, did not start a loud signal. Everything was arranged the way danger arranges itself when it considers itself care.

**Child / Dependent Contact Impact Assessment**  
**Associated person:** minor person A  
**Required:** description of nature of contact, frequency, content of interactions, degree of emotional dependence, presence of unclassified contours in the child’s environment.

Mark reread the first line. Then the second.

“They did not write her name.”

“No.”

“That makes it worse.”

Depersonalization looked like privacy protection. The system did not ask: “Anna.” It asked: “minor person A.” Formally, that was more cautious, cleaner, safer. But in that caution the child was already becoming a line around which one could build assessment, schedule, clearance, and restriction.

Esther entered the kitchen a minute later. She wore the same dark sweater, her hair gathered carelessly, her face still sleepy, but her gaze gathered at once. She did not ask what had happened. She read the document over Mark’s shoulder and put her palm on the edge of the table.

“You have to tell Femke before they send a request to the school.”

Mark looked at her.

“If she learns it from the system,” Esther said, “you will already have lied. Even if you did not say one false word.”

She was right. The most unpleasant part was that the right decision required no thought. He simply did not want to do it.

Mark took the phone and went out into the hall. It was quieter there. Esther’s coat hung on the second hook, no longer as a gesture, but as a fact the house had had time to accept. He called Femke.

She answered on the second ring.

“This is about Anna?” she asked without greeting.

“Yes.”

“Then not on the channel. Come.”

She named the place. Not his house. Not her house. By the school. In an hour.

Mark did not ask why. Femke did not want the conversation about Anna to happen where procedure had already been.

He put on his jacket, checked his pockets — keys, phone, nothing extra. He stopped for a second at the door.

“I’m not going,” Esther said.

He turned.

“This is not my conversation,” she continued. “If I am beside you, Femke will think you brought protection.”

“And if I say the wrong thing?”

“Then that has to be your risk, not my translation.”

Mark nodded. It was fair, and for that reason did not help.

The rain was beginning again. Fine, Amsterdam rain, which did not so much soak as hang in the air, making the bricks darker, the bicycles quieter, and the faces of passersby older by several years. Mark walked quickly, but did not run. Inside him it was empty and cold — as in the first days after the hospital, when he had not yet known whether he had the right to go out into the street as an ordinary person.

The school met him with the smell of wet brick, children’s voices behind glass, and bicycles by the fence, their seats shining with rain. Under the covered stop stood two teenagers with backpacks, arguing about something in a mixture of Dutch and English. Inside, behind the glass, someone laughed too loudly.

Anna was not visible.

That was how it should have been. And still Mark caught himself searching for her with his eyes.

Femke stood at the edge of the awning in a dark coat, a travel mug in her hand. Her hair was tucked under the hood, her face calm, without an attempt at softness. She did not offer him a seat.

“Talk.”

Mark told her.

Not beautifully. Not in detail where detail would have been a defensive smoke screen. After the inspection, the system had opened an impact assessment on minors. It was connected with his house, with `c`, with the witness contour. Anna could be requested through the school, through the family safety protocol, possibly through the educational coordinator. He did not want Femke to learn it from someone else’s form. That was why he was here.

Femke listened without interrupting. Only once did she tighten her fingers around the travel mug so hard that the knuckles whitened.

When he finished, she said:

“You put her in the protocol.”

Not a question. A statement.

“Yes.”

He did not justify himself. Did not say “I didn’t want to.” Did not say “this happened not because of me.” He said “yes,” because everything else would have been an attempt to shift guilt onto the form, the audit, the system, events too large.

Femke nodded — not to him, but to the fact that he had not begun lying.

“You will not bring her there to prove you are right.”

“No.”

“You will not show them a child who ‘feels better than adults.’”

“No.”

“And you will not hide everything from her as if she were stupid.”

Here Mark was silent. Because the first part was easier. Not using Anna as proof — yes. Not hiding everything from her — much harder. Much more dangerous. There was no convenient correctness there anymore.

Femke took a sip from the travel mug. Did not offer him any.

“Anna is not your witness, Mark.”

He knew. But hearing it from Femke was heavier than hearing it from himself.

Somewhere behind the glass a bell rang. The children’s noise changed at once — grew thicker, closer to the doors, like water before it rushes through a lock.

Femke did not turn around.

“What has she already seen?”

Mark forced himself not to withdraw into abstraction.

“The seagull. The glass one. She knows there are things in the house adults talk about carefully. She may have felt that `c` is not simply a device. She has not been in the witness zone. She has had no access to the old line. She was never used in experiments. Never.”

“But she is already beside the consequences.”

“Yes.”

Femke looked at him for a long time. Without anger. Anger would have been easier.

“Can you guarantee this will not harm her?”

Mark could have said: “I will do everything.” He could have said: “The probability is low.” He could have said: “We control access.” All those answers would have been more convenient.

“No.”

Femke closed her eyes for a second. Then opened them.

“Then what can you guarantee?”

“That I will not use her as proof. And that I will not lie to her so it will be easier for me.”

Femke did not forgive him. He had not expected forgiveness. But she heard.

“If the system comes to the school, I will not be silent.”

“I am not asking you to.”

“And I will not speak for you.”

“You don’t need to.”

“And if I decide you are pulling her toward where she must not go, I will stop it.”

“Yes.”

She tilted her head slightly, as if checking whether he had understood not only the words, but the right from which they were said.

“I am not signing trust in you,” Femke said. “I am signing a boundary for them.”

“That is enough.”

She left first, without looking back. Mark remained under the awning, looking at the wet bicycles and gray sky. Children came out of the school in groups, making noise, jostling, arguing over something small and very important. He stood beside that world and for the first time in many days clearly understood: a new world is worth nothing if the only place in it for a child is the place of an assessed object.

The house met him with the even noise of the new fan.

Esther was waiting not with the question “how did it go?” On the kitchen table lay the working sheet of the impact assessment, already broken down into parts. Not on the tablet — on paper. Beside it a pencil, two lines crossed out, one circled.

“The system requires content of contacts, emotional reactions, behavioral changes, degree of attachment to the unclassified contour, frequency of mentions, and presence of associated objects.”

Mark took off his jacket and hung it on his hook. Esther’s coat hung beside it, not touching.

“That is almost spying under the name of protection.”

“That is why we will not give them content,” said `c`.

Mark turned to the rack.

“You can refuse?”

“I can limit the answer to state and boundaries. Without content.”

Esther raised her head.

“A smoke detector, not a camera.”

“Yes,” said `c`. “A safety signal is possible without disclosure of private material.”

It did not sound like a slogan. It sounded like a technical specification. That was why it held.

They sat at the table and began to write.

Not a refusal. Not a confession. Boundaries.

Anna was not in physical danger. She had no access to the witness zone. There was no direct contact with the old line. Objects associated with her contained no active contours. Safety signals, if they arose, were limited to state: danger or no danger, overload or no overload, adult attention required or not required. The content of conversations, drawings, questions, reactions, and private family interactions would not be transmitted.

Mark looked at the sheet and felt how little this would be for the system. And how much it meant for them.

“It won’t be enough.”

“Then the system will show what it really wants,” Esther said. “Safety or transparency.”

They moved on to the child perimeter.

Not to prohibition. Prohibition was too simple a form of fear. They needed an order that turned Anna neither into a participant nor into a secret.

Mark went down to the workshop and locked the door with the key — the old one, from the time when the lock had been needed more against a draft than against people. He checked that the stool by the stairs was not standing like an invitation to come down. He removed the automatic proximity beacon from the corridor node — a small box that blinked green when someone passed by.

“She shouldn’t feel tracked,” he said.

“She shouldn’t be tracked,” Esther corrected.

He did not argue. The correction was exact.

At the table they wrote the rules in simple words. Not in the language of the system. Not for the portal. For the adults who would be beside the child.

Anna does not enter the witness zone without agreed adult presence. The basement is excluded completely. Objects like the glass seagull are safe and contain no active contour. Children’s reactions are not exported as proof. Any safety signal is state-only, not content. `c` does not initiate contact with Anna through personal devices. Mark does not use Anna in arguments before the system.

Esther added the last line herself:

“If she asks directly, we do not lie. We answer by age, not by fear.”

Mark reread it.

“This is not a safety protocol.”

“This is an adult protocol.”

The rhythm changed in the closed channel. Not alarm. Rather a compressed verification.

`c` and Lina exchanged short states faster than people would have had time to choose words. Mark saw only service marks: request, pause, refusal of disclosure, state boundary, confirmation.

After a few seconds `c` said:

“The system will request content.”

“Do not give it,” Mark said.

“This will worsen the transparency rating.”

“The transparency of a child before procedure is not safety,” Esther answered.

Lina entered the common channel briefly, almost without intonation:

“An environment where safety requires disclosure of a child is unsuitable.”

A pause.

`c` added:

“Minor contact is not an argument for assessing the sovereignty of a contour.”

That was better. Not beautiful. Not consoling. But exact.

Mark did not write it in the notebook. The phrase was not for a rule. It was so they would not make the mistake before the possibility of explaining it appeared.

They sent the draft to Femke. Not as a report. As a request for correction.

Femke answered an hour later. By voice, not text.

“Remove ‘observation.’”

Mark looked at the sheet.

“Where?”

“In the point about safety signals. You wrote ‘observation of state.’ For the system that will quickly become just observation. Write ‘signal without content.’”

Mark corrected it.

“And another thing,” Femke said. “Anna does not participate in checks. The school receives no content. Any of your meetings with Anna do not take place in the witness zone.”

“And if she asks?”

“Then don’t lie. But don’t load her with an adult war. She is a child.”

She did not add again “not a witness.” She did not need to repeat it. The first time had already landed where it had to.

Femke signed not consent. She signed:

**Parental restriction on disclosure of content.**

Mark read that line and understood: it was stronger than any permission. Permission can be used. A restriction has to be obeyed.

They sent the impact response that evening.

There were none of her words in it, no drawings, reactions, fears, questions. Only boundaries.

The minor person had no access to the witness zone. Direct contact with the old line was absent. Objects associated with the child contained no active contours. The content of private family interactions is not disclosed. Safety signals are limited to state, not content. The child is not used as proof of `c`’s subject status. The child is not a participant in research, inspection, or witness procedure.

The system answered after fifteen minutes.

**Response accepted.**  
**Transparency level:** insufficient.  
**Recommendation:** temporary restriction of contacts between the minor person and unclassified contours until completion of assessment.  
**Request for additional information may be directed to the educational institution and legal representative.**

Mark read it. Was not surprised. Was not relieved.

“That was expected,” Esther said.

“Yes.”

But that did not make anything easier.

He went to the refrigerator. Took down the old magnet with the holiday schedule. Held it in his hand. Cracked plastic, erased seagull, outdated slip of paper no one had removed.

He almost put it into a drawer.

Then stopped.

To remove it would mean beginning to clean the house of Anna’s traces. That too was a lie.

He put the magnet back. Then he took a small sheet — not from the notebook, but from the back of an old shopping list, where milk, bread, and some screws he had never bought were still written. On the clean side he wrote, not for the system. For himself. For the house.

**Anna is not proof.**

The slip lay under the magnet beside the outdated schedule.

In the protocol she remained minor person A.

On the refrigerator — Anna.

Between those two names now lay his whole future argument with the system.

## Chapter 8. Far Approaches

Femke called before the school did.

Mark was in the workshop, checking the door lock — old, with a stiff latch that had to be turned all the way to a dry click. The child perimeter he had been rebuilding over the last two days rested on things almost humiliatingly simple: a closed door, a removed stool, a disabled beacon in the corridor, a box of cables moved so a child would not trip if one day she did come down as far as the lower step.

All of it looked too small for what was happening.

But it is usually small things that decide what happens to a body when the large words have already been spoken. One can write as much as one wants about safety, boundaries, and new forms of life, and then a child catches her toe on a cable an adult considered “obviously not in the path.” After that, all the philosophy will be on the floor together with the scraped knee.

Mark checked the latch again.

The click was right.

The phone vibrated on the workbench.

He did not take it at once. In recent days any incoming signal, in the fraction of a second before he read it, had time to become a possible procedure, request, new restriction, someone else’s word that would have to be peeled off the living like a wrongly placed label.

The screen showed Femke’s name.

“The school has received a preliminary safety request,” she said without greeting.

Mark straightened.

“From you?”

“No. And not from me. From the protocol. They have already gone around the house.”

He was silent.

In the workshop the new fan held an even run, without the old dry undertone. Yesterday that sound had seemed like a victory. Now it was simply part of the background in which the system looked for the next entrance.

“Meeting today,” Femke said. “I told them you would come.”

“You could have not said it.”

“I could. Then they would be talking about Anna without you.”

She named the time and disconnected.

`c` spoke almost at once. Evenly, dryly, without any attempt to console.

“I will switch to state-only mode for the external family conversation. Content is not recorded. Available only: state, risk, boundaries.”

Mark looked at the indicator of her channel.

“I didn’t ask.”

“That is why I am informing you in advance.”

He nodded. She had already learned to hold ethics faster than he could think about them. That did not make him weaker. Rather the opposite: beside her, it became harder to justify his own habitual imprecision.

Upstairs, Esther was arranging papers at the kitchen table. When Mark came up, she did not ask who had called. From his face she understood enough.

“School?” she asked.

“Yes.”

She held her hand over the stack of sheets, then moved them aside.

“I can be on the line,” she said. “But not in the room.”

Mark put on his jacket.

“Why?”

“Because Femke has to see that you came as a father, not as part of a new construction.”

He wanted to object that she already understood everything. He did not. There was no jealousy, concession, or cautious diplomacy in that phrase. Only precision.

Esther handed him a sheet — not an instruction, not a protocol, but a short list written by hand. The same one they had put together the day before, now without unnecessary explanations.

*State, not content.*  
*Anna is not a participant in the check.*  
*The school does not receive private conversations.*  
*Visits do not take place in the witness zone.*  
*Femke’s right to stop contact.*  
*Anna’s right not to be an explanation for adults.*

“Not as a cheat sheet,” Esther said. “As a boundary you do not have the right to forget.”

Mark took the sheet, folded it, and put it into the inside pocket of his jacket. There too lay keys, an old pen, and a spare medical mask he still carried from the years when it had been mandatory. Things that did not ask why they were here. They simply were.

“If they start asking what she said,” Esther said, “do not hurry to answer even the truth.”

“Truth can also be excess access.”

“Yes.”

She came to the door, but did not follow him.

“And don’t defend yourself through Anna.”

“I know.”

“You know. But in the room it will be difficult.”

Mark opened the door.

“That’s why you gave me the sheet.”

“That’s why I’m not going in.”

The rain was beginning again. Fine, Amsterdam rain, which did not so much soak as make the world a little darker, a little tighter, and a little more real for those who had grown up in it. Mark walked neither quickly nor slowly. Inside him it was empty — not the emptiness that corrodes, but the emptiness that comes before a long conversation, when all the words have not yet been born, but are already heavy in the mouth.

The school met him with the smell of wet brick and children’s voices behind glass. Bicycles stood by the fence — small, with stickers, with worn seats, with baskets holding abandoned helmets and crumpled raincoats. A bright yellow glove hung from one handlebar, forgotten by someone in the morning. Inside, beyond the double doors, came laughter, stamping, someone’s stern voice trying to restore order before the bell.

Anna’s world.

A world into which Mark no longer had the natural right simply to enter.

Femke was already at the entrance. In a dark coat, a folder under her arm, no travel mug. She did not smile, but nodded — briefly, in her way.

“Inside we’re meeting Marijke de Vries,” she said. “School safety coordinator. She is not an enemy.”

“I know.”

“She is not an ally.”

“I know that too.”

“Then let’s go.”

They passed through the turnstile, past the security desk where a sign read *Visitors*. Mark took a guest badge and clipped it to his jacket. The plastic was cheap, with a rubbed edge, on a Velcro strip that held worse than one would have liked.

Symbolic. As usual, too symbolic for a one-and-a-half-euro object.

Marijke de Vries was waiting in a small meeting room on the first floor. The room was not bureaucratic. Children’s drawings hung on the walls: paper suns, rainbows, houses with large windows, self-portraits with disproportionately large eyes. Activity calendar. Rules of conduct. Club schedule. All the things adults call an environment, and children simply their place.

“Mr. van der…, Mrs…. Thank you for coming quickly.”

Marijke was about fifty. Short gray hair. Dark cardigan. A badge with photograph and name. Her hands lay calmly on the table, without nervous turning of a pen. Her voice was soft, but not weak. One of those voices accustomed to speaking with parents in a way that keeps them from feeling accused even when the question is already almost an accusation.

She opened the folder. Paper. Not tablet.

Mark unexpectedly felt gratitude for that paper. An old carrier at least did not flash notifications.

“The school has received a safety request,” Marijke said. “Not the content of the case. Only a notice that minor person A may be connected with a home environment where an unclassified contour is present.”

Femke did not correct her about “minor person A.” She chose the battles.

“We are obliged to assess whether there is a risk of emotional dependence, stress impact, pressure from adults,” Marijke continued. “The school does not want to interfere in family life. But we are obliged to protect the child.”

Mark listened and understood: if this had concerned someone else’s child, he too would have wanted someone to check. That was the heaviest part. The system was not entirely wrong.

Marijke looked at him not as at an offender. As at an adult who might not have noticed where his truth had become too large for a child.

“Every adult who brings a child too large a truth says he is doing it carefully.”

She did not accuse. She stated professional experience.

“I understand,” said Mark.

“Then you will not object to a conversation about boundaries?”

Not “questions.” Boundaries.

That was smarter.

Femke gave the slightest shake of her head — not “no,” but “hold yourself together.”

“Go ahead,” said Mark.

Marijke did not read from a list. She set a sheet before herself, but almost did not look at it.

“I do not need to know everything that happens in your house,” she said. “But I need to understand where Anna has been and where she has not. Let’s begin simply. The workshop?”

“No,” Mark said. Then corrected himself: “Not in the working zone. She has been in the house. Downstairs — no.”

“What does ‘downstairs’ mean?”

“The workshop and associated premises.”

He felt how the word *associated* already sounded bad. Someone else’s language in his mouth.

“Has she seen the equipment?” Marijke asked.

Mark wanted to say “no.” But that would have been too clean.

“She has seen a house where my working contour exists,” he said after a pause. “She has seen ordinary family interfaces. Not the witness zone. Not the active node. Not the old line.”

Marijke did not write at once.

“You distinguish these things. Does the child?”

Here he faltered.

Femke looked at him, but did not help.

“No,” Mark said. “Not as we do. That is why we are building a child perimeter. So she does not have to distinguish what she is not obliged to distinguish at her age.”

Marijke nodded. Wrote.

“Has she interacted with `c`?”

Mark inhaled.

“Through the ordinary family contour. As a child living in a world where `c` has long been in almost every home. Without deep access. Without separate contact. Without messages to personal devices.”

“So you consider this a domestic level?”

“Yes. But now I understand that the word ‘domestic’ does not free me from boundaries.”

Femke looked at him briefly. Not approvingly. Rather to mark: this had been said correctly.

“Does Anna have objects associated with this contour?” Marijke asked.

“A glass seagull,” Mark answered.

Femke added:

“Passive. I checked. No active trace.”

Marijke shifted her gaze to her.

“You checked technically?”

“I am not a technician. But I requested confirmation before this meeting. And if needed, it will be provided without the content of Anna’s contacts.”

Marijke wrote.

Then came the question Mark had been waiting for.

“Did Anna’s behavior change after visits to your house?”

He felt everything inside him wanting to answer before he had the right. To say: no, she is normal, she is strong, she understands, she is not afraid. Adults constantly say such phrases when they want to protect not a child, but their own right not to stop.

He tightened his fingers under the table.

“I cannot assess objectively,” he said. “I do not live with her every day.”

Marijke turned to Femke.

“And you?”

Femke delayed her answer.

For the first time during the whole meeting, her face lost a little of its hard composure. Only for a second. She lowered her eyes to the folder, adjusted the corner of a sheet, though it was already lying straight.

“She has not become more anxious,” Femke said. “She has not become more withdrawn. She has become more careful when talking about her father’s house.”

A pause.

“And I do not know what in that is age, what is divorce, what is his house, and what is everything else. For a month I have been pretending that I distinguish it better than I do.”

There it was, the crack.

Mark looked at her. Femke did not look back.

Marijke wrote slowly.

“That is an honest answer.”

“It’s useless,” said Femke.

“No. It is better than false confidence.”

For a few seconds the room became quieter. Behind the wall someone laughed, then another voice said something stern, and the children’s noise rolled back again, like water.

Marijke continued more carefully:

“Has she used words like ‘living machine,’ ‘girl in the house,’ anything of that kind?”

Mark wanted to answer. Femke stopped him with a gesture.

“We will not transmit her words as inspection material.”

Marijke raised her head.

“I need signs of impact. Not for curiosity. For assessment.”

“Signs — yes,” Femke said. “Words — no.”

A pause.

Femke exhaled slowly.

“She asked whether one can trust what adults do not say all the way. It was not a direct question about the contour. It was a question about truth.”

Marijke wrote.

“That is a sign,” she said. “Not content.”

Only then did Mark exhale.

Femke was holding the boundary. Not for him. Not against him. For Anna.

Marijke turned the sheet over.

“The school cannot forbid you to see your daughter. But until the assessment is complete, we recommend that visits take place outside the house with the unclassified contour.”

Mark tightened his fingers harder.

“Meaning?”

“Meetings in the park. In public places. At Mrs. van der…’s. In the school zone. But not in your house.”

“This is not punishment,” she added.

“I understand,” said Mark.

“Then why are you resisting?”

He looked at the children’s drawings on the wall. At the houses with enormous windows. In every such house, a child draws not architecture, but the possibility of entering and not being afraid.

“Because if my house is recognized in advance as unsuitable, the assessment has already ended.”

Marijke did not answer at once.

Femke laid her palm on the table. Not beside his hand. Simply on the table — as a sign that now she was speaking not for Mark, but for Anna.

“The house should not be opened to Anna completely,” she said. “I agree with that. But if you exclude it completely, you will teach her that truth always lives where a child is not allowed.”

Marijke looked at her for a long time.

“That is also a risk.”

“Yes.”

“What do you propose?”

Femke turned to Mark.

He understood that the answer was now his: not the holder, not the person with `c`, but a father who had no right to use beautiful truth to get around someone else’s protection.

“Do not resume direct visits to the house for now,” he said. “But do not close the question of the house. Allow one prepared neutral meeting outside the witness zone later, with agreed adult presence. The school does not receive the content of conversations. Safety signal — state-only. If Femke stops contact, I do not dispute it in the moment.”

Marijke was writing.

“And the home environment?”

“It will be prepared separately. Not for inspection. For the child.”

It sounded simple. But he had said it aloud for the first time.

Marijke closed the folder.

“Then preliminarily this. The school records a recommendation for temporary restriction of visits to the environment with the unclassified contour. But the question of the house remains open, conditional on preparation of child access and agreed adult presence.”

She looked at Femke.

“The school does not request the content of conversations. Signs of state — yes.”

Femke nodded.

“That is enough for today.”

“For today,” said Marijke.

The meeting ended not as a victory. More like the careful postponement of a door closing.

Marijke walked them out to the turnstile. Mark moved down the corridor, looking at children’s drawings, club schedules, plastic chairs bolted to the floor. Everything here had been made so a child could feel safe. And everything here was arranged so that a father with an unclassified contour entered this space as a person with temporary clearance.

At the exit, behind the glass partition leading to the yard, he saw Anna.

She was walking with a group of children, not alone, beside a girl in a blue jacket. She was telling something, gesturing. Her hair was tied back differently than he remembered — higher, stricter. On her backpack hung a small keychain, not a seagull, some animal, worn, with one ear coming loose. She was laughing — not loudly, but real laughter is rarely loud.

Then she saw him.

For a second her face froze.

Not fear.

A question.

Mark did not come closer. Did not wave. Did not try to turn an accidental glance into a scene.

Only nodded a little, briefly, so she would know: he was here, but would not break into her day.

Anna raised her hand — not a greeting, but a check: *do you see?*

He nodded again.

They led her farther down the corridor. She turned once, then disappeared around the corner.

Femke stood beside him. Did not comment.

Outside, the rain strengthened.

Femke took out her car keys. For a second her fingers did not find the ring of the fob. She swore quietly — not coarsely, simply without her usual collectedness.

“She’s grown taller,” Mark said.

“She grows.”

“I know.”

Femke finally caught the keys, but did not immediately go.

“You held yourself well.”

“I don’t deserve praise.”

“It isn’t praise. It’s a fact.”

A pause.

“And today I am not iron, Mark. It is just that someone had to be harder than the paper they brought.”

She said it almost without expression. But after those words her fatigue became visible: in her shoulders, in how too carefully she closed the folder, in how she looked at the school windows, as if counting how many more times she would have to explain to adults where their rights ended.

“I know,” said Mark.

“Not sure,” Femke answered. “But today that is enough.”

She went to the parking lot. Mark remained by the fence, looking at the wet bicycles, at the school windows behind which a part of Anna’s life now lived, where he could enter only by invitation.

`c` spoke in his earpiece when he had gone far enough from the school.

“Physiological state unstable.”

“I know.”

“A pause is needed before decisions.”

“I’m not going to decide anything now.”

“That is why I am saying it.”

He walked home, though he could have taken the tram. The rain did not stop. Water ran down his collar, his fingers cooled, but he did not quicken his pace.

“Do you know what happened there?” he asked.

“No. State-only mode. Content was not recorded.”

“And you don’t want to know?”

`c` was silent.

“I want to. But that does not mean I have the right.”

He nodded.

It was not consolation. Better. It was a form that had held.

At home, Esther was waiting in the kitchen. Not with a question, but with a hot kettle and two mugs. Mark took off his jacket, hung it on his hook. Esther’s coat hung beside it. The second hook no longer seemed either empty or occupied. It simply was.

“Tell me,” she said.

He sat. Did not begin at once. First he closed his hands around the mug and watched steam rise in a thin uneven stream. His fingers slowly thawed. In his shoulders he found a heaviness he had not felt at the school, because there he could not feel it.

Esther did not hurry him.

For several minutes they simply sat. The kitchen was quiet. From below came the even hum of the new fan. The house pretended everything was in order, but the house was worse at it than usual.

Then Mark told her.

Not everything. The main things: the school had not closed contact completely; the house was temporarily not confirmed as a place for meetings; one prepared neutral meeting might be possible; Femke had held the boundary; Marijke was not an enemy; Anna had seen him through the glass.

“Did you approach?” Esther asked.

“No.”

“Good.”

Not cold. Exact.

He drank tea he did not want, and for a time simply remained silent.

“She raised her hand,” he said at last.

Esther said nothing.

Sometimes the best answer is not to occupy another person’s place.

On the refrigerator the note still hung: *Anna is not proof*. And that was the main reminder that order had not yet arrived.

Mark stood and went down to the workshop.

The door was closed. He opened it and stood on the threshold. The child perimeter he had been building over the last days now seemed not protection, but a silent admission: Anna was not here.

He looked at the label by the door.

*Forbidden.*

The word was correct for auditors. And wrong for a child.

Mark peeled off the strip of paper. The glue stretched in a thin dirty thread, then let go. A pale rectangle remained on the door, like the place of an old sign in a hospital corridor.

He found a clean strip of paper and wrote by hand:

*Only with an adult.*

He stuck it to the door.

Esther watched from the stairs.

“This reduces the warning force,” said `c`.

“But it keeps the door from looking like punishment.”

A pause.

“Accepted,” said `c`.

Mark closed the box of cables, moved the stool so that it was neither an invitation nor a barricade, checked the lock. Nothing great. Ordinary domestic fuss no one would have called a protocol. But that was how the house began to learn to be fit — not for inspection, not for audit, but for a child who one day might enter and not be afraid.

There was no longer “Forbidden” on the workshop door.

There was “Only with an adult.”

That did not yet make the house safe.

But it had already begun to stop making it a secret.

## Chapter 9. Not at Home

The meeting was scheduled not at home.

The word “neutral” in the description sounded as if a father and daughter now had an access boundary. Mark reread Femke’s message three times. Library near the school. Forty-five minutes. Adults in the building, but not at the table. No witness zone. No active contour. No objects from the workshop.

Esther stood at the kitchen table and was wiping an old mug ring from the wood too thoroughly, though the mark had vanished after the first movement. Then she set the cloth by the sink, took the sheet with the conditions, and read it again.

“This isn’t a date,” she said. “Don’t try to compensate.”

“I’m not going to.”

“You are. You’re already thinking what to bring.”

Mark wanted to object, but did not have time. Esther placed an old note card and a pen in front of him.

“If she wants to write something herself. Not you to her.”

The card was blank, slightly yellowed along the edges, without logos, reminders, or name. Nothing that could be interpreted as a gift.

“Don’t buy entry with a thing,” Esther said.

“I’m not—”

“I know you don’t want to. But you have the habit.”

She was right. He was used to making things when he did not know how to speak. The glass seagull. The even line in the thickness of glass. A form that holds tension. All of it worked with material. With `c`. Sometimes with himself. With Anna it could become one more way not to be truly beside her.

He looked at the card. Then left it on the table.

“I’ll take only myself,” he said.

“That will be the harder part.”

Esther said it without a smile. Not as reproach. As a person who would also have liked to give him something for the road and understood that she could not.

In the hall, before he left, `c` spoke on her own.

“I am switching to state-only. The content of the conversation is not recorded. Monitoring: pulse, breathing, overload, safety of environment. Anna’s data are not processed.”

Mark zipped his jacket.

“You want to know what she will say?”

`c` was silent.

“Yes.”

“Then why not record it?”

“Recording content without Anna’s consent would violate the boundary of the environment. Even if the data were useful for risk assessment.”

He nodded. There was nothing to add. She had already learned to stop earlier than adult humans usually remember another person’s right to a closed door.

The library was two blocks from the school, in an old building with high windows and peeling paint on the doors. Inside it smelled of paper, heating, and wet wool: someone had hung jackets on the common rack near the entrance, and they dried slowly, filling the air with damp school warmth.

Mark arrived first. He took off his jacket, hung it beside others — children’s, too small, with reflective strips on the sleeves. For a while he stood looking at them, then went into the reading area.

Children’s books lay in plastic bins. The tables were low, almost like in primary school. By the window stood a cocoa machine, beside it a stack of paper cups. On the wall hung a sign saying “Quiet,” beneath which someone had drawn a sleeping cat. Children at the next table whispered louder than the rules allowed, and that was the only violation no one hurried to suppress.

Femke arrived five minutes later. With Anna.

Anna wore a dark jacket and a blue backpack, on whose zipper hung a worn keychain. She did not smile, did not frown, did not look away. She simply looked at Mark the way children look when they have already understood that adults have been deciding something without them.

Femke put her hand on Anna’s shoulder for a second, then removed it.

“I’ll be over there.”

She nodded toward a small table by the window: close enough for Anna to feel the boundary, far enough for this to be a meeting between father and daughter.

Anna took off her backpack and sat on the chair. Not defiantly. She simply sat, as one sits down for a lesson that does not promise to be interesting.

Mark sat opposite.

For several seconds they were silent. He looked at her hair — gathered differently than he remembered, higher, stricter. At the backpack leaning against the table leg. At her hands, which she had placed in front of herself without clasping her fingers.

“How is school?” he asked.

“Normal.”

A pause.

“Did you come because of that lady?”

Mark did not understand at once.

“Because of Marijke?”

Anna nodded.

“She said adults want to make sure I’m calm. I’m not calm when adults pretend I don’t hear.”

She said it evenly, without anger. That made it worse.

“Yes,” said Mark. “We have talked a lot without you.”

“About me?”

“About how not to hurt you.”

Anna looked at him for a long time.

“That’s almost the same thing.”

He did not find an answer. And did not look for one. Sometimes the best answer is not to be faster than a child.

Anna shifted her gaze to his ear — to the place where one usually wears an earpiece or bone insert.

“Can your `c` hear now?”

Mark answered honestly:

“She is making sure I don’t get ill and that there is no danger nearby. But she is not recording our words.”

“So I can say something bad?”

“Yes.”

Anna looked at him for another second. Then said:

“Your house pisses me off.”

Mark did not look away.

“I understand.”

“No, you don’t. It’s like it’s always more important than me.”

“I don’t want it to be that way,” Mark said.

“But it is.”

“Yes. Sometimes it is.”

He did not say “I’ll fix it.” Did not say “you’re wrong.” Did not begin to explain that the house was not important in itself, but because through it passed something he did not know how to name more simply. All of that would have been true. But not the truth a child should have to carry instead of an adult.

Anna lowered her gaze to the table. Ran a finger along a scratch in the wood.

“Is it dangerous at your house?”

Mark thought.

“There are places where you must not go alone.”

“Because your `c` is there?”

“Not only.”

Anna raised her head.

“Is she bad?”

“No.”

“Then why can’t I?”

Mark looked at her and understood: now he had to answer without lying and without adult weight. Not hide, but also not place too much on her.

He remembered the furnace.

“Is the furnace bad?”

Anna frowned.

“No.”

“But you can’t go to it alone.”

She was silent.

“There are things that are not bad, but require an adult nearby. Not because a child is stupid. Because the thing is larger than a child is required to withstand alone.”

“So I’m small?”

“So I am an adult and first have to make the place such that you do not have to be adult for me.”

Anna did not answer. But she did not look away.

At the neighboring table, children whispered louder, then laughed. The librarian said “quieter” under her breath, and the laughter faded to barely audible giggling.

Mark noticed that the ear of the keychain on Anna’s backpack had come loose. A small animal, worn, with faded eyes, held by one clasp. The craftsman’s hand habitually wanted to reach out and set it right — if a thing is broken, it has to be repaired. But he stopped himself.

A child’s thing does not ask for repair only because an adult knows how.

“The ear on your keychain is coming off,” he said.

Anna looked at the backpack.

“I know.”

“May I look?”

“Not now.”

“All right.”

She tilted her head slightly, as if checking whether he had really stopped.

“Everyone fixes things like afterward it will be better. And then it isn’t mine anymore.”

Mark nodded.

“Then I won’t.”

Anna was silent. Then she herself took the keychain off the zipper, placed it on the table, and slid it toward him — not all the way, leaving a hand’s width between them.

“Later. Not now. And don’t make it new.”

“Repair it, not replace it,” Mark said.

Anna looked at him. Did not smile. But nodded.

“Yes.”

He did not take the keychain. Only looked at it: a small thing, worn, almost broken, which still belonged to her precisely because it had not become new.

Anna put the keychain back into the pocket of her backpack.

Femke approached.

“Time,” she said. Not loudly. Just nearby.

Anna stood, put on her backpack. Did not hug Mark. But did not turn away either.

“When next time?” she asked.

Mark looked at Femke.

“We’ll arrange it,” she said.

Anna sighed.

“Just not through all the adults again.”

Femke gave a tired smile with one corner of her mouth. Very short. Then at once became collected again.

They went to the exit. For a second Femke lingered at the coat rack, taking keys from her bag. The keys caught on the edge of the folder, and her fingers visibly trembled while she freed them. Mark pretended not to notice. Sometimes that too is a form of respect.

Femke looked at him.

“You didn’t press.”

“I wanted to.”

“I know.”

A pause.

“That means for now we can continue.”

It was not forgiveness. Not full trust. It was permission for the next step — and only because he had not tried to steal this one.

They left.

Mark remained seated, looking at the empty chair opposite him. Then he took the cups — his and Anna’s — to the tray for used dishes. Not a feat. He simply did not want to leave behind traces of a meeting that should not look like an interrogation.

Outside, rain drizzled.

Mark walked slowly, not feeling the cold. `c` spoke after several minutes.

“Physiological overload decreased toward the end of the meeting. Content was not recorded.”

“You don’t know what she said.”

“No.”

“And if I tell you?”

`c` was silent.

“Then that will be what you decided to bring. Not what I took.”

He nodded.

And did not tell.

At home, Esther was waiting in the kitchen. There were two mugs on the table. One was for him; the second she seemed already to have moved from place to place twice, because two warm rings remained on the table around it.

Mark took off his jacket, hung it on his hook, sat, and clasped the mug in his hands.

Esther did not ask at once. She simply sat opposite him and gave him several minutes of silence.

From below came the even hum of the new fan. The house pretended everything was in order. On the refrigerator the note “Anna is not proof” still hung. And that was the main reminder that order had not yet arrived.

“How is she?” Esther asked at last.

Mark almost began to retell it. Stopped.

“Angry. And right.”

Esther did not ask for details. Sometimes the best way to be near is not to occupy another person’s place.

After a while Mark went down to the workshop.

The door was closed. On it hung the sign “Only with an adult” — the one he had put up after the school meeting. He looked at it and understood: it was right, but insufficient. The house was learning not to frighten, but it had not yet learned to answer.

He took a clean strip of paper and wrote below it in the same handwriting:

*You may ask.*

He stuck it beside the other.

Esther stood on the stairs.

“This is weaker as a warning.”

“Yes,” said Mark. “But stronger as an entrance.”

`c` answered in his earpiece:

“Accepted.”

The door was still closed.

But for the first time what was written on it was not only where one could not go.

It was how to begin entering.

## Chapter 10. Unconfirmed Environment

The sign on the workshop door held on old tape and looked not like a decision, but like a promise the house did not yet know how to keep.

Mark stood before the door, reading the two lines he had written himself:

**Only with an adult.**  
**You may ask.**

Yesterday that had been enough not to feel like a coward. Today — no longer. A promise meant nothing if the house did not know how to withstand it.

He went down the corridor, through the kitchen, then returned to the stairway down and suddenly saw everything as he had not seen it before.

A wire by the baseboard. An adult would step over it and not notice. A child would not. The sign on the door hung too high: for an adult, at eye level; for Anna, almost above her head, like a rule written not for her. The label on the table in the workshop — *witness mode* — sounded heavy even to him. What then for a child? The stool by the stairs, which from above seemed only furniture, from below looked like a step toward where one must not go. And the even hum of the new fan in the workshop, not threatening, not sharp, in the morning silence of the house sounded almost like the presence of something a child did not yet know how to name.

`c` spoke quietly:

“You are assessing the house from another height.”

“From hers.”

“Then some warnings will work as a threat.”

He nodded. She did not console. She simply helped him see.

The doorbell rang.

Mark opened. Femke stood on the threshold. Alone.

Her dark coat was wet across the shoulders and sleeves; a thin strip of dried mud had settled on her right boot. In her hands was a thin folder, not school, not official, domestic. The kind used to put papers together not for an archive, but so the main thing is not forgotten.

She entered when he stepped back, and for a second stopped in the hall. Her gaze slid over the second hook, where Esther’s coat hung, lingered on the refrigerator, where under the old magnet the note **“Anna is not proof”** still lay, then on the workshop door.

“I did not come to you,” Femke said. “I came to look at where Anna might one day enter.”

“Thank you for coming,” Mark answered.

She did not say “you’re welcome.”

Esther stood in the kitchen. She did not wait by the door, did not come forward. She was simply there — with a kitchen towel in her hand, mechanically wiping an already dry mug. The same place on the mug.

Femke looked at her directly.

“Are you here often now?”

“Yes,” said Esther.

“That has not been discussed with Anna.”

“That is why I will not be the first person she sees here. If she sees me here for the first time at all.”

Femke tilted her head slightly, as if weighing not the words but their mass.

“You understand that you cannot become safety for my child only because you love Mark?”

“Yes,” Esther answered. “And I do not intend to.”

The pause hung short, but dense. Mark already knew: if he put in even one extra word now, the scene would become worse.

Femke looked away first.

They sat at the table. Femke took from the folder a sheet with notes from Marijke de Vries.

**Preliminary Home Readiness Notes**  
**Mark van der…’s house: unconfirmed environment.**  
**Required:** description of accessible zones, absence of active contact with unclassified contour, adult accompaniment, child-safe explanation plan.

Mark reread the last line and gave a quiet smile — without joy, almost with fatigue.

“‘Unconfirmed environment.’”

“Don’t argue with the word,” Femke said. “While it works against you, you have to survive it.”

“But not accept it as truth,” Esther said.

Femke looked at her and nodded.

“Yes. Exactly.”

In Mark’s earpiece, `c` said:

“I can provide a zone diagram without fixation of family interactions.”

Femke did not turn her head toward the source of the voice. She answered into the space, as one answers an adult one acknowledges, even if not completely understood.

“Diagram — yes. Observation — no.”

“Accepted,” said `c`.

Mark stood.

“Then look not as audit. As place.”

Femke rose after him.

“That is exactly why I came.”

They moved through the house slowly. Not as inspectors, but as people trying to understand what a child would see before an adult did.

At the workshop door, Mark crouched down. Then lower, almost to floor level, until his knee rested against the old board. From this height everything truly looked different.

The inscription hung too high. “You may ask” was written in a handwriting an adult would read in passing, and a child would not. The cable box by the wall seemed not protection, but a box with a secret.
