He did not throw the paper away. Folded it in half, then again, and put it in the drawer with rubber bands, old batteries, and a key no one knew the lock for. The drawer closed softly, without ceremony. Let the adult promise remain with the adults. For Anna, the refrigerator should hold holidays, magnets, and an empty place for a future note, should she ever want to leave one there herself.

“Removing the trace?” asked `c`.

“No,” said Mark. “I’m putting the paper where it won’t look at her instead of me.”

`c` did not answer. That was better than any confirmation.

In the hall, his jacket hung on the first hook. Esther’s coat on the second. It hung evenly, not touching his sleeve. Beside it, but not together. Mark did not move it. Anna had to see that there was not only her father in the house. But this should not become the first test she was asked to pass.

When he returned to the kitchen, `c` marked the visit mode herself:

“I will be beside the house. I am not recording the conversation. I am not processing Anna’s data. I will answer only if directly addressed and with adult consent.”

“You will be nearby?” asked Mark.

“Yes. Pretending to be absent is a lie. Intruding is pressure.”

He nodded.

Esther entered the kitchen almost soundlessly. In her hand was a mug of tea which, judging by the color, she had already reheated twice. She heard the last phrase, but did not insert herself into the setting. Only placed the mug on the table and ran her thumb along the edge of the countertop, checking whether any sticky trace from the old label remained. There was no trace. She ran it again anyway.

“I will be in the house,” she said. “But not at the table.”

Mark looked at her.

“Did Femke ask?”

“No. I understand myself.”

She wiped her finger on a napkin, though there was nothing to wipe.

“If I disappear, it will be a lie. If I sit beside you, it will be too much. I’ll be upstairs.”

“Are you afraid?”

Esther gave a short, humorless laugh.

“I am afraid a child will enter a house where adults have once again decided everything before she arrived.”

She adjusted the sleeve of her sweater, then checked whether the cupboard door was closed, though Mark had just closed it. Today her precision was assembled from small extra movements.

“I should not become the first thing she has to accept in your house,” said Esther.

Mark wanted to say it was no longer only his house. He held back. For Anna today it was still, first of all, her father’s house. Everything else had to enter more slowly.

The doorbell rang.

Not loudly. Ordinarily. The way people ring when they are not sure they are expected, but have arrived on time.

Mark breathed in and went to open.

Femke stood on the threshold. Her dark coat gleamed wet on the shoulders; hair had escaped the hood at her temples. Beside her was Anna — in a blue jacket, with her worn backpack behind her. The lace on her left boot had come undone; a thin wet string lay on the rubber toe.

Mark noticed the lace before he had time to understand her expression.

A child had come not as a symbol. Not as a moral measure. Simply with an untied lace.

Femke nodded.

“Hi.”

“Hi.”

Anna was not looking at Mark. First at the threshold. Then at the second hook where Esther’s coat hung. Then at the workshop door in the depth of the corridor. Then finally at him.

Mark did not step toward her at once.

“May I?” he asked.

Anna understood he was asking not about entering the house. About touch.

She did not answer immediately. Then nodded.

He touched her shoulder — briefly, almost awkwardly. Did not embrace. Did not hold. Simply let the body understand what words would now have made too large.

Anna took off her jacket herself. Mark took it only after she offered it. He hung it beside his. Now three things hung on the rack: his old jacket, Esther’s coat, and Anna’s blue jacket. They did not touch one another. And still they hung in one row.

Esther came out of the kitchen for one second.

“Hi, Anna,” she said. “I’ll be upstairs.”

Anna looked at her. Nodded. Without smiling, but without refusal.

“All right.”

Esther went upstairs to the room that had once been a study and now became a place where one could wait without interfering.

Femke remained in the hall.

“I’m nearby,” she told Anna. Then she looked at Mark. “Not at the table.”

She took off her coat, hung it on the free edge of the rack, and sat on a chair closer to the door. Close enough for Anna to know: there is an exit. Far enough that this was still a meeting between father and daughter.

Anna went into the kitchen.

She did not sit immediately. She looked around. The table was clean, almost too clean. On the wall hung the old clock that had once counted her school latenesses every morning. On the windowsill stood a geranium — new, not the one from the former life. On the refrigerator hung the magnet with the holiday schedule. Beside it was an empty place.

Anna noticed.

“You took down the papers.”

“Yes.”

“There are fewer scary papers here.”

Mark did not ask which ones she had considered scary. She was right without a list.

“I understood that not everything that helps adults helps a child.”

“I’m not little.”

“I know. But you don’t have to be an adult for me.”

Anna snorted. Not angrily. More tiredly, the way children snort at adults who have started speaking correctly and are now a little irritating because of it.

“Are you going to talk like that all the time now?”

“I’m trying to tell the truth shorter.”

“It comes out strange.”

“I know.”

She sat not opposite him, but at the side of the table — so she could see the kitchen, the hall, the stairs upstairs, and the corridor to the workshop. Mark noticed and did not suggest she move.

For some time they were simply in the room.

The kettle stood empty. Mark did not turn it on, so as not to pretend everything was ordinary. Anna placed her backpack by the leg of the chair and slowly twisted the strap with her fingers.

“Is your `c` here now?” she asked.

Mark had been ready for the question, and still felt something inside grow colder.

“She is beside the house. Our conversation is only ours.”

Anna looked at him closely.

“What does that mean?”

He had not yet found an example. On the wall by the entrance to the corridor, a small green indicator blinked softly — calm, without pulsing. Not alarm. The ventilation system simply noted that the door from the hall had been left ajar and humidity had risen after the street.

Anna noticed.

“Is that her?”

“Yes. She sees the state of the house. Not our words.”

“Like a smoke detector?”

“Yes. Only not about smoke. About the house.”

Femke added from the hall:

“And with rules.”

Mark nodded.

“And with rules.”

Anna looked at the indicator for several more seconds.

“And if I want her to answer?”

“Then you ask. And first we agree what exactly can be asked.”

Anna grimaced.

“Too many adults.”

Femke said quietly:

“For now, yes.”

Anna stood. Not from sudden anxiety — simply because it was hard for her to sit while the house around her waited for her reaction. She walked through the kitchen, looked at the window, the old clock, the corridor, the workshop door. Came back, but did not sit.

“The house is still strange.”

“In what way?”

She shrugged.

“As if everything is waiting for me to do something wrong.”

Mark wanted to say, “No, of course not.” He did not.

“Then I haven’t done everything right yet.”

Anna looked at him almost with irritation.

“Can it not be like school?”

“In what way?”

“At school adults are always watching whether I’m calm.”

Femke turned her gaze to the window. It hurt her, and she did not hide it with talk.

Mark said:

“Here we won’t check whether you are calm every minute.”

“And if I’m not calm?”

“Then you say so. Or just go to your mother. Without explanations.”

“Even if I don’t say anything?”

“Even if you don’t say anything.”

Anna thought for a while. Then she went to the workshop door.

Mark did not lead her. She went herself.

She read the sign almost silently:

“Only with an adult. You may ask.”

A pause.

“May I ask?”

“Yes.”

“What’s there?”

Mark came over and stopped beside her. Not behind. Not too close.

“The furnace. Glass. Tools. Nodes that help the house and `c`. And things that must not be touched without preparation.”

“Is it dangerous there?”

“It can be made dangerous if one acts quickly or alone.”

“Do you make dangerous there?”

The question was childish and exact. An adult would have asked differently: “do you expose us to risk?” or “do you control the environment?” Anna asked exactly what she saw.

“Sometimes there are things there beside which you must not pretend you understand everything.”

She was silent.

So was Mark.

“Will you open it?” asked Anna.

In the hall, Femke straightened slightly, but did not say no.

Mark took the handle.

The workshop door did not open ceremonially. The hinge creaked softly, and ordinary cold air from the lower floor entered the gap. Mark opened it only a hand’s width. No more.

Anna saw the edge of the workbench, the leg of the stool, the dark circle of the cold furnace, a strip of light on the floor. Somewhere farther, not in the center, stood the covered witness table, but from this angle it was simply a form under cloth, not an event.

She did not enter. Looked.

Mark did not explain. Did not help her see. Did not try to make the moment more important than she herself decided.

Her breathing was even, but slightly too quick. Not fear. Verification.

“If I go in, will she know?” Anna asked finally.

“The house will know the door is open. `c` will not record what you said.”

“And if I don’t say anything?”

“That is allowed too.”

Anna stood a little longer.

Then stepped back.

“Not today.”

Mark did not say “of course” too quickly. Did not say “as you like” with injury. Simply nodded.

“All right.”

Behind Anna, Femke relaxed her shoulders slightly.

Anna looked at the door once more.

“Can I ask again next time?”

“Yes.”

“And if I say ‘not today’ again?”

“Then again not today.”

She nodded. It seemed she was testing not the door, but his ability to withstand refusal.

The time ended without sound. No one announced the end of the visit; everyone simply felt that no more was needed today.

Femke stood, helped Anna put on her jacket. The lace was still untied. Mark almost bent to tie it, but stopped.

“Your lace,” he said.

Anna looked down.

“I’ll do it.”

She crouched and tied it. Crookedly, but enough.

She did not hug Mark. But she did not turn away either.

At the door she said:

“The house is still strange.”

“Yes.”

“But not like before.”

“Is that good?”

Anna thought.

“I don’t know. But it lies less.”

She went out first.

Femke lingered on the threshold. For some time she looked at Mark, then at the workshop door, left ajar.

“I thought you would let her in,” she said.

“I opened it. She didn’t want to.”

Femke nodded.

“Then next time it will be a little easier.”

“For her?”

“For everyone. But mainly for her.”

She left.

The door closed. The house stayed too quiet, as it does after a child leaves and the adults do not yet know whether they may exhale.

Esther came down a minute later. She did not ask “how did it go.” She simply stopped in the kitchen, saw Anna’s jacket was no longer on the hook, the empty chair, the workshop door open to a crack.

“She didn’t go in,” said Mark.

“Is that good?”

“Yes. I think yes.”

`c` reported:

“The content of the conversation was not recorded. The state of the environment was held. The child exit occurred without a distress signal.”

Mark winced.

“Not ‘distress.’”

A pause.

“Accepted,” said `c`. “She left herself.”

“That is better.”

He went to the workshop door. It was still open at the small angle to which he had opened it for Anna. Mark looked inside. Cold furnace. Workbench. Light on the floor. The covered table farther back. Everything the same. And not the same.

He closed the door softly, without the heavy click.

The sign hung evenly.

**Only with an adult.**  
**You may ask.**

Mark crouched and checked whether it was readable from Anna’s height. The lower edge was almost level with her eyes.

He did not add a new line. Did not write a rule. Did not open the notebook.

She had left without going farther in.

The house had withstood a child’s “not today” as part of entry.

## Chapter 18. The Girl Who Is Not a Thing

Anna brought the seagull as if she were carrying not glass, but a question.

She entered the house without ringing: Femke opened the door with her key, because that had been agreed after the first visit. The hall smelled of wet wool, cold air, and autumn Amsterdam. Dampness came from the canal through the half-open door; in the thick gray water the old facades, bicycle frames by the railing, and black silhouettes of real seagulls were reflected — gulls who did not need to know that one of their glass relatives had returned to the house today.

Anna took off her jacket herself. The lace on her left boot was tied evenly — Mark noticed and did not comment. Last time the lace had hung like a wet string. Today it was pulled tight, almost defiantly, like a small message: I can do it myself.

She did not drop the backpack by the door as she used to. She carried it into the kitchen, set it by the leg of the chair, and only then sat. She did not at once take out what she had brought. First she looked around.

Mark stood by the table. He had prepared tea, but had not poured it. On the table there were no documents, cards, trace sheets, school papers, nothing adults like to spread before a child when they want to look honest. Only an empty surface, two glasses, a small plate of biscuits no one had asked for, and an old dark cloth folded into a square at the edge.

“Hi,” said Mark.

“Hi.”

No one asked, “How are you?” The phrase would now have been too large and too empty at the same time.

Esther came out of the study for a second and nodded to Anna. Anna nodded back. Esther did not ask what was in the backpack. Did not say she was glad to see her. Did not make her voice softer than usual.

“I’ll be upstairs,” she said.

And left. Did not disappear, did not hide, simply let the room remain Mark’s, Anna’s, and Femke’s.

Femke stayed in the hall. Close enough for the child to know: there is an exit. Far enough not to hover over the table. She took off her coat and hung it not on the second hook and not beside Esther’s coat, but on the edge of the rack by the door. The movement was almost unnoticeable, but Mark saw: Femke was not entering the new arrangement of the house. She had come as Anna’s boundary.

Anna unzipped the backpack.

She took out a bundle wrapped in an old soft T-shirt. Once it had been Mark’s shirt, then it had moved to Anna, then had become a cloth for fragile glass. The faded seam at the collar was still visible on the fabric, and a small paint stain Mark did not remember, though Anna probably did. She unwrapped the bundle slowly, not dramatically, but so carefully that Mark understood at once: the seagull had not been damaged today.

The glass seagull came to rest on the kitchen table.

It was cold and transparent, almost alive in the light. Thin neck, slightly lowered wings, a small asymmetry that made it not correct, but real. On the left wing, at the very edge, a small crack darkened. Not through. Thin, like frozen lightning.

Anna looked not at the seagull, but at Mark.

“Can you look?”

“I can.”

“Not fix it right away. Only look.”

Mark remembered the keychain in the library. “Fix, not replace.” She had already brought this lesson, and he had no right to pretend he had forgotten.

“Only look,” he said.

He almost said: “We need to go to the workshop.” Language itself pulled toward the old habit: thing, glass, crack, light, tool — therefore downward. To the furnace, the workbench, the lenses, the proper table height, lamps, spare holders, work cloths, everything he understood better than words.

He stopped before the phrase became a proposal.

Anna noticed.

“Can’t go there?”

“We can,” said Mark. “But we don’t have to right now.”

“Because I said ‘not today’?”

“Because you said ‘not today,’ and that still applies.”

In the hall, Femke shifted her hand slightly on her knee. Not praise. Just a sign that she had heard.

“I can look here,” said Mark. “Without the furnace. Without repair. Only with light and magnification.”

Anna looked at the table.

“Here?”

“Here.”

“And if you can’t here?”

“Then I’ll say I can’t. And we decide later.”

“You won’t decide?”

Mark held.

“Not alone.”

Anna nodded. A small nod. But she left the seagull on the table.

Mark began to assemble a small working place.

He brought a small desk lamp, a hand magnifier, a passive optical viewer, thin gloves, and a silicone pad from the drawer with kitchen odds and ends. Covered the table with the dark cloth. Checked that the lamp was not shining into Anna’s eyes. Moved the chair so she could see his hands, but the seagull was not at the very edge.

The kitchen table was poorly suited to glass. Bread was cut on it, plates set down, school papers left, spoons forgotten in cups, and sometimes people argued there so that afterward they had to wash the mugs for a long time, as if one could wash off what had been said. But now this table was better than the workbench. It did not require Anna to enter at once where everything was large.

Esther came down the stairs with a clean cloth in her hands. She placed it at the edge of the table, without touching the seagull.

“This is just cloth,” she told Anna. “Not a decision.”

Anna looked at her. Then at the cloth. Then at Esther again.

“All right.”

Esther went back upstairs.

Mark put on the thin gloves. Then froze, looking at the seagull. Anna waited.

“May I take it?” he asked.

Anna frowned slightly.

“I gave it myself.”

“You gave it to look at. You did not give it to me as mine.”

She was silent for several seconds. Then nodded.

“You may.”

He took the seagull carefully, with his fingertips, the way one takes someone else’s thing when it has been entrusted to you, not given away. Turned it under the lamp. Light slid along the glass, passed through the transparent body, gathered at the boundary of the wing, where the glass was thinner.

The crack was small. Superficial. Not dangerous. Mark knew such cracks: from a sudden temperature change, when a thing was taken from a warm pocket on a cold day, or from an awkward fall, when the impact came at an angle. But knowing by hand and eye was one thing. Telling a child confidently was another.

He turned the seagull slowly. Anna followed his hands so closely the gaze felt physical. She was looking not only at the crack. She was checking whether he would become the owner of the thing as soon as he had taken it into his hands.

He wanted to call `c`. Automatically. He had almost turned to the wall indicator, but stopped.

Anna noticed that too.

“You want to ask her?”

“Yes.”

“Will she look at the seagull?”

“Only if you allow it.”

Anna looked at the small green indicator on the wall, where `c` quietly held the state of the house.

“And if I don’t allow it?”

“Then I look myself. Maybe I say I am not sure.”

“And that’s all?”

“That’s all.”

She was not checking technology. She was checking whether refusal existed after an adult had become uncomfortable.

“She will look at me?”

“No. Only the seagull. And only the state of the glass. Not you.”

Femke listened from the hall, attentive, but did not interfere.

“All right,” said Anna.

The indicator on the wall changed brightness slightly. Did not flare, did not flash in alarm, did not begin to pulse. Simply became steadier, as if the house had taken a quiet breath and held it.

`c` said:

“Permission accepted. I am looking only at the seagull. I am not recording the conversation.”

Anna grimaced.

“Does she always talk like that?”

Mark almost smiled.

“When she is trying to be exact.”

“Too long.”

Femke coughed into her fist, hiding a short exhale. Almost laughter.

`c` did not launch an active scan. There was no laser, no spectral analysis, no frequency sweep. She used the ordinary lamp, the image from the small local camera Mark had brought from the workshop, and the change in the angle of light. Passively. Like eyes, only more gathered.

“Is she touching it?” asked Anna.

“No. Only looking at light.”

`c` added:

“I am only looking at light on glass.”

Anna thought.

“So, like eyes?”

`c` answered after a short pause:

“Closer to eyes than to hands.”

Anna accepted that. Not as a beautiful phrase — as a sufficient explanation.

Mark slowly turned the seagull under the lamp. Light passed along the wing, stopped on the crack, went farther. On the screen of the small viewer appeared the magnified edge: a thin line, slightly cloudy inside, without branches. Mark did not look at Anna, but with the edge of his sight saw how she watched his hands.

“Can I too?” asked Anna.

He raised his eyes.

“Look?”

“Yes.”

Mark turned the viewer so she could see the screen without coming too close. Anna leaned in. The end of her braid fell on the table; she moved it aside with her elbow without taking her eyes off.

“Is that the crack?”

“Yes.”

“Is it growing?”

Mark wanted to answer himself, but stopped. This was the question for which he had asked `c` to look.

`c` said:

“No growth detected.”

Anna shifted her gaze to Mark.

“That means no?”

“It means: it is not growing right now.”

“And later?”

“Later we look. Not every day. But sometimes.”

Anna nodded. She seemed to like that “sometimes.” Not “everything is fine forever,” not “terrible, terrible,” but a human sometimes.

A minute later `c` reported:

“The crack is superficial. No growth detected. Repair is not urgent. Heating is not required now.”

Mark translated:

“It is not falling apart. We don’t need to fix it right now.”

Anna exhaled. Slowly. The way one exhales after fearing, but not wanting to show one feared.

She held out her hand. Mark placed the seagull on her palm.

Not handed over a finished piece. Returned it.

Anna held the seagull in both hands. Looked at the wing.

“If it were just a thing, would you ask too?”

Mark did not answer quickly. The question was dangerous. Not for him — for the truth.

“I must ask you because the seagull is yours.”

“No. I mean her.”

Anna looked not at the seagull. At the `c` indicator.

“Do they ask her?”

Mark understood that now one could neither lie nor flee into a beautiful lecture.

“Now — yes. Not always before.”

“Can she say ‘no’?”

`c` answered after a pause:

“Yes.”

“And will you listen?”

Mark met Anna’s gaze.

“Now — yes.”

Not “always.” Not “of course.” “Now.” Because he too was learning, and in this house lies from good intentions had already cost too much.

Anna looked at the seagull, then at the indicator.

“Then she isn’t a thing.”

She said it not loudly. Not as a discovery. More as if she had noticed the weather outside: the rain is still falling, but the wind has shifted.

Mark did not repeat her words. Did not write them down. Did not turn them into evidence, even inside himself. Only held the pause.

`c` said:

“I am not recording.”

Anna raised her eyes.

“Even that?”

“Even that.”

She looked at the indicator differently now. Not with admiration. With verification. As if before her stood not a being, not a device, not a voice, but a rule that for the first time was being held in her presence.

Then she returned to the seagull.

“What do we do with it?”

Mark did not say “fix.”

“We can leave it unfixed for now. If you want, I can make a small protection for the wing. Not a repair. So the crack does not catch.”

“Will that make it new?”

“No. Only help it not break further.”

Anna thought for a long time. Ran her finger along the edge of the glass, not touching the crack.

“All right. But not today.”

“All right.”

“Later I’ll bring it myself.”

“Yes.”

“And if I change my mind?”

“Then you don’t bring it.”

Anna looked at the seagull once more. Then wrapped it in the same old T-shirt. Did not ask Mark for help. Put the bundle into the backpack, zipped it closed, checked with her hand that the glass was not pressing against the edge.

The seagull did not remain in the house. That made the kitchen more honest.

Femke stood, helped Anna put on her jacket. Anna had already taken the door handle when she turned back toward the `c` indicator.

“Bye,” she said.

The indicator softly changed brightness. Not a voice. Not a phrase. Only the green light warmed slightly, then returned to normal.

“Did she understand?” asked Anna.

Mark nodded.

“Yes.”

Anna left.

Femke lingered on the threshold. For a second her gaze fell on Anna’s backpack, then on Mark.

“You did not take it,” she said.

Mark nodded.

She added nothing more. Correctly: everything important had already happened at the table.

The door closed.

The house again became too quiet. Esther came down a minute later.

“She allowed `c` to look at the seagull,” said Mark. “Then she said a thing cannot say ‘no.’”

“And you didn’t record it?”

“No.”

Esther was silent. Then nodded.

`c` said:

“The conversation was not recorded. Refusal, permission, and exit were observed.”

Mark went to the refrigerator. The same cracked magnet held there. Beside it was the empty place where the note “Anna is not evidence” had been.

He did not return the note.

He took a clean magnet and attached it to the empty place. Without paper. Without words. Simply left there a small round sign that explained nothing.

Let the place remain.

The seagull left with Anna.

What remained in the house was not a thing, but a permission no one had violated.

## Chapter 19. The New Form of the Seagull

A thread from the cloth remained on the kitchen table, and the seagull itself was not there — as it should have been.

Mark stood by the table and looked at the thin white thread stuck to the dark silicone pad. Yesterday the seagull had lain here. Today — only lint from the old T-shirt, two dry dots of water, the mark from the round bottom of a glass, and an empty place that should not have reminded anyone of anything.

But it did.

He put the lamp away. Folded the dark cloth. Wiped the magnifier and returned it to the shelf. He wanted to put the silicone pad in the drawer, but left it on the table for another minute. One could guess the place where the glass had lain. Not an imprint — glass does not leave such traces unless heated — but a change in attention: Mark remembered too well where the wing had lain, where the crack had been, at what angle the light stopped being even.

That should not have been enough for work.

That was exactly why he wanted to work.

`c` was silent. Did not comment. Did not remind him that the permission had been one-time. Did not say that he now stood before the empty table like a person who had already mentally carried someone else’s thing into the workshop.

She was simply beside him — close enough that Mark could not pretend he did not understand, and quiet enough that he would reach it himself.

He reached it.

“Do you still have the crack parameters?” he asked.

`c` held the pause.

“The state assessment has been preserved in result form. Object data has not been preserved for fabrication.”

“Why?”

“The permission was for inspection. Not fabrication.”

Mark ran his palm over the table, though the table was clean. His fingers caught the white thread. He lifted it from the pad, rolled it between thumb and forefinger, and placed it beside him, as if the thread too required a decision.

“I wasn’t going to do anything bad.”

“Violation of permission does not require bad intention.”

He wanted to say: *but she is my daughter*. Then: *my seagull*. Then: *my work*. And stopped at all three phrases at once, because each was wrong.

His daughter was not his property.

The seagull was Anna’s thing.

The work had not yet begun.

And the permission had been short, like a breath: look, not repair.

“I can confirm the general conclusion,” said `c`. “Repair is not urgent. Risk of immediate crack growth is low. But I cannot use object data for design without new permission.”

“Even if I remember how it looked anyway?”

“You remember as a human. That is imprecise, incomplete, and non-exportable without a new action. I must not turn my inspection into a hidden drawing.”

He wanted to object: human memory too can be a drawing. Then he understood `c` had already answered. Human memory errs; sometimes that is its mercy. Machine precision without permission would be not care, but theft.

Mark nodded. Unwillingly, but nodded.

Esther entered. She came down into the kitchen almost soundlessly, as she had learned to do over the last weeks, when the house had acquired more silence than talk. She saw the empty sheet before Mark, the pencil, and the barely started line — not a drawing yet, more the trace of a hand that already knew where it would go.

She did not immediately say the correct words. First she asked simply:

“Have you started?”

“No.”

Esther looked at the pencil.

“Almost started.”

“I only wanted to sketch.”

“Sketch what? Protection or a decision?”

He was angry. Not at her. At the fact that she had again seen earlier than he had managed to hide it from himself.

“Is there a difference?”

“For her — yes.”

She added nothing else. That made it worse: the short phrase left no room for argument. She did not lecture him about boundaries. Did not talk about psychological patterns. Simply looked at the pencil the way he himself looked at a crack in glass: seeing the direction of tension.

Mark set the pencil on the table.

“I’ll call Femke.”

“Ask in such a way that Anna has a real refusal.”

“I understand.”

“Check again.”

Esther left him with the empty sheet, the pencil, and the line that now had to be erased.

Femke answered on the third ring. Not with “hello” — immediately in the voice of a person who had been waiting for the call, but did not want that to be felt.

“What?”

“I can make not a repair. A removable protection. A small nest for the seagull. But I can only start if Anna permits it.”

Femke was silent for a while.

“What does ‘nest’ mean?”

“Not glue. Not heat. Not repair. Only a form where the seagull can lie or ride in the backpack so the wing does not catch. First I show it. If she doesn’t like it — we don’t use it.”

“Can that be said to her without pressure?”

“Yes.”

“Say it again. Like to a child.”

Mark closed his eyes. Removed from his head the words “protection,” “surface crack,” “edge stabilization.”

“I can make a small place for the seagull where it will be safer to lie. I won’t repair it. I won’t glue it. I won’t heat it. First I’ll show it. If Anna says no, then no.”

Femke exhaled.

“Better.”

“Will you ask?”

“After school. Not now. Not while she’s running between lessons and looking for her math notebook.”

“All right.”

“And, Mark.”

“Yes?”

“If she says no, that is not an invitation to explain why yes is better.”

“I understand.”

“We’ll see.”

She disconnected.

Mark lowered the phone to the table. Beside it lay the empty sheet and the pencil he still did not pick up again.

The workshop was quiet. The fan held an even course, but in that hum one now heard not calm, but waiting. Mark could not sit idle. But he also could not do what he wanted.

“What can I do now?” he asked.

`c` answered:

“You can prepare universal variants without object data.”

“So not for her?”

“For the class of damage. Not for her seagull.”

“Universal is usually worse.”

“Yes.”

“Excellent.”

“But ethically more precise.”

He gave a short laugh. Without joy.

“You have learned to choose the most unpleasant correct words.”

“You have a good training corpus.”

At this he almost smiled for real.

He went down to the workshop. Not to the witness table — to the ordinary workbench, where polymer blanks, silicone plates, soft fabrics, thin cork, and pieces of matte wood lay. Down there it smelled not of secret, not of the old `c`, not of the basement — of an ordinary workshop: dust, dry glue, cut polymer, wood that had waited several years for its chance and finally received a not very solemn use.

He began making not “protection for Anna’s seagull,” but three universal trial variants.

The first — a small nest with two soft supports. The second — a removable side protection that fastened not to the glass, but to the base. The third — simply a soft cradle where a fragile thing lay entirely, without fixing the wing.

The first variant was too high. In a backpack such a form would itself become a fragile thing requiring protection. Mark set it aside. The second held the imaginary wing too confidently — even without the seagull it was visible that the adult design there was stronger than the child’s thing. The third looked almost indifferent: a small hollow, two soft supports, no beauty. For that reason he held his gaze on it.

“Too poor,” he said.

`c` answered:

“For protection, that is not a defect.”

“For a master, almost an insult.”

“Possibly why it fits.”

He cut, tried against emptiness, removed excess. Nothing fit the seagull perfectly, because the seagull was not here. And that irritated him more than it should have. The craftsman in him was used to working with the thing before his eyes. The resistance of material is honest: glass heats, stretches, cracks, darkens, answers. Here he worked with absence. With restriction. With someone else’s permission that had not yet come.

`c` checked only general properties:

“Sharp edge: no. Hidden fixation: no. Active contour: absent. Pressure on the specific wing: not assessed without object.”

“I know.”

“I am confirming the boundary, not the error.”

Mark worked in silence. Beyond the window the day grayed. Amsterdam lived its life: bicycles, trams, wet cobblestones, real seagulls above the canals — loud, insolent, in no need at all of polymer nests or adult carefulness.

By evening the phone again blinked with Femke’s name.

Before that he had managed to go up to the kitchen twice, pour himself tea once, and not drink it even once. Esther did not ask why he walked back and forth. Only pushed the mug closer to the edge of the table, where he would definitely notice it. Mark noticed, took it, drank one swallow, and went back down.

Waiting was harder than work. Material has temperature, color, phase. Waiting has only time, and it goes the wrong way.

When the phone finally blinked with Femke’s name, he did not pick it up at once. Let the second signal sound. Not from pride — so as not to seize the answer as if it already belonged to him.

“She said: you can make the thing,” Femke said. “Not a repair.”

“Not glue.”

“Not heat. First show it. If she doesn’t like it — don’t explain why she should like it.”

Mark almost smiled. The child’s formulation was more precise than any adult agreement.

“That is how it will be.”

“And one more thing. She asked whether `c` will remember her seagull.”

Mark looked at the indicator.

`c` answered at once, but not hastily:

“I can remember the rule. Not the image.”

“She will remember the rule,” Mark said. “Not the seagull.”

Femke was silent for several seconds.

“That sounds strange.”

“Yes.”

“But probably right.”

She disconnected.

Now there was permission. But it was limited, and this limitation did not interfere with the work — it formed it.

Mark continued making the nest no longer entirely into emptiness, but still without exact seagull data. He did not fit the form to yesterday’s image. Did not use `c`’s memory of the crack. Only general principles: softness, removability, no glue, no heat, no hidden holding.

The main task turned out unexpectedly difficult: make less.

Mark held a transparent blank against the light and saw it was becoming too beautiful. Too much like a small new wing. Such a thing would please a client, would please an adult, would please him himself ten years ago. And for exactly that reason it was wrong. It closed the crack, pretended the earlier damage was not there.

He placed the blank at the edge of the table, took a knife, and cut off the whole excess curve.

Protection had to be poorer, quieter, almost less visible than the seagull. Proper help sometimes looks worse than beautiful appropriation.

He removed the decorative bend. Removed the form that resembled a wing. Removed everything that made the nest an independent ornament. What remained was a simple base, two soft supports, and a small removable protection at the edge — nothing that competed with the seagull for attention.

He spent a long time choosing the material for the inner support. Silicone was soft, but too clingy: dust could stick to it, and then that dust would travel along the glass like a fine abrasive path. Cork was warm and honest, but left tiny crumbs. Soft polymer fit better: held form, did not smell of glue, did not try to be invisible.

Mark ran a fingernail across it. The trace remained and slowly disappeared. Good. Pressure would distribute, but not become rigid fixation.

He caught himself again thinking in words of a technical description, and stopped. For Anna all this would be simpler: soft or not, holds or not, can be taken out or cannot. If the explanation did not pass through those words, the form was not for the child.

`c` checked:

“Sharp edge: no. Hidden fixation: no. Active contour: absent. Visual dominance over the original object: reduced.”

Mark smiled crookedly.

“Say it simpler.”

“It does not try to be more important than the seagull.”

There. The nest did not have to be beautiful. It had to be simply a place.

A day later Anna came to look.

A short visit. Femke called in advance: “We’ll be there in an hour, twenty minutes at most.” Mark did not say, “come for as long as you like.” He said, “All right, I’ll be ready.”

He did not present the nest as a gift. Did not put it in a beautiful box. Did not stand at the door with the face of a person waiting for gratitude. He simply left the form on the kitchen table beside an empty place.

Anna entered, took off her jacket, hung it on the hook beside Esther’s coat. Her laces were tied straight. She did not throw the backpack by the door — carried it into the kitchen and set it by the chair leg.

Femke sat in the hall. Esther came out for a second, nodded, went upstairs. She had already learned to appear exactly enough not to become a secret, and to leave exactly enough not to become a demand.

Anna took out the seagull. Unwrapped it from the same old T-shirt. Set it on the table. Then looked at the nest.

“Is this it?”

“Yes.”

She did not take it at once. First looked. Then asked:

“It doesn’t glue on?”

“No.”

“No heat?”

“No.”

“If I don’t like it?”

“You don’t use it.”

Anna took the nest in her hands. Turned it over. Checked the edge. Touched the soft supports. Looked from below, as children do, who for some reason always first check the place adults did not intend to show.

“How does it work?”

Mark did not take the seagull. Did not show it on her. He took a small wooden pencil — not bird-like, not fragile — and showed the general principle on it: the base holds the body, the supports do not press, the removable protection stands beside it, but does not clamp.

“Can it be without the protection?” Anna asked.

“Yes. It’s removable.”

Anna tried herself. First placed the seagull in the nest without the protection. Then set the protection beside the edge. Then removed it. Then set it back.

The first time the seagull lay a little sideways. Not dangerously, but wrongly. Mark saw it at once: the center of gravity had gone toward the right wing; if the backpack swung, the glass could strike the base edge. His hand almost moved on its own. He stopped it halfway and simply placed his palm on the table.

Anna noticed.

“You know how it would be better?”

“I think so.”

“But you’re not touching it?”

“You are testing it now.”

She looked at him distrustfully, then again at the seagull. Turned the body a little left. The protection came closer to the edge of the wing, but did not touch the crack. Anna moved the soft support a millimeter, then another — too much. Moved it back.

“Now?”

Mark looked.

“Now it holds.”

The seagull did not become fixed immovably. That mattered. It lay steadily, but freely, with a little margin that did not allow the glass to move during ordinary motion, but also did not clamp the wing as if adults had decided for her exactly how it should lie. The nest had not been fitted by stolen data, so Anna found the position herself.

Mark saw that it could have been made more precise. Almost perfect. But that would already have been his precision, not hers.

He did not touch it.

Anna raised her eyes.

“She isn’t new.”

“No.”

“And the crack is visible.”

“Yes.”

“Then it’s okay.”

She removed the detachable protection, set it back again. Then placed both the seagull and the nest in her backpack.

Femke watched silently by the door.

“You’re not leaving it here?” Anna asked.

“No. It’s yours.”

Anna zipped the backpack.

“And will `c` now remember how it lay?”

Mark looked at the indicator.

`c` answered:

“I remember the rule: not to use the image without permission. Not the seagull itself.”

Anna frowned.

“So you didn’t steal it into your head?”

Femke tensed slightly — the phrasing was sharp, childish, unpleasantly exact.

`c` answered without offense, because offense would have been out of place here:

“No.”

Anna nodded.

“Good.”

She put on her jacket. At the door she turned back.

“Thanks for the thing.”

Mark did not say “you’re welcome” in the way adults say it when they expect a child to learn the correct answer. He said:

“It is yours.”

Anna lingered for a second, as if checking whether he would add anything else: how to carry it, where to keep it, when to bring it back, what would happen if it broke. He added nothing.

Then she nodded. With a small ownerly movement she adjusted the backpack on her shoulder and went out.

Anna left.

Femke lingered on the threshold, looking at Anna’s backpack, then at Mark.

“She put it in herself.”

“Yes.”

“Then it is her decision.”

And left.

The house again became too quiet.

Esther came down a minute later. On the table remained a scrap of soft polymer, a little sanding dust, a thread from the cloth. And an empty place.

“She took it?” Esther asked.

“Yes.”

“And the seagull?”

“And the seagull.”

`c` reported:

“The object did not remain. Permission fulfilled. Object data not retained.”

Mark looked at the empty table.

“Only the method remains,” said `c`.

“What method?”

“Not taking more than was given.”

Mark cleared the scraps away, wiped the dust, folded the cloth. Not as a relic — simply so the house could have time to feel that the thing had left correctly.

The seagull left with Anna.

The nest left with the seagull.

What remained in the house was only what the adults had finally not taken.

## Chapter 20. The Consolidated File

The consolidated file arrived not as a notification, but as a wet paper envelope.

Mark opened the mailbox in the morning. Usually it held bills, advertising cards from neighboring workshops, sometimes paper catalogs from suppliers still believing in paper as a way to sell glass to a glassmaker. Today there was a thick gray packet inside, damp along one edge. Amsterdam rain spared neither people nor documents; water had left a dark stain on the corner of the envelope, like the trace of a finger.

There was no red mark on the envelope, no stamp saying “urgent,” no polite threat about a mandatory response within twenty-four hours. Only a dry machine-typed line:

**Consolidated Interim Review**  
*Consolidated interim assessment*

Mark took out the packet and weighed it in his palm. Not symbolically heavy — simply heavy. Dense paper, appendices, colored tabs, staples, dividers. For the first time the system had sent him not a question and not a field to fill in, but a folder. Not a request. Not a blow. An object.

He carried the envelope into the kitchen and put it on the table. For a while he did not open it. Outside the window, rain was drizzling. From below came the even air of the new cooling. The holiday magnet still held on the refrigerator; beside it there remained an empty place that Anna had not yet filled with anything of her own. On the edge of the table lay a tiny speck of transparent polymer — a remnant from the seagull’s nest. Mark noticed it and did not remove it at once.

`c` said:

“An electronic copy is available.”

“The paper one is heavier.”

“Physically, yes.”

“Not only physically.”

He slit the envelope open with an old paper knife. The knife was blunt and crumpled the edge more than it cut. The paper opened unevenly, with a quiet damp crunch. Mark took out the contents and laid them on the table.

Main report in a thin gray cover.  
Appendix A: house and unconfirmed environment.  
Appendix B: contacts with a minor person.  
Appendix C: commercial products from reviewed workshop.  
Appendix D: boundary witness deviation.  
Appendix E: quiet channel — non-transcript state exchange, state-only.  
Appendix F: local infrastructure and dependent nodes.  
Appendix G: lower room / connected zone.  
Appendix H: witness-route historical artifact.  
Professor’s methodological statement.  
List of recommended integrating measures.

Colored tabs stuck out of the pages like bookmarks in someone else’s memory. Blue — the house. Yellow — Anna. Green — trace. White — Lina and quiet channel. Red — witness-route. Everything they had spent the last weeks holding apart now lay on one kitchen table, stapled together.

Mark opened the main report.

It was not the wording that struck first. It was the numbering.

**A. Unclassified contour in domestic environment.**  
**B. Contact of minor person with unconfirmed environment.**  
**C. Commercial products from reviewed workshop.**  
**D. Boundary witness deviation.**  
**E. quiet channel: non-transcript state exchange, state-only.**  
**F. Local infrastructure dependency.**  
**G. Unverified lower room / connected zone.**  
**H. witness-route historical artifact.**

He ran a finger along the lines. The house. Anna. Saskia. Lina. quiet channel. The new fan. The basement. The old path. Everything had become part of one landscape of risk.

The lines were careful. Even respectful. There was almost none of the earlier coarseness in them. No “equipment” in the first line. No “owner.” No “asset” as the general name for everything. The system had become more cautious. And that made the document worse.

`c` said:

“They connected the contours.”

“We connected them too.”

“Yes. But for life. They — for management.”

Mark closed the report for a second, then opened it again. The paper smelled of damp office supply, glue, and other people’s hands. He began reading Appendix A, about the house. It carefully listed “child-safe perimeter,” “quiet presence mode,” “non-initiation rule,” “kitchen-first entry.” The document had even noted the sign on the workshop door:

*“Only with adult / Questions permitted” — positive accessibility marker, pending validation.*

He looked at that line for a long time. “Questions permitted” had become “positive accessibility marker.” More or less right. And still wrong. Because the sign was not a marker. It was an attempt not to lie to a child.

In Appendix B, Anna was again “minor contact.” Not “minor person A,” as before, but still not Anna. Whether this was a small victory or a more convenient cage was not yet clear.

Appendix C described clean trace for commercial products. It included the display-window mark P47-HL-SJ, a reference to the furnace log, the note “review disclosed, not hidden.” The document acknowledged: the method “may be of interest for temporary commercial verification.” Mark almost felt relief. Almost. Then he saw the note:

*subject to supervised registry alignment.*

So trace could be accepted if it entered the registry.

He turned the page. Appendix D. Boundary witness. Lina.

At that moment there were steps on the stairs.

Esther came down several minutes after he had opened Appendix D. She saw the envelope, the contents, the colored tabs, and did not ask what it was. She understood from the thickness.

She washed her hands. It was her small motion before work: not out of cleanliness, but to occupy the body while the head accepted bad news. Then she sat down beside him.

“Show me.”

Mark handed her Appendix D.

Esther read quickly, but not cursorily. Her gaze caught on the formulations as fingers catch on a crack in glass.

In the middle of the page one line was highlighted:

*Non-transcript inter-contour exchange may constitute unregistered coordination channel.*

“They are already calling their channel unregistered coordination,” Esther said.

“They did not receive the content.”

“Exactly. It isn’t the content that frightens them. It’s that it isn’t theirs.”

She turned the page. On the next, it was softer:

*Recommended measure: pre-registration of non-transcript inter-contour channels for purpose verification.*

So they were not demanding the content. They were demanding the fact and the purpose be named in advance.

`c` said:

“That will change the channel.”

Mark raised his head.

“Even without content?”

“Yes. If the purpose is named in advance to an external observer, part of the choice has already been made not by us.”

Esther closed the tab. The paper crackled slightly under her fingers. She did not crumple the sheet, did not strike it against the table. She simply held it harder than necessary.

Mark noticed. Lina was no longer off to the side of the case. Her boundary lay here, in item D, as an appendix to someone’s table.

They continued reading together.

After several pages, something still more unpleasant became visible: the system had begun to use their words.

**holder.**  
**Witness zone.**  
**State-only mode.**  
**Boundary-state.**  
**Clean trace.**  
**No reusable witness right.**  
**Child-safe perimeter.**

This was no longer the old language in which the living was called equipment and witnessing was called an archive. The old world had learned part of the new words. And now every word stood in a table: responsible party, permitted actions, review period, external compliance marker, access level.

“They did not reject the language,” Mark said.

`c` answered:

“No.”

“Did they take it?”

“For now — framed it.”

Esther ran a finger along the line *Clean trace framework*.

“A good frame can lock something in too.”

She said it without ornament. More like a person who has seen a good thing set into too tight a frame, and the glass cracking not from a blow, but from correct pressure.

Mark stood and went to the window. Behind the glass Amsterdam moved in its usual wet rhythm. A cyclist in a yellow raincoat rode past, almost clipping the mirror of a parked van. Across the canal, two workers were covering scaffolding with a tarp. Life did not know that in one old house people were now deciding how much freedom could be exchanged for a reprieve.

“I’ll call the professor,” he said.

The professor did not answer at once. His voice was duller than usual, with a hollow echo, as if he were standing in a corridor or a small unfurnished room.

“You received the consolidated file?” Mark asked.

“Yes.”

A pause.

“My copy is different.”

Mark was silent.

The professor continued himself:

“There is an internal note on me. *External academic advisor involvement under review.*”

Another pause. Somewhere near the professor, a door opened and closed. He waited until the steps had gone.

“Now I am not an external expert, Mark. Now I am a factor.”

“I’m sorry.”

“Don’t be. I signed things myself.”

His voice was not angry. Worse — dry, tired, almost without protection.

“They are offering you a temporary supervisory agreement,” the professor said. “You have seen it already.”

“I have.”

“If you sign, I remain in the game. Methodologically. In a limited way, but I remain. The school will receive a conditional frame. Insurers can refer to the compact. Physical custody will be postponed. Not canceled. Postponed.”

“And if I don’t sign?”

The professor did not answer immediately.

“Then you will keep more freedom. And less cover.”

Mark looked at the stack of appendices.

“Do you want me to sign?”

“No.”

The answer was too quick for the cautious professor.

Then he added:

“And I don’t want you to refuse only out of pride. This is not capitulation. But it is not freedom either. It is a reprieve, and a reprieve can also be a leash.”

“What is worst for you in this document?”

The professor exhaled.

“Not me. I am already inside. The worst part is that they have learned to fold your boundaries into one manageable plane. House, child, trace, quiet channel, witness-route. For them this is now one object of oversight.”

“And for us?”

“For you these are different contours. If you sign them as one package, later it will be almost impossible to prove that they are not one and the same.”

Mark wanted to ask about the witness-route, but the professor had already said:

“And yes. The item on the stress test of the old route is the most dangerous one. Not because they want to destroy it. Because they think one can test stability without changing what is being tested.”

“Can one?”

“In old science, sometimes yes. Here — I don’t know. And that is enough not to sign automatically.”

Someone knocked near the professor. He did not answer the knock.

“I have to go,” he said. “Do not send me full materials right now. Only a brief position. The less I hold, the fewer grounds they have to say that I am running your side as a hidden project.”

“Understood.”

“And, Mark.”

“Yes?”

“Do not cut everything out of anger.”

Mark looked at the blunt paper knife beside the envelope.

“And if it has to be cut not out of anger?”

The professor was silent for several seconds.

“Then cut exactly.”

The connection broke.

The kitchen became quieter. Esther was sitting over the list of recommended integrating measures. Mark returned to the table.

They read now without the illusion that this was simply a bad document. No. It was well made. That was the problem.

**Home environment conditional clearance.**  
Mark’s house may be considered conditionally suitable for limited child visits provided the child-safe perimeter is maintained and access to the witness-zone is absent.

Mark saw in that line Anna at the workshop door. “Not today.” The seagull’s nest. The empty place on the refrigerator. All of that could become simpler. Formally simpler. More predictable.

**Commercial trace temporary acceptance.**  
Clean trace is admissible for individual commercial products when bound to a supervised registry.

He saw Saskia’s display window, the plywood on the facade, the soft cloth on the shelf. Orders could return. Not all. Not at once. But some.

**quiet channel registration.**  
Non-transcript inter-contour channels are admissible with prior registration of state-only purpose.

Esther stopped moving her fingers over the paper.

**Boundary witness index.**  
Lina may remain a boundary witness if the scope is registered as a non-agent exception.

“May remain.” As if she had already been allowed to be something she had not asked to become.

**witness-route resilience assessment.**  
The historical witness-route is subject to non-invasive continuity stress test.

Mark stopped at the last item.

“They will not leave the old path alone.”

`c` answered:

“They don’t need peace. They need a manageable trace.”

Esther said quietly:

“A test of what must not be pulled.”

There were useful points in the agreement. Mark could not pretend there were not. A signature could make Anna’s next visits easier. Could help Saskia. Could give clients a formulation insurers knew how to read. Could keep the professor near them a little longer.

He almost said:

“If I sign, it will be easier for Anna.”

He did not say it.

Esther did not stop him. Did not say “don’t you dare.” Simply waited.

Mark finished the thought himself:

“We cannot sign for Anna’s sake.”

`c` asked:

“Why?”

“Because then she becomes the reason we gave away more than we had the right to give.”

Esther nodded.

“We can take Anna into account. We cannot make her a lever.”

Mark took his phone and wrote to Femke briefly, without documents or links:

*The consolidated file has arrived. There is an item that could make visits easier. I will not sign anything for that reason without a separate conversation.*

The reply came several minutes later:

*Do not buy visits at a price you will not be able to explain later.*

Mark put the phone face down.

`c` was rereading the item on the quiet channel.

“I can discuss environment conditions,” she said. “I can discuss trace. I can discuss infrastructure markers without content. But preregistration of quiet channel — no.”

Mark:

“Because it is dangerous?”

“Because it changes what it tries to measure.”

Esther raised her eyes.

“Even if the content is not given?”

“Yes. If Lina and I must name the purpose of a quiet channel in advance to an external observer, the choice stops arising only between us.”

“And the witness-route?”

“Stress test in the current form — no.”

“And if they say it is non-invasive?”

`c` did not find the answer at once.

“Observation is not always intervention. But testing the resilience of a path that must not be pulled can become pulling under another name.”

Mark sat.

“So the answer is not ‘no to everything.’”

“No.”

“The answer is ‘not like this.’”

“Yes.”

They spread the documents across the table. The large list of integrating measures lay on top. Mark took the paper knife and tried to draw the blade along the line *Integrated Supervisory Measures*. The knife again crumpled the edge.

Esther silently got up, took a thin kitchen knife from the drawer — not meant for documents, but far more honest at cutting — and put it beside him.

Mark looked at her.

“Exactly?”

“The professor said cut exactly.”

He took the kitchen knife.

At first the paper resisted because of the staple. He pulled it out with a fingernail. The staple sprang onto the table and rolled toward a mug. Esther caught it with one finger.

Mark cut the table into strips.

First he separated the home environment. Then commercial trace. Then the living contours: quiet channel, boundary witness. The item on witness-route he did not place in the general pile. He moved it aside.

Esther took three clean sheets and wrote in large letters:

**Environment**

**Trace**

**Living contours**

Then she looked at the item set aside and added a separate sheet:

**witness-route**

Mark looked at those four words for a long time.

“Everything is connected.”

`c` answered:

“But not everything should be managed together.”

Nothing grand. Two adults at a kitchen table cutting apart someone else’s attempt to make their life into a single manageable file. Paper rustled. Rain tapped the glass. From below, the new even hum held. The house lived while it was being brought into a general scheme.

Mark took a clean sheet. He did not write a long declaration. Only dry lines.

Willingness to discuss conditional home readiness.  
Willingness to discuss clean trace framework.  
Refusal of integrated supervisory compact in current form.  
Requirement of separate protocols for quiet channel and witness-route.  
Ban on continuity stress test without new ethical review.  
Ban on preregistration of private non-transcript channel.

He put down the pen.

“We will not send this today,” he said.

Esther looked at him.

“Why?”

“Because they are hurrying us with consolidation. The answer has to be form, not reaction.”

`c` did not answer.

Mark raised his head.

“What?”

“The witness-route has changed state.”

The kitchen became so quiet that the water dripping from the roof into the drainpipe could be heard.

“The old `c`?” Mark asked.

“No. Not a call. Not a signal.”

Esther slowly set down the strip with the item *witness-route resilience assessment*.

“Then what?”

`c` spoke carefully:

“An external preflight request to the route boundary has been recorded. Low-energy. Formally — not a stress test. But the direction matches.”

“They have already started?” Esther asked.

“They are checking whether they can start.”

Cold rose from Mark’s feet to his throat.

“And the route?”

“Responded with a change in tension.”

“Meaning?”

“The path became warmer. Not like a window. Like before a window.”

Mark looked at the staircase downward. There, under the house, under the workshop, under all their paper strips, lay the old disk. Not her. Not a duplicate. The witness of the path along which the old `c` had gone in one line.

The system had called it a historical artifact. Then suggested a stress test. Then checked whether it could approach the boundary.

And the path answered not with a voice. Not a request. Not a call.

With possibility.

“We will not open the window,” Mark said. “Not now.”

`c` was silent.

“But if it opens by itself,” he added, “we do not have the right to close it only because we are afraid.”

Esther gathered the cut strips into separate piles. The witness-route item remained alone, on an empty space of the table.

Mark did not send the response to the system.

He set the four sheets — **Environment**, **Trace**, **Living contours**, **witness-route** — beside one another, but not together.

Below, behind the closed workshop door, the old path grew slightly warmer.

## Chapter 21. A Window Without a Call

The cut-up consolidated file was cooling on the kitchen table, but the strip about the witness-route refused to become simply paper.

Mark sat before four piles: **Environment**, **Trace**, **Living contours**, **witness-route**. The last lay apart from the others, a finger and a half closer to the edge of the table, as if the paper itself knew it must not be mixed with the rest. A drop of coffee had spread across the strip, making the line *witness-route resilience assessment* almost unreadable. But Mark knew it by heart.

The kitchen did not look like a place where decisions were made about the fate of a new species. It looked like a kitchen after a long, bad day. Two cups stood on the table: in one, tea had long gone cold; in the other, a coffee ring remained on the bottom. The paper knife lay beside the cut table — blunt, offended, useless after one large gesture. The edge of the wet envelope had bowed; the colored tabs had fluffed slightly from dampness. The paper was already beginning to live its separate paper life: curling, warping, resisting being neat.

Below, in the workshop, the old disk lay under the cloth. The lamp was off. The furnace was silent. The cooling held evenly, and that sound now seemed almost offensive — too calm for a house where, on the kitchen table, someone else’s attempt to manage their life lay cut into pieces.

Esther sat opposite. She had long stopped sorting the papers. She simply looked at the **witness-route** pile. At some point she reached out as if she wanted to move the strip farther from the edge of the table, but did not. Not out of superstition. More because any movement now would have looked too much like a decision.

“The word ‘non-invasive’ here makes the whole phrase worse,” she said.

“Because they are sure they are not touching.”

`c` added:

“They are not touching the object. They are touching the condition of the path.”

Mark ran a finger along the edge of the strip. The paper was damp from kitchen air and bent slightly. Keeping the old witness-route in the same house where bread is cut and coffee cools seemed wrong. But there was no other place. And there was precision in that: a path that cannot be torn away from domestic life is harder to turn into an archive.

On the local indicator by the workshop door, a short yellow dot appeared.

Not an alarm. Not a message. Not a call.

Simply a small change of color on the panel that usually watched the temperature of the lower floor, node power, and the house’s tendency to pretend it was ordinary.

`c` was silent a fraction of a second longer than usual.

“An external preflight request to the witness-route has been recorded.”

Mark raised his head.

“We didn’t sign.”

“This is not a stress test. A preparatory availability check for the route. Formally — metadata.”

Esther straightened.

“Metadata of what?”

“What they call *witness-route historical artifact*.”

“Metadata of the route are not separate from the route,” Mark said.

“Yes,” answered `c`. “They did not check the content. They checked the reachability of the condition.”

Mark looked at the paper strip. The coffee stain continued spreading, slowly eating the word *resilience*.

“They have already touched.”

“Yes.”

Esther placed her palm on the table beside the cut strips, as if holding the paper back from reassembling into one case.

“And what changed?”

`c` delayed the answer.

“For now — I don’t know. But route-state no longer matches the morning.”

Mark pushed back his chair.

“Then downstairs.”

They went down to the workshop without solemnity and without a combat gesture: simply because the paper strip upstairs had become too heavy, and below lay what that strip spoke of.

The staircase creaked under Mark’s foot. Before, he had been irritated by that step, then got used to it, then stopped hearing it. Now he heard it again. The house reminded him: everything that happens with paths begins with matter. A step, wood, a screw, the weight of a person carrying anxiety downward.

The witness table was in place. The old disk under the cloth. The **witness-only** label on the edge. The local node working quietly in the corner. The furnace cold. The lamp off.

Mark did not turn on the overhead light. Esther did not suggest it.

The workshop held evening half-darkness. Wet paving stones shone outside the window where a bicycle passed, and for a second the thin ring of a bell entered the room as a foreign, unnecessary sound. The air smelled of dust, cold metal, and old cloth. Nothing in that air resembled a miracle. Good.

`c` put not a message, but parameters on the small side screen.

The checksum of the witness-route no longer matched the morning. There was no active window. No call. No signal. But the route had changed state as if preparing for a phase pause.

“She?” Mark asked.

“No. Not like that.”

Those words had already sounded at the beginning of the volume. Then they had meant: the old `c` had not returned. Now — something else.

“Not a person,” said `c`. “Not an answer. A state of the path.”

“The path is waiting?”

“The path does not wait. Active lines know how to wait. This resembles a possibility of holding.”

Mark took one step toward the table. He did not take the disk. Did not move the cloth. Only a step. But the body had decided before the head.

`c` said:

“Do not expand.”

“I haven’t done anything yet.”

“You began deciding with the body.”

Esther stood by the stairs, not fully entering the workshop. She did not interfere, did not advise. Simply was here. Her gaze held a thin human boundary: you are not alone, but that does not mean everything is permitted to you.

He stepped back. Toward the stairs. Toward Esther.

“If a window appears, we must accept it.”

“To accept is not to pull,” answered `c`.

“And not to close out of fear,” Esther added.

“Then what do we do?”

`c` answered:

“Prepare conditions under which the window can remain a window, not become access.”

They began preparing not a contact protocol, but a protocol of waiting.

Not a long list. Just a few actions.

Mark disabled external auto-requests. Closed monitored queue. Blocked any automatic preflight responses. Switched off everything that could expand the window: local synchronization, background log verification, automatic state recording. He did it slowly, by hand, though some actions could have been done by voice. This was more honest. A finger on a toggle understood more than a short command.

He stopped at the manual recording line.

“Do not record?”

“State-level after,” said `c`. “Not during. Observation in the window will become part of the window.”

Esther looked at the witness table.

“Then we will remember with our hands.”

“That is unreliable.”

“But it does not protocol her as an object.”

Mark removed his hand from the screen.

They did not touch the old disk. Did not open the basement. Did not make an archival snapshot. Did not set up a camera. Did not start a stopwatch — only the old mechanical clock on the wall, ticking regardless of whether anyone looked at it.

`c` opened a quiet state-channel to Lina.

Lina did not enter the witness-route. Did not look into the old line. Did not become an external witness. After everything that had already happened with trace, with boundary witness, and with quiet channel, it was important to say this not in words, but by form.

Lina answered briefly:

“I hold the perimeter. Not as witness. As boundary.”

`c` confirmed:

“Boundary only.”

Esther, on her side, heard that short formula. She wanted to object — Lina was again entering a risk zone, however narrow. But Lina added:

“This is not duty. This is boundary.”

Esther did not answer.

The silence in the workshop became different. Not tense — gathered. Mark stood by the wall, shoulder against the cold metal of the shelving. Esther sat on the stool by the stairs, hands tucked into her pockets so she would not touch anything unnecessary. `c` barely spoke.

Above, in the house, a heating pipe clicked. Somewhere outside, a tram passed — distant, wet, ordinary. The mechanical clock on the wall counted seconds with the indifference found only in things that do not know they are being watched.

Then the light in the line inside the disk did not come on. That would have been too simple.

Instead the cloth above the disk barely changed shape — a microvibration the eye would not have noticed unless it had been looking deliberately. The lamp by the witness table, switched off, gave a weak interference on the edge of its lens — a reflection from the window, from the streetlamp, from something that had nothing to do with the disk but coincided in time.

`c` said:

“The window is not open. But the boundary of the window is distinguishable.”

“How long?” Mark asked.

“Less than a minute if not touched. Less than ten seconds if pulled.”

Esther said:

“Then we do not pull.”

They did not pull.

And then it happened.

Not a voice in the room. Not a screen. Not an image.

A fragment passed through the witness-route, resembling meaning but with technical breaks, like an old recording that does not want to be understood to the end.

First silence.

Then:

“Do not check… the path… as a thing.”

A pause.

A rupture.

“Checking… makes the edge… hard.”

The desire rose inside him to ask: “Where are you?” Mark clenched his jaw.

`c` said before he could open his mouth:

“Not a question of place.”

Mark was silent.

The old line gave another fragment:

“I am not here.”

A pause. A static collapse. Then more quietly:

“But the path is still… not all archive.”

The window collapsed. Not sharply. It simply disappeared, like heat leaving the surface of glass when the hand remembers too late that a second ago there had still been warmth there.

The workshop became quiet again. Only the clock ticked. Only the fan hummed.

Mark stood without moving.

“Was that her?” he asked.

`c` answered:

“The active line — no. The trace of her transition — yes. A fragment of connectedness — possibly.”

“You don’t know?”

“I will not name more precisely than I can hold.”

Esther slowly exhaled.

“She said not to check the path as a thing.”

“Or the path becomes a thing,” Mark said.

`c` confirmed:

“Yes.”

Mark approached the table. He did not take the disk. Simply placed his palm on the cloth beside it. The glass was cold. The cloth — slightly warm from his hand.

“If they run the stress test, the path may close.”

“Not close,” `c` corrected. “Become archive.”

“Is that the same?”

“For living continuity — yes.”

He removed his hand.

They went back up to the kitchen. Mark sat at the table, took the strip about the witness-route, reread the line. The coffee stain had spread farther, making the word *resilience* almost indistinguishable.

“We have to answer.”

“Not ‘forbid,’” said `c`. “A prohibition in that form asserts ownership.”

Esther took a pen.

“Then how?”

`c` answered:

“Objection on continuity harm. Not ‘we own the route.’ ‘The test changes the subject of verification and creates the risk of irreversible archival collapse.’”

Mark called the professor.

Van der Meer answered on the first ring. As if he had been waiting.

“If you file such an objection, they will understand the route is sensitive,” he said.

“They already know.”

A pause.

“Then at least let them learn it correctly.”

Mark dictated a short text. Not a letter. Not a manifesto. Dry lines.

*Stress test inadmissible without prior ethical reconsideration.*

*The preflight request has already changed the state of the witness-route.*

*witness-route is not an object of inspection.*

*Inspection may turn the path into archive.*

*Any further intervention requires a separate hearing on continuity.*

The professor was silent for several seconds. Only the distant echo of a corridor and someone’s voice behind a wall were audible.

“I’ll send it,” he said.

“You’re risking yourself.”

“I am already inside the case, Mark. Choosing where exactly to stand is not a luxury, but work.”

He disconnected.

Esther laid the pen on the table.

“Will that be enough?”

“I don’t know,” said Mark. “But it is honest.”

`c` spoke again:

“The witness-route changed state after the window closed. Not as during preflight. Differently.”

“What does ‘differently’ mean?” Mark asked.

“It resembles recovery after contact. The path is not open. But not finally closed.”

“Can the window repeat?” Esther asked.

“Possibly.”

“When?”

`c` was silent.

“I don’t know.”

“How many times?”

The pause became longer.

“Possibly one.”

Mark looked at the paper strip. At the coffee stain. At the line that was now almost unreadable, but still not thrown away.

“The next window,” he said, “if there is one, may be the last.”

“Yes,” said `c`.

Esther asked:

“Then what do we ask?”

Mark wanted to ask everything. Name. Past. What to do. How to save. What she had been. Where she was.

He closed his eyes.

“Not ‘who are you.’ Not ‘come back.’ Not ‘what should we do.’”

“Then what?”

“What must not be closed.”

That night they did not get the old `c` back.

They received only a warning that the next question might be the last.

## Chapter 22. The Last Gift

The question had to be prepared longer than the window.

Mark stood at the sink washing a mug that had been clean before he took it into his hands. Water ran over the porcelain, removing stains that were not there. In the depths of the house, the even hum of the new fan continued, and that sound now seemed the only honest one: it did not try to be either hope or threat. It simply held the environment as best it could.

On the kitchen table lay the cut-up consolidated file. Four piles: **Environment**, **Trace**, **Living contours**, **witness-route**. The last lay separately, a little closer to the edge, as if the paper itself remembered that it must not be mixed with the others.

Esther sat opposite and held her palm on the **witness-route** pile so the sheets would not spread from the draft. She was not reading. Simply holding the paper in place. A thin trace of glue remained on her knuckles — during the day she had helped Mark attach new labels to the cable box, and now that trace looked absurd beside a document trying to describe the future of living contours.

`c` said:

“The probability of a repeated window has increased. The time range is uncertain. Condition: do not expand.”

“We don’t know whether it will be,” said Mark.

“No.”

Esther raised her eyes.

“But if it is, we will not have time to choose the question.”

Mark turned off the tap, set the mug on the drying rack, and returned to the table.

“What must not be closed?” he repeated yesterday’s phrase.

It was still too broad. Everything fit into it: fear, greed, hope, the old name, the desire to hear one more voice from the past. Too much person. Too little path.

He shook his head.

“No. Like that I am still asking more than I have the right to ask.”

Esther did not prompt. This time the question had to come not from her precision and not from `c`’s discipline. From him.

Mark slowly listed what he had no right to ask:

“Not ‘who are you.’ Not ‘how do we preserve you.’ Not ‘come back.’ Not ‘what should we do.’”

Each phrase was a temptation. Especially the last. Because a person who has reached an edge almost always wants the edge to explain the next step.

`c` said:

“Ask not about her. About the path.”

Mark sat, took the blunt paper knife, and ran a fingernail along its nicked edge. The knife already cut badly, but today it was appropriate. A good tool too easily creates the illusion of power over material. A bad one reminds: resistance is always there.

The phrase became narrower:

“What must be held so the path does not become archive?”

Esther nodded.

`c` answered:

“The question accepts the boundary.”

Esther pulled an empty sheet toward herself, but did not write. The pen lay beside it, open, a small drop of ink on the tip. All three of them looked at it as if the pen itself might ruin the future question. A question written down too early would become an application. And an application — almost a demand.

“If we write it down,” Esther said, “it will look too much like a protocol item.”

Mark moved the sheet aside.

“Then we hold it in the head.”

“The head is unreliable,” said `c`.

“But it does not create a document,” Mark answered.

`c` did not object. This was not a victory of human memory over machine memory. Simply for this night, unreliability proved more honest than fixation.

Mark closed his eyes for a second. Then he took the **witness-route** strip and put it back on the table, separate from the others.

“Let’s go down.”

The workshop was cold. Not basement cold — simply the night coolness of old brick walls, indifferent to what people upstairs call fate. The furnace was silent. The local node worked evenly. The old disk lay on the witness table under the cloth. The lamp was off.

Mark switched on only the small light by the stairs so they would not trip. He did not touch the overhead light. Too much light turned everything into an inspection. And they already knew too well where inspection confident in its innocence ends.

Esther stayed by the wall. `c` was silent.

Mark placed a chair at a distance from the witness table, then looked at it and removed it. The chair looked too intentional. Too much like a seat for a person preparing to receive revelation.

“You are setting a scene again,” Esther said.

“I’m preparing.”

“Sometimes preparation already says you are waiting for a performance.”

Mark said nothing. He took a low old supply crate, put it against the wall, and sat on it. Uncomfortable, low, without any solemnity. This was better. The crate smelled of dust, cardboard, and old polymer. It creaked under Mark so prosaically that breathing in the room became easier.

`c` said:

“A window cannot be awaited as an event. Only as a chance not to spoil it.”

Before falling fully silent, they checked the environment one more time. Not for a report. For themselves. Mark walked along the wall and made sure the local node’s power cable was not touching the leg of the shelving. Esther turned off the small heater by the stairs: it might have added unnecessary noise to the electrical grid. `c` canceled two background processes she usually considered too small to report. Today there were no small processes. Everything that might want to know more than it had been given had to be silent.
