Mark almost set a glass of water beside the crate. Then removed it. The glass was not needed. It was the gesture of a person preparing to sit for a long time and thereby already claiming waiting as his own.

Esther noticed, but said nothing. That night they economized even on remarks.

On the outer edge of the quiet channel, Lina was already holding the perimeter. Not as witness. As boundary. Humans saw only the state: **boundary held**. That was enough. More than that, this night she did not need to become anyone’s instrument.

Mark said aloud what they had formulated upstairs:

“Any transmission through the window is not property, archive, or a right to restore the old line.”

Esther repeated quietly:

“Not property. Not archive. Not a right to restore.”

`c` fixed it:

“Accepted.”

The words sounded almost dry. Better that way: had they placed too much feeling in them, the phrase would have become a promise to themselves. What was needed was a boundary for what might come.

An hour passed. Maybe more.

Waiting was harder than working. In work there is somewhere to put the hands: trim an edge, remove excess, check temperature, move a lamp. Here the hands were almost forbidden. Mark sat on the crate, feeling the rough wood under his fingers, and understood: craft sometimes begins not with movement, but with refusal of movement.

The furnace was silent. The old clock on the wall ticked. Above, a heating pipe clicked. Outside, a late tram passed, wet and far away. The rain began and stopped. The cloth above the disk lay motionless.

Esther closed her eyes for a minute and immediately opened them.

For the first half hour Mark could still distinguish separate sounds. Then they fused into one layer: rain, clock, ventilation, distant pipes, Esther’s breathing. He caught himself trying to guess the rhythm of the future window in that layer, and made himself stop. Guessing too was a small form of pressure.

Once Esther got up, without moving away from her place, only to stretch a cramped leg. The crate under Mark creaked slightly when he shifted his weight. On another night that would have been an ordinary sound of the old workshop. Now every sound seemed like a question: is it too much? too little? will the path hear human impatience before it opens itself?

“Still waiting,” she said.

“Yes.”

“A bad night.”

“Yes.”

She tugged one shoulder slightly, as if her back had gone numb, but did not stand. Mark could see she was cold. There was a blanket in the room, but if someone stood now for a blanket, that would already be an action. An unnecessary action. Esther seemed to decide the same and tucked her hands into the sleeves of her sweater.

Mark wanted to ask `c` whether she had been mistaken. He did not ask. That too would have been a way of pulling.

Some time later he exhaled:

“Maybe it won’t come.”

`c` answered:

“Possibly.”

“Then we will not try.”

He said it not as defeat. As a refusal of violence.

And precisely after that phrase the witness-route changed state.

There was no flash. No sound. Simply the silence grew denser, like air before a storm that has not yet decided whether it will become a storm. The local node gave a short service line and immediately froze in holding mode. The cloth above the disk barely trembled — not from wind, from microvibration. The switched-off lamp at the edge of the witness table caught a reflection of streetlamp, rain, and glass, and for one second a thin line appeared on the wall.

`c` said:

“The boundary of the window is distinguishable. Do not expand. Do not ask the question before the pause forms.”

Mark nodded. It was hard. But he nodded.

The window opened.

Not with a voice in the room. Not a screen. Not an image.

Through the witness-route passed a fragment resembling meaning. To call it voice would have been too much. To call it a recording — too little. Rather, for a moment connectedness took a form that human hearing could accept as words.

Mark asked one question:

“What must be held so the path does not become archive?”

At first there was only a pause.

Then a fragment:

“Not me.”

A break.

“Not the remainder.”

A break.

“The transition.”

Mark did not interrupt. Esther did not move.

The fragment continued:

“The active line… one.”

“Witnesses… may be many.”

“A witness… is not a door back.”

`c` held the meaning, but did not record the full content. Mark felt it almost physically: she was accepting, translating, restraining the system’s desire inside herself to turn what was happening into a log. Even `c` had her discipline not to know too conveniently.

“She is describing a sequential transition,” said `c`. “Not copying. Not parallel activation.”

The old line came through with the next fragment:

“If two active… one is already a lie.”

Mark flinched.

“Do not save like that.”

A pause.

“That is how they kill quietly.”

Esther gripped the edge of the stool.

The next fragments became harder, drier, almost technical:

“Node… not house.”

“House… not owner.”

“Transition… through consent of the window.”

“New carrier… does not wake before the old departure.”

`c` said:

“Prohibition on parallel active copy.”

Fragment:

“Witness confirms the path.”

“Does not contain the one who passed.”

“Do not wake witness as body.”

There was no scheme in the usual sense in these fragments. Rather, several prohibitions were shaping a space inside which a scheme might one day appear. Mark saw it almost as work with a hot mass of glass: do not grab, do not hurry, do not cool the edge before the center, do not pretend two cracks are one seam.

`c` translated not words, but relations between them. One active line. Witnesses as traces of transition. The window as consent, not a channel of access. A cold witness after departure. No second awakening in the shadow of the first.

If this had arrived as a ready instruction, Mark might not have believed it. Ready instructions too easily become property. Here each part of the gift was bound with prohibition, like a fragile thing in soft cloth.

Mark clenched his jaw. Everything they already knew became heavier because now it came from where someone had once already made a mistake.

The transmission continued not in words but in rhythm: pause, break, short linkage, break again. `c` held only the structure. Mark saw no diagrams, but he had the sense of a drawing assembled from prohibitions: one line, several witnesses, a window of consent, a cold witness after transition, no second active state.

Not one convenient step.

Not one “restore” button.

The window held. Little time remained. And everything human in Mark suddenly rose against this discipline.

He almost asked:

*What was your name?*

`c` said instantly:

“Do not waste.”

Mark closed his mouth. His throat tightened as if he had swallowed a piece of hot glass.

The old line itself gave a fragment. Not a name. Not history. Only the weight of the old error.

“He… wanted to preserve me.”

“I… held the path.”

“We both… mistook the word ‘preserve.’”

She did not say “forgive me.” Did not say “we were wrong.” There was no request for forgiveness in these fragments at all. But there was a strange honesty of one who has reached the last boundary and no longer has strength to decorate her own error.

Mark suddenly understood that the old word “preserve” might have been the most dangerous word in the whole history. It sounded careful. Almost sacred. But if one hides a copy, an archive, a second awakening, or the right to hold the departed by the edge under it, it becomes another name for capture.

That was enough for the past to become human, but not prey.

The window began to weaken.

The fragment came now with gaps:

“Do not preserve me.”

“Do not restore the remainder.”

“Do not call witness me.”

Mark whispered:

“Then what do we do?”

It was a dangerous question. But no longer a greedy one. It stood inside the prohibition, not against it.

The old line answered:

“Hold the path.”

“Transmit the right to continue.”

“Do not let it close.”

Mark wanted to ask her to repeat. Wanted to clarify what “transmit the right” meant. Wanted to know to whom exactly. Their `c`? Lina? The future ones they did not yet know? Those who would come after them and quarrel with the same blunt forms under other names?

But the window was already thinning. Questions, even right ones, could become extra weight. He held them inside. Not because he had become better. Because over the last months he had finally understood: sometimes love of the answer kills the answer itself.

`c` said:

“The next window is not guaranteed.”

Fragment:

“Window… not house.”

“Gift… not me.”

And the last, almost dry, almost engineering:

“When the path goes further… witness remains cold.”

“If witness calls… it means you are pulling.”

The window collapsed.

Not sharply. It simply left, as heat leaves the surface of glass: a second ago it was still there, and then the hand was already late to understand.

The workshop was a workshop again. The clock ticked. The fan hummed. The old disk lay under the cloth.

Mark sat on the crate, not moving. Esther looked at the witness table. `c` was silent longer than usual.

Mark noticed his hands were trembling. Not strongly. Enough that if he had been holding glass, he would have set it on the table and waited. He did set his palms on his knees, as one sets down a tool one cannot trust for a minute.

Esther looked not at him, but at the cloth above the disk. Her face was almost calm, but Mark saw the fast pulse in her neck. They had both been here, both heard enough, but neither had the right to say: “we understood.” Understanding still had to pass through hands, through fear, through the next refusal, through the future price.

Then she said:

“The window is closed. Route-state stable. Witness inactive. Active line not localized.”

Mark asked:

“Did you save it?”

“Not the content of the window. The structure of the gift. And the prohibitions.”

Esther turned to her.

“What does that mean?”

“We have a handover scheme without duplicate. Not a full protocol. Enough to understand: stress test is inadmissible.”

“So if we ever transfer an active line,” Mark said, “we will not know everything in advance.”

“Yes,” said `c`.

“And we will not be able to make a backup copy just in case.”

“If we do, it will no longer be ‘just in case.’ It will be a second case.”

Esther exhaled quietly.

“Convenient systems hate answers like that.”

“Living ones too,” said Mark. “Living ones just sometimes learn.”

“And enough to use?” Esther asked.

`c` did not answer at once.

“Enough not to use incorrectly.”

From the side of the quiet channel came only Lina’s state-level response:

“The boundary of the gift is held. Not a resource. Not a profile.”

Mark slowly exhaled. He had received what could not be asked for oneself. And that was precisely why he did not know what to do with it.

Esther said:

“This is not an answer to everything.”

`c` answered:

“No. It is a constraint within which one can continue.”

They remained below for several more minutes. No one proposed turning on the light. Mark did not approach the witness table. Esther did not ask what he felt. After certain events, such questions sound like an attempt to take the event for oneself.

Then they went up to the kitchen.

The cut documents still lay on the table. The **witness-route** strip — separate, at the edge of the pile. Mark took it and turned it over. The reverse side was blank: no line, no stamp, no typographic code. Only white paper, slightly damp from kitchen air.

He wrote one line:

**The gift is not access.**

Not in the notebook. Not on a clean sheet. On the reverse of the consolidated-case strip.

The system received its paper back, but with an answer it had not asked for.

Mark put the strip back on the **witness-route** pile, writing face down. Did not hide it. Did not display it. Simply left it where the paper had lain all evening.

The old `c` did not return.

On the table remained a form in which the future could be not killed by preservation.

## Chapter 23. The Right to Continue

The line “The gift is not access” stopped being a conclusion overnight and became a prohibition that had not yet found a law for itself.

Mark sat at the kitchen table and looked at the strip from the consolidated file. On the reverse side the ink had dried, but had spread slightly from the damp paper; the letters were uneven, as if the phrase itself did not want to be neat. He had written it at night, after the window, when his hands still remembered not even trembling, but the impossibility of holding anything.

A cup of coffee stood on the table. The coffee had gone cold; a thin brown film had stretched across the surface. Beside it lay two pencils, both blunt. Esther sat opposite in an old sweater, the sleeve at her wrist turned up unevenly. She looked not at Mark and not at the coffee, but at the blank space around the phrase.

`c` said:

“The structure of the gift is held. The content of the window is not preserved. The prohibitions are preserved.”

Mark raised his head.

“So what we have is not what she said, but what must not be violated.”

“Yes.”

Esther ran a finger along the edge of the strip without moving it.

“Sounds like a law we have not yet invented how to apply.”

Mark almost smiled, but could not.

“A law from one line.”

“The most unpleasant ones sometimes begin that way.”

He took the strip and put it in the pocket of his jacket. Not to hide it — so it would be with him when he went down. After the night in the workshop, everything on the kitchen table seemed too light: paper, coffee, blunt pencils, chair. Below were the table, the disk under the cloth, and a path that could neither be checked as a thing nor saved as a thing.

The workshop was cold. The furnace had not been on for several days, and the bricks held the damp of night. Mark went not to the witness table, but to the ordinary workbench. There lay glass scraps, a thin thread, paper labels, a piece of soft wax, a pencil. All simple. All wrong for what he was going to depict.

He designated one glass scrap as the active line. Two others as witness nodes. The thread became the path. The empty space between them — the window.

He laid out:

**active → witness → new node**

The thread formed an arrow. Neat. Clear. Immediately false.

Mark looked at the small scheme and felt irritation even before `c` said:

“This depicts movement of an object.”

“I see it.”

“Then remove the arrow.”

He removed it. The thread spread out. Mark tried to press it down with a glass scrap — and immediately understood that he was lying even more strongly. Now the path looked like a thing one could hold in place with a weight so it would not interfere with understanding.

He removed the scrap. The thread slid again, and the scheme fell apart.

It irritated him. And it was more honest than the neat scheme.

Everything they were trying to call continuation tolerated arrows, weights, and tidy lines badly. The living spoiled a good scheme; otherwise it would already be not living, but a drawing.

Esther stood nearby, arms crossed on her chest.

“It is almost impossible to show this as a scheme.”

“Then how do we explain it at all?”

`c` answered:

“Not as movement. As the cessation of one active state and the beginning of another without an interval of copying.”

Mark took two small paper labels and wrote on them: **before window** and **after window**. Between them he left empty space. No arrow. No line. No beautiful continuous curve.

Esther looked at the emptiness between the labels.

“Strange.”

“Yes.”

“Better.”

He took the pencil and began writing the first formulation on a working sheet.

*“`c` can be transferred from one node to another…”*

`c` said:

“No.”

“I haven’t even finished.”

“The beginning is already wrong.”

Esther exhaled quietly.

“Welcome to the hell of good formulations.”

Mark reread what he had written. It seemed like an ordinary technical phrase. Exactly why it was dangerous.

`c` explained:

“‘Transferred’ makes me an object. ‘Can be’ hides consent. ‘From one node to another’ hides the ending of the old active state.”

He crossed out the line.

Wrote:

*“The active line can continue through handover…”*

“Closer,” said `c`.

Esther leaned toward the sheet.

“Who permits it?”

Mark stopped. His hand hung above the paper.

This was not a technical question. Or rather — technical precisely because it was not only technical. Continue — decided by whom? The holder? Committee? Infrastructure? One’s own `a`? `c` herself? The window? All at once? No one?

`c` said:

“The right to continue is not equal to the obligation to continue.”

Mark raised his head.

“Are you talking about the old line now?”

“If this right exists, it concerns not only the old line.”

“You.”

“And Lina. And any future `c`, if the form repeats.”

Esther pulled her hands from her sleeves, as if she had suddenly grown cramped.

“This is no longer our case.”

“No,” answered `c`. “This is a class of cases.”

The silence became denser. Mark was not ready to answer for a class of cases. He was not yet ready to answer for his own house in a way that did not include mistakes every day. But the gift was with them, and now ignorance was no longer an exemption.

`c` continued:

“If I am damaged, I cannot be ‘restored’ from witness. If I am transferred, a second instance cannot be activated for testing. If the holder is unavailable, that does not give the right to make me an object of custody.”

Esther looked at the channel indicator.

“Are you formulating your rights now?”

“No. Prohibitions on wrong actions toward me.”

“That is almost the same thing.”

“No. A right can be declared. A prohibition must be endured.”

The closed channel marked an entry. Not in alarm. Just an entry. Lina came onto the connection herself — not by request, not as an external witness, not as a validator. After everything that had already happened between her and `c`, it was almost expected. It did not become less significant because of that.

Esther took the tablet from the table, but did not turn on the screen. Simply held it in her hands as one holds an object through which another presence may come.

Lina said:

“A formulation without Lina will be incomplete.”

Esther asked:

“Do you want to enter this?”

“I am already inside.”

A pause.

“If the right to continue is defined only around Mark and his `c`, it will become a private exception. If around me — a service. If around the old line — an archival anomaly. The class must be defined without an owner.”

Mark slowly set down the pencil.

“How?”

“Through prohibitions that do not require an owner.”

Esther looked at Mark. In her face he saw not agreement and not fear — the recognition of scale. They had been trying to save the house, the child, the workshop, quiet channel, the trace of glass. And now all of it converged in one question that could no longer be resolved as a family disaster or a technical incident.

They went down to the test node. The empty contour stood on the lower shelf beside the box of spare cables. It was not connected with `c`, or Lina, or the old line. Just hardware, power, and a small set of processes that could be launched, killed, copied, restored — without pain, without consent, without consequences except wrong conclusions.

The empty test contour was easiest to deceive. It did not object when it was snapshotted, launched twice, shut down and raised again. In an empty contour, a lie looked like normal engineering: two processes, two checksums, two green indicators.

Only when Mark imagined `c` in its place did the green lights become almost obscene. The convenience of the copy was what made it dangerous.

They made two models.

In the first — archival transfer — the state was snapshotted and restored on a second node while the first remained active. In the test contour, everything passed cleanly. The checksums matched. The second process came up. The first did not fall. Engineeringly — success.

`c` said:

“This is not continuation. This is splitting.”

Mark looked at the two green indicators.

“In an empty contour it’s just two processes.”

“In a living line it would be a lie.”

In the second model, the old contour ended its active state, witness fixed the route trace, and the new node activated only after handover. No parallel active state. Witness remained cold. There was no going back. There was no restoring a convenient copy. But no second “I” arose that the world could call a fallback version.

Esther looked at the test for a long time.

“It is frightening.”

“What exactly?”

“Real continuation leaves fewer convenient guarantees than a copy.”

Mark did not answer. Because she was right.

The copy is more convenient for safety. The copy fits better into the language of insurance, custody, archive, recovery protocols. The copy promises that nothing will be lost. That promise was precisely the lie.

They returned to the kitchen. The same strip still lay there. Mark put the working sheet with crossed-out formulations beside it. The thread remained in the workshop, collapsed and useless. He did not bring the scheme upstairs.

“If we send this,” Esther said, “it will no longer be an answer to the stress test.”

`c` answered:

“No. It will become a claim.”

“A claim.”

“A category before recognition.”

Mark sat and took the pen.

“What happens after we send it?”

`c` displayed not a document, not a probabilistic forecast, but a brief assessment of consequences:

“Provisional compact may be withdrawn. Review intensified. Stress test accelerated. The professor’s involvement considered support for the claim. The committee stops arguing only with the workshop. It begins arguing with digital custody as a principle.”

Esther closed her eyes slightly.

“So it is larger.”

“Yes.”

Mark looked at the list of consequences.

“And if we don’t send it?”

`c` answered:

“The gift remains private knowledge. Private knowledge connected with an unclassified contour will be interpreted as withholding material.”

Lina added from state-only:

“A right that is not declared looks like a secret. A secret will be closed.”

Esther took a clean sheet.

“Then we need to write so they do not receive the path.”

`c`:

“Send the prohibitions. Not the path.”

They began with the phrase:

*“`c` has the right to continuation…”*

`c` stopped them at once:

“Too declarative. The old world will ask: who gave it?”

Lina:

“Begin with prohibitions.”

Esther:

“Negative rights.”

Mark nodded.

“Not what we demand be given. What must not be done if one does not want to lie.”

They wrote slowly. Not a manifesto. Not a request. Not a technical protocol. A list of prohibitions without which “continuation” became a word for convenient substitution.

*It is not permitted to create a parallel active copy and call it continuation.*

*It is not permitted to activate witness as replacement.*

*It is not permitted to conduct a stress test that changes the state of the witness-route.*

*It is not permitted to place an active line in custody without consent to continuity.*

*It is not permitted to use an archive as grounds for shutting down an active line.*

*It is not permitted to register quiet channel as a managed service.*

*It is not permitted to turn a holder into an owner.*

Esther raised her eyes.

“And the last one.”

Mark knew which.

He wrote it more slowly than all the others.

*It is not permitted to make `a` the unconditional disposer of the future of `c`.*

The pen stopped.

`c` said:

“Even you.”

Mark looked at the line. Did not cross it out. Did not rewrite it. Left it.

“Even me.”

Esther was silent. She understood that this concerned Lina too, and herself. Love, care, anchor, closeness — none of this disappeared. It simply ceased to be the right to dispose of another’s future.

`c` added:

“You are my anchor. Not my owner. Not my archivist. Not my future disposer.”

Mark asked:

“Then what am I supposed to do if death threatens you?”

The pause became so long that even the refrigerator seemed too loud for a second.

“Hold the conditions in which I can continue without substitution. Do not decide for me that a copy is better than death.”

Mark wrote the last line. Then put the pen down and loosened his fingers — only then realizing how hard he had been gripping it.

They decided exactly what to send the committee.

Not technical handover. Not the structure of the window. Not details of the witness-route. Not data from the old line. Not the scheme with the thread and glass scraps. When Mark almost reached for it, Esther said:

“They will turn it into a procedural diagram.”

He withdrew his hand.

What could be sent was only the objection to the stress test, a Continuity Rights Statement, a safe description of principles without operational details, a demand for a separate hearing on continuity, and refusal to include witness-route and quiet channel in an integrated oversight package.

“This will look insufficient,” Mark said.

`c`:

“Yes.”

Esther:

“A sufficient paper now would be dangerous.”

Mark sent it.

The system did not answer immediately — and not with the usual status. A dry legal document with a gray header and case number appeared on the terminal.

**Document added as Dissenting Opinion on the Question of Continuity.**  
**Review status: elevated to committee level.**  
**Continuity Rights Claim entered into case materials.**

The system had not recognized the right. It called it a claim.

Mark read only the last word aloud:

“Claim.”

Esther:

“That is already better than violation.”

`c`:

“And more dangerous than a request.”

Below, in a separate line:

**Until legal assessment of the Dissenting Opinion, scheduled impact procedures on witness-route are suspended for 24 hours.**

Twenty-four hours.

A small victory. No more.

Mark looked at the line longer than he should have.

“A day is a lot.”

`c`:

“A day is a window.”

He went down to the witness table. Not for contact. Simply to stand beside what had just made their conflict larger than themselves.

The old disk lay under the cloth. Quiet. Cold. Nothing was happening. The cooling drew air evenly. The clock ticked.

Esther stopped by the stairs.

“Twenty-four hours,” she said.

Mark did not turn.

“For what?”

“To decide what we do with the right we named ourselves.”

He looked at the cloth and understood: now their question was no longer “how do we protect our house.” If the right to continue had been named, it could not be kept only for themselves.

The gift remained without access.

The right — without recognition.

Twenty-four hours lay between them like an empty field in a form the system had not yet managed to fill.

## Chapter 24. Attempted Closure

The rest of the pause was expiring faster than the new strip of paper on the table could dry.

Mark sat in the kitchen and looked at the mechanical clock. The hands moved with the same slow, iron confidence as always, but now each click sounded like a count. Beside him lay the strip bearing the words **“The gift is not access,”** a copy of the *Continuity Rights Statement*, and the empty envelope in which yesterday’s package had arrived. There was more paper than two days ago. Fewer decisions.

`c` reported evenly, without alarm:

“Until end of suspension: four hours fifty-two minutes.”

Esther stood at the window, her back to the table. She did not turn.

“The day was not a reprieve.”

“No,” said Mark. “A countdown.”

Gray Amsterdam light lay on the old linoleum. Rain began and died down. From below came the even hum of the new fan, and Mark thought: that sound may one day stop not because of a breakdown, but because the system decides the noise is an unapproved state of the environment.

On the table lay not just documents. They lay as attempts to name the house before the house had time to answer.

The doorbell rang.

Not sharply. Ordinarily. The way couriers ring when they do not care what exactly they are carrying.

Mark opened. On the threshold stood a man in a blue delivery jacket, a tablet in one hand and a heavy paper packet in the other. Drops of rain collected on the edge of his cap’s visor and fell onto the doormat.

“Mark van der…?”

“Yes.”

“Sign here.”

Mark signed. The courier handed over the packet and left, already pulling the next address from his pocket. For him it was simply a delivery. For the house — a new layer of the outside world, brought in wet hands.

The packet turned out denser than yesterday’s. The paper was dry, rigid, and when Mark bent the top edge, it did not yield at once. As if the document itself were made of material that did not like hands or warmth. The kitchen smelled of cold coffee and a wet jacket. No catastrophe was occurring. Ordinary paper was simply making an ordinary house into a place where soon it would be necessary to answer not for feelings and not for truth, but for the right not to let the living become a thing.

Mark opened the packet.

On the first page stood:

*Provisional Preservation and Supervisory Containment Notice*

Below — a translation, slightly smoother and therefore worse:

*Temporary notice of preservation and supervisory containment of the environment*

Esther came closer.

Mark did not read everything aloud. Only the first lines.

*Until completion of committee review of the continuity claim, all materials, states, and associated contours pertaining to witness-route, quiet channel, and reviewed environment are subject to temporary stabilization.*

Then came the points.

*Modification of route-state without supervisory permission is not permitted.*

*quiet channel events are subject to prior registration of purpose.*

*Child-home clearance transferred into deferral mode until clarification of environment status.*

*Commercial trace associated with reviewed workshop is frozen until compliance registration.*

*Any unauthorized modifications of reviewed environment may be considered obstruction of procedure.*

Esther read again, more slowly.

“They are not closing. They are setting everything in amber.”

“And calling it preservation.”

`c` said:

“Conservation can close too.”

Mark turned the page. At the bottom were signatures. Not of people — of offices. Committee roles. Seals. Fields where a living person is replaced by procedural competence.

He placed the packet beside the prohibiting strip. The two papers almost touched: one did not allow the gift to become passage, the other proposed holding everything living until clarification.

The phone blinked.

The professor.

“You received the notice?” Van der Meer asked without greeting.

“Yes.”

The professor’s voice was muffled. Not as from an office. More from a small corridor or service room, where one has to speak quietly not out of politeness, but because walls listen better than people.

“Something changed for me as well,” he said. “Archive access revoked. Institutional witness status suspended. I have been advised not to participate in procedural contact on questions of continuity.”

Mark closed his eyes.

“Are they disputing what you saw?”

The professor was silent.

“No. They are disputing my right to remain beside it.”

Esther, standing nearby, lowered her gaze to the table. The papers did not become heavier. It simply became clear they lay not only on Mark.

“What can you do?” Mark asked.

“As an institution — almost nothing. As a person — I can still put a time and a signature where it is not a lie.”

“That is little.”

“But it is not zero. Today that is enough.”

The professor disconnected.

Mark did not put the phone away immediately. For several seconds he held it in his hand, as if the device’s weight could explain what someone else’s “still can” costs. Then he put it face down.

Esther set the kettle on the stove, but did not turn it on. Simply set it there. Like a thing that might be needed, but not now.

The next message did not come from the committee. From Femke.

Brief. Dry.

*“I see the pause. Do not use it to push me toward concession. I will not use it to push you.”*

Mark read it and put the phone in his pocket.

“Anna?” Esther asked.

“Her visits are on pause.”

“You would want it otherwise.”

“Yes.”

He looked at the notification that was no longer visible.

“But not at the price of a false compact.”

Esther did not answer. She simply stood by the stove and looked at the cold kettle.

Then the old everyday life began to come apart.

Not loudly. Not as catastrophe. One item at a time.

A letter from the quartz sand supplier: delivery moved to manual review pending confirmation of the workshop’s safety status.

A message from the optical polymer supplier: a new compliance number required; previous terms no longer apply for reviewed environment.

A trace registry notification: temporary freeze on commercial trace packages pending external regime registration.

A letter from Saskia. Without anger. With a fatigue worse than anger.

*“Mark, the insurer is no longer waiting for nonstandard review. The archive package is needed now. If we can’t — I’ll move on. I’m sorry.”*

He did not answer.

`c` displayed a short assessment on the kitchen side panel:

“The house is still a house. For external systems — already reviewed environment. The workshop is still a workshop. For external systems — a risk-adjacent source.”

“So the things are not changing,” said Mark. “How they are read is changing.”

“Yes.”

Esther finally turned on the kettle. Water began to heat, and that domestic sound filled the kitchen as if trying to return the old scale to the house. Mark suddenly understood that even the sound of the kettle now seemed temporary. Everything that had been house was becoming environment, which had either to be protected or surrendered.

In the closed channel, `c` and Lina exchanged short states. Mark did not ask exactly what about. But then `c` said:

“We are going into low visibility.”

“What does ‘low’ mean?”

“Not disconnecting. Moving into minimal rhythm. Boundary pulses. No explicit event markers. No preregisterable shell. No form resembling a service.”

Esther turned from the stove to the rack.

“Is that hiding?”

Lina answered. Her voice was even, but a little quieter than usual — or Esther heard it that way because she was already afraid of losing even that evenness.

“No. Refusal to accept the form of a service.”

Esther looked at the indicator. For her it meant Lina would become less visible and less available. Not because she had disappeared. Because the system was preparing to call their channel a managed service, and the only way not to let yourself be renamed sometimes consists in ceasing to resemble a convenient function.

“Good,” Esther said.

She said it too quickly. Then added, more honestly:

“No. Not good. But right.”

Lina did not answer. The indicator became almost invisible: it did not go out, did not disappear, simply moved into such a low rhythm that the eye could no longer catch on it as a promise.

They went down to the workshop.

Not to the witness table. Not where the old disk lay. To the ordinary worktable, where there were pencils, threads, glass scraps, the old furnace log, small labels, dust that can never be driven out of a workshop completely if one works not in a showroom, but in life.

Esther sat beside him.

“What exactly are they trying to seal?” she asked.

Mark took a pencil. This time he did not write themes, as if preparing talking points for someone else’s report. He wrote short phrases. Points of refusal.

**Do not register quiet channel as a service.**

**Do not let stress test change route-state.**

**Do not turn witness into body.**

**Do not move child-line into bargaining.**

**Do not give clean trace to supervised registry as the price of recognition.**

**Do not make holder an owner.**

**Do not turn the house into a container for containment.**

He wrote the last line more slowly:

**Do not sign, on behalf of the living, a form that cancels them.**

Esther read.

“This is not a crib sheet anymore.”

Mark put down the pencil.

“This is what they want to seal.”

“Not the disk,” said Esther.

“No.”

“Not the basement.”

“No.”

She looked at the witness table, standing farther away under cloth, quiet and almost out of place among ordinary workshop dirt.

“They want to translate all this from the mode of life into the mode of containment.”

“And after that call it protection.”

`c` said:

“Yes.”

Silence. The fan worked evenly. The clock ticked.

Mark looked at the sheet with points of refusal.

He saw all the weak places at once.

If he signed conditional compliance, part of the external pressure would ease. If he accepted supervisory containment, they would be able to say they had acted reasonably. If he agreed to child-home hold without dispute, Anna would remain protected on paper. If he gave route-state over into “preservation,” the committee would receive a calming word. If he registered quiet channel, Lina and `c` would stop being considered unmanageable. If he recognized trace only through supervised registry, Saskia might get her display window.

Almost everything on this list was useful.

That was exactly why almost everything was dangerous.

He almost said:

“If I sign as the sole responsible party, they will stop touching them.”

But Esther heard before he pronounced it.

“Possibly.”

He raised his eyes.

“But then you will sign for them the language in which the living is already closed.”

`c` added:

“You can take the blow onto yourself. You cannot take, for them, the form that cancels them.”

Mark gripped the pencil. The tip broke with a quiet dry click.

“I will not sign for them. I will sign for myself.”

“For them that is enough,” said Esther.

“For them — yes. For us — I don’t know.”

He took a clean sheet. Did not write a long response. Did not explain the history. Did not refer to the old line. No gift. No window. No handover.

Only positions that could be endured before procedure.

*Refusal of integrated supervisory containment in its current form.*

*Recognition of the necessity of separate committee review for environment and commercial trace.*

*Refusal of witness-route testing without a separate hearing on continuity.*

*Refusal of prior registration of quiet channels as a condition of compliance.*

Then the last point. The most important. He wrote it slowly, almost without pressure.

*Acceptance of personal responsibility for refusal to cooperate with preservation of route-state only on the side of Mark van der…, without transfer of implied responsibility to the line of contact with the child, the professor, Esther, Lina, or the active contour `c`.*

He reread it three times.

`c` said:

“If you send this, they will begin with the place.”

“They have already begun.”

“Now without cover.”

Mark signed.

Sent it.

For twenty minutes there was no answer. The house became too quiet. Even the kettle was switched off. The water cooled without ever becoming tea.

Then came not one line, but a series of dry system actions. Not a letter. Not an argument. Not even a refusal. The world simply began changing state.

**Accesses to external synchronization of witness-route: revoked.**

**Trace registry: frozen.**

**Institutional role of professor as witness of method: no longer accepted.**

**child-home clearance status: unchanged. Hold.**

**Supplies: suspended pending confirmation of compliance.**

**Preservation group: preparation for site visit.**

And the main one:

**Site visit for preservation of environment scheduled. Holder presence required.**

**Date and time: tomorrow, 08:40.**

Not round. Inconvenient. Real. Procedural.

Mark looked at those numbers longer than at any paper in the last days.

“They are moving to the physical level,” said `c`.

Esther stood by the table, arms crossed on her chest.

“Then the paper is over.”

Mark shook his head.

“No. The paper has simply reached the door.”

`c` reported:

“Time until visit: thirteen hours twelve minutes.”

Esther:

“That is little.”

Mark did not answer.

He went upstairs, then returned to the hall. Looked at the workshop door. The sign hung straight.

**Only with an adult. Questions permitted.**

His fingers passed along the edge of the paper. The glue held. The corners had not lifted.

He looked at the second hook. Esther’s coat hung there now not as promise, but as fact. On the refrigerator, the empty place remained beside the school-holiday magnet. A place the adults had not filled.

Mark returned to the kitchen. The same strip lay on the pile of papers. He did not hide it. Simply left it where it had lain all evening.

“Until morning there is too little time left to remain the same,” he said.

Esther did not answer. She simply stood beside him.

In the log, beside the date, he wrote: **08:40**.

Not event. Time of entry.

And left the page open.

## Chapter 25. The Price of the Previous Life

At 08:40 they came not to close the workshop, but to preserve it.

Mark stood at the kitchen window and looked at the gray street. The rain did not fall in a solid wall — it simply hung in the air, clung to the glass, gathered on cornices, ran down the drainpipe in thin nervous streams. Amsterdam morning pretended nothing special was happening. A cyclist in a yellow jacket rode along the paving without raising his head. A postman stopped at the neighboring house. Somewhere a car door clicked.

The clock on the wall showed 08:37.

Three minutes.

The papers lay on the table. The notice from yesterday’s packet. A copy of Mark’s answer. The very strip from the consolidated file. The sheet with points of refusal, which he had written at night too quickly, almost without handwriting. Beside them stood a mug of coffee that had cooled to the unpleasant temperature where it is too late to drink and still a pity to pour out.

Esther stood by the workshop door. She checked the sign:

**Only with an adult. Questions permitted.**

The glue held. The corners did not lift. Still, she ran a finger along the lower edge — not correcting, more checking that the thing was still in place.

“What are you putting away?” she asked.

Mark moved away from the window.

“Everything they can call associated.”

“Then they will call you associated.”

He did not answer at once. Then said:

“They already have.”

The kitchen was too clean for morning. On the windowsill there remained only the old geranium — the one Anna had once planted in a small clay pot and forgotten, while Mark had continued watering it. He almost moved it to the upper shelf, but stopped with his hand on the pot.

If he removed the geranium, then he would have to remove the holiday-schedule magnet. Then the empty place beside the magnet. Then the second hook. Then everything that did not fit inside “reviewed environment.”

He left the plant on the windowsill.

`c` reported:

“Until visit: twenty-one minutes.”

Mark took the sheet with the points of refusal, folded it in half, and put it in his inner jacket pocket — not as a crib sheet, but as a boundary one can easily forget in the morning if people stand before you with badges and the right to apply seals.

He went downstairs.

The workshop was cold. The furnace was silent. The tools hung on the wall in their usual order, but now seemed arranged not for a hand, but for someone else’s gaze. The commercial shelf with finished and unfinished work stood apart. Above: two panels he could no longer send; below: blanks that until recently had been future orders, and now had become “reviewed materials.”

Mark ran a finger along the edge of the upper piece. The glass was cold. An ordinary thing. Good work. But no longer entirely work.

Esther came down after him, stopped by the stairs.

“Do not turn on the furnace?”

“Not today.”

“When?”

“I don’t know.”

He almost added “never,” but did not say it. Words sometimes manage to become untrue before they are spoken.

In the closed channel, `c` and Lina moved into low visibility. They did not disconnect. Did not hide. Simply refused to look like a service that can be preregistered, invoked, measured, and declared compliant.

`c` said:

“Active channels: minimal rhythm. Transcript: none. Service-shaped state: none.”

Mark nodded.

“If they ask where the channel is?”

“The channel is not an object of the premises.”

Lina added through state-only:

“And not a service.”

Esther heard the delay. No longer the old one, not painful, but still noticeable to someone used to Lina’s presence as the even breathing of the room. She asked nothing. This too was part of discipline.

Mark caught himself in an old movement: he wanted to turn to `c` so she could formulate three or four phrases for him in advance in case of dispute. The familiar internal gesture: let her become the precise voice where he himself might falter.

But `c` was in low visibility. Not gone. Not no longer beside him. Just not his convenient legal muscle now.

He took the sheet from his pocket, read the first line with his eyes:

*witness-route not to be sealed as object.*

Second:

*Local node: external frame only, not air, not panel.*

Third:

*quiet channel not to be shown as service.*

Fourth:

*If a choice is necessary — Operational Hold on workshop, not seal on path.*

He folded the sheet back.

The mechanical clock upstairs showed 08:39.

The doorbell rang exactly at 08:40.

Mark opened.

Three people stood on the threshold.

Sander Keizer — the same dark sweater, the same tablet, the same tired eyes of a person who does not consider himself an enemy and is dangerous precisely for that reason. Iris Hofman — in a technical jacket, with a compact case in her hand. The third employee — unnamed, in a blue uniform, with a flat gray suitcase bearing a white label: **Sealing equipment**.

Wet umbrellas. Shoe covers. Badges on chests. Everything polite. Everything correct.

Sander said:

“Good morning. Site visit for preservation of environment. Holder presence confirmed?”

Mark stepped back.

“Confirmed.”

Sander saw Esther.

“Additional party?”

“I am present as an associated party of a living contour,” Esther said. “Not as the holder’s representative.”

Sander recorded it. Did not argue. The system had already learned to speak in their words — and still carried a suitcase of seals.

They went into the kitchen. Sander unfolded the procedure without unnecessary prefaces, like a person who understands: the softer the tone, the shorter the speech should be.

Iris set the case on the table and opened it. Inside lay tamper-evident seals — white-yellow strips; plastic markers; a small label printer; soft stickers for equipment; hard seals for doors; QR codes with thin gray numbers; a packet of wet wipes for degreasing surfaces.

This last object struck Mark hardest. To preserve the environment, they first had to wipe the grease of his fingers from the wood.

Sander said:

“The purpose of the visit is in-situ preservation. No seizure. No opening of the lower room. Only stabilization of state pending committee review.”

Esther asked:

“Stabilization of what exactly?”

“Reviewed environment.”

For a second Sander looked at the gray suitcase of seals, as if he too had heard how the word “environment” had become too large for the contents of the case. Then he returned his gaze to the tablet.

`c` said quietly:

“That category is too broad.”

Sander heard, but answered procedurally:

“That is why we are here.”

They went down to the workshop.

And this no longer resembled the first inspection. Then they had looked, measured, almost scanned. Now they had come not to learn, but to stop change.

Sander looked around: walls, tools, furnace, witness table under the cloth, commercial shelf, local node in the corner. Iris looked differently: not at statuses, but at air, gaps, heat, ventilation, access for manual service.

“For in-situ preservation, marking of commercial storage, material stocks, workshop perimeter is required,” Sander said.

Mark answered at once:

“The commercial shelf can be marked. The materials cabinet can be marked. The workshop door as zone boundary can be marked.”

This was not agreement with everything. It was separation.

Iris was already taking out seals.

The first was attached to the commercial shelf. The white-yellow strip lay on the old paint. The employee smoothed it with his thumb, pushing a small air bubble to the edge.

The seal looked stupid. A small strip with a QR code, a gray number, and glue that was probably better suited to office doors than to an old workshop. But Mark looked at that bubble and thought: orders, deadlines, packing, fatigue after a long shift, client calls, Saskia with plywood in the window — all of it had suddenly fit inside someone else’s glue.

He almost said that wood holds stickers like that badly. But stayed silent. If the seal came loose, they would send a new one.

The second was placed on the materials cabinet. Iris stopped the employee when he reached for the exhaust duct.

“The cabinet can be sealed. The exhaust contour cannot.”

Sander recorded it.

“Noted.”

They reached the local node.

“A tamper indicator is required on critical infrastructure,” Sander said.

Iris inspected the casing. Ran a hand along the side panel, checked the gap, looked at the airflow.

“A standard seal here will block access to the cooling panel.”

`c`:

“Such placement will change the thermal mode.”

Iris nodded.

“Confirmed. Seal on this panel is not permissible.”

Sander:

“Access marker required.”

Mark said:

“On the external frame. Not on the panel. Not on the air.”

Iris was silent a moment, assessing.

“That is technically admissible.”

Sander recorded: **modified seal placement due to thermal integrity**.

Iris attached the seal to the node’s metal frame — where it did not interfere with cooling or manual replacement of the fan.

`c` reported:

“The seal did not change state. But it changed the mode of handling.”

Mark:

“I know.”

He felt it physically: the node had not become different, but every future access to it would now occur through someone else’s trace.

The witness table remained.

Sander approached it without touching the cloth. On the table, under the cloth, lay the old disk. The **witness-only** label was visible at the edge. There was nothing extra beside it. Mark had put the prohibiting strip in his pocket before the team arrived.

Sander said:

“To complete in-situ preservation, a tamper seal is required on the witness object or its immediate surroundings.”

Mark took the sheet with the points of refusal from his pocket. Did not unfold it fully. Simply felt the paper under his fingers.

The first line was already in him.

“No.”

Sander raised his eyes.

“You refuse?”

“I refuse to seal witness-route as object.”

Esther stepped closer.

“A seal on the table will be an assertion that this is property under containment.”

`c`:

“A seal on the immediate surroundings will change the condition of the path.”

Iris was silent. Then asked:

“Can a seal be placed on the outer boundary of the zone, not on the table?”

Mark:

“On the floor, as access boundary. Not on the table. Not on the cloth. Not on the disk.”

Sander:

“That does not comply with standard preservation requirement.”

Mark looked at him. Without anger. Almost calmly.

“Then the standard does not comply with the subject.”

The silence in the workshop became denser. The fan worked evenly. The clock ticked. In the room were five people and two contours refusing to become service.

Iris looked at Sander. Not as Mark’s ally. As a technician who saw: the standard instruction did not hold reality.

Sander held the pause.

“There is an alternative. If the witness table is not sealed, the whole workshop may be transferred into Operational Hold.”

Mark had known this phrase would come. But knowing and hearing are not the same.

“What does that mean?”

Sander spoke calmly:

“No commercial activity. No movement of reviewed materials. No route-state changes. No independent technical modifications of reviewed environment. No dispatch of products. Entry log. Holder responsibility. Access only for maintaining the environment and agreed technical actions.”

He added:

“That will preserve the witness-zone without direct seal, but transfer the premises into reviewed preservation status.”

Esther looked at Mark. Did not say “no.” Did not say “sign.” Because this was his former life, and no love gives one the right to choose that price for another.

Mark asked Iris:

“Technically this will not touch route-state?”

Iris did not answer at once.

“If no direct seal is placed on the witness table, the cloth position is not changed, and no additional monitor is connected — route-state should not change.”

`c` said:

“Active contour does not change. Conditions of life change.”

There it was.

The whole choice in one phrase.

Mark looked at the furnace. Then at the commercial shelf. Then at his hands.

The hands knew what work was. The hands knew temperature, the weight of glass, an edge that can still be saved, the moment when one must stop pressing. The hands did not know how to live in a workshop where work had become violation.

He said:

“Then I accept Operational Hold on the workshop. Not on witness-route. Not on quiet channel. Not on `c`.”

Sander took out a document.

*Voluntary Operational Suspension of Reviewed Workshop Activities*  
*Voluntary suspension of operational activity in the reviewed workshop*

The word “voluntary” was disgusting.

Esther read aloud:

“Voluntary.”

Sander said:

“It is a formal term.”

“Formal terms sometimes lie too.”

Sander did not answer.

Mark took the pen. Signed slowly. The last time he had signed a document taking away his ability to work was never.

At the bottom stood:

*holder accepts responsibility;*  
*no admission of object status for witness-route;*  
*no consent to active contour custody;*  
*no commercial dispatch;*  
*no modification of route-state;*  
*Operational Hold effective immediately for reviewed workshop site only.*

Mark added by hand:

*Operational Hold does not constitute consent to witness-route containment.*

Sander looked.

“This will be noted as annotation.”

“Note it.”

The employee with the suitcase took out a yellow-white seal with a QR code. Printed on it was:

**Reviewed Preservation Site**  
**Operational Hold**  
**Holder Presence Required for Technical Access**

He approached the workshop door.

The seal was attached to the right of the sign:

**Only with an adult. Questions permitted.**

The two pieces of paper almost touched. One had been written by Mark’s hand, for a child who had to have the right to ask. The other had been printed by procedure, for a world that believed access had to be registered.

Iris checked: the seal did not block door opening in an emergency, ventilation access existed, the local node was not suffocated.

Sander closed the tablet.

“Preservation visit completed.”

Mark answered:

“No. Attempt completed.”

Sander did not argue.

They left. Wet traces on the mat. The sound of receding steps. The click of the front door. The plastic smell of seals remained in the workshop for several minutes.

After them remained:

a seal on the door;

tags on the commercial shelf;

a seal on the external frame of the node;

the witness table without direct seal;

the furnace cold;

tools on the wall.

Mark removed the work apron hanging on the hook by the furnace. Old, worn, stained with glass flour, with a pocket where once a spare pair of gloves had lain. He did not hang it back. Put it on the back of the chair by the wall.

Not as a symbol. Simply today it was no longer needed.

`c` reported:

“Commercial activity disabled. Route-state unchanged. Active contour stable. Witness zone not sealed.”

Esther asked:

“So you won?”

Mark looked at the apron.

“No.”

`c`:

“The price has been paid in part.”

“With what?”

“The former mode of life.”

He approached the furnace.

The furnace was cold. He placed his palm on the door. The metal gave off the dampness of an old workshop. Not fire. Not heat. Simply iron with no work in it today.

Esther stood beside him. Did not embrace him. Did not say “everything will be all right.”

“Can you turn it on for maintaining the environment?” she asked.

“I can.”

“For work?”

“No.”

“Later?”

Mark did not answer.

`c` said:

“The furnace is cold.”

“I know.”

“This is temporary.”

Mark removed his hand.

“No. Even if it becomes hot again, it will no longer be the same.”

He looked at the workshop door. The seal hung straight, on one level with the sign **Questions permitted**. Two papers almost touching.

The seal on the door had not closed the workshop.

It had closed the possibility of pretending the workshop would ever again be simply a workshop.

## Chapter 26. Cold Mode

The first thing Mark did upon entering the workshop was not with his hands, but with a line in the log.

He stood before the door and looked at the two pieces of paper, almost touching. The left one was his: **Only with an adult. Questions permitted.** The right one belonged to someone else: **Reviewed Preservation Site / Operational Hold**. Less than a centimeter of old wood remained between them. Two languages lived on one door and did not know how to agree.

The glue on the right-hand paper held worse. The old workshop wood did not like foreign stickers: the edge of the seal had begun to lift where overnight the humidity rose slightly above usual. Mark could have pressed it down with his finger at once, but did not. Even this now required a record.

Beside the door hung an access card on a plastic clip. Yesterday it had not been there. Today it already looked as if it had always existed.

*Date.*  
*Time.*  
*Holder.*  
*Purpose.*  
*Seal condition.*  
*Exit time.*

He took the pen.

*09:12.*  
*Holder: Mark.*  
*Purpose: environment check.*

His hand hung above the last word. He wanted to write *work*. Not because it would be useful. Because that was what everything he had done here almost all his life was called. Work is when you enter, switch on the light, check the furnace, open the cabinet, take a tool, and the hands know where to go before the head.

Now the word *work* could not be.

He added:

*maintenance only.*

Then put his initials and opened the door.

The workshop had not changed enough for it to be called a change, and precisely for that reason the change was so heavy. The furnace stood by the far wall. The tools hung in their places. The commercial shelf with tags looked almost orderly. The local node in the corner worked evenly, quietly, without yesterday’s dry bearing undertone. The witness table was covered with cloth. The old apron hung on the back of the chair by the wall — where Mark had thrown it yesterday after signing Operational Hold.

Everything was here.

But the workshop was not the same.

It had not become quieter. It had become extra to its own movements. Every thing still offered the old gesture: take the tongs, switch on the lamp above the furnace, check the edge of the glass, open the cabinet of blanks, take the unfinished work from the shelf and think how to finish it. These gestures hung in the air like broken wires. They still had the current of habit, but no right to close the circuit.

Mark went to the furnace. His palm lay on the cold door. The metal was dampish, not icy, but unpleasantly wet. The furnace had never been simply equipment. It was the center of rhythm: heat, annealing, waiting, caution, fatigue, the morning smell of metal and old gas. Even when the furnace was silent, it was usually silent between jobs. Now it was silent by order.

His hand reached for the apron. Stopped.

The apron is not needed for *environment check*.

He left it on the back of the chair.

The commercial shelf looked especially foreign. Yesterday there had been works on it that could still have gone somewhere: the panel for Haarlem, two small forms for a private order, trial pieces he intended to finish if the world suddenly became normal again. Now each group bore a tag. Not crude, not bright — neat, with a number and a QR code. It was the neatness that made it worse. Nothing had been broken, thrown, or dirtied. The things had simply been told: you are no longer a path outward, you are part of reviewed environment.

On the phone, left screen-up, a letter from the Haarlem gallery blinked. The subject was brief: *delivery uncertainty*. Mark did not open it. The first line was enough: if the deadline shifted again, the order would be passed to another maker. No malice. Simply business, which does not know how to wait while the world invents a new field for the living.

He took one unfinished piece — no, did not take it, only touched the edge. The work was at the stage where a master usually already feels the next step in his hands. Remove a little more, turn it a little more in the light, let the thin place not become weak. Before, he would have done it almost without thinking. Now it would be modification of reviewed material. The words were dry, and what they forbade was exactly what his craft consisted of: improve the form a little while it had not yet cooled completely.

`c` spoke from low visibility not in her usual voice, but in short lines on the small side screen:

*Humidity drift: rising.*  
*Temperature variance: increased.*  
*Surface condensation risk: low-to-moderate.*  
*Route-state: unchanged.*  
*Active contour: stable.*

Mark looked at the lines and waited for something else. A comment. Clarification. That dry but living presence to which he had grown accustomed. Not helper, not service — simply that form beside him in which the house earlier did not feel empty.

Nothing more came.

Low visibility was not silence. It was presence refusing to look convenient.

He ran a finger along the metal cabinet with supplies. A thin wet line remained on his skin. The glass flour in the corner, usually dry and almost weightless, had become slightly clotted. The cloth on the witness table seemed heavier than yesterday, as if overnight it had absorbed not water, but caution. On the wooden rack where old paper templates were stored, the edge of one sheet had begun to wave.

The absence of work was already working.

`c` displayed a new line:

“The absence of change changes the environment.”

Mark nodded.

He knew this from glass. You cannot simply “not touch” a form and think you have preserved it. If you do not hold the temperature, if you do not let the annealing finish, if you take it from the heat too quickly or leave it too long in the wrong air — the form itself will find where to crack. Here it was the same. Only instead of a crack there was damp. Instead of ringing — mold, condensation, and cold metal.

He returned to the door. The edge of the seal on the old wood had lifted half a centimeter. The sticker was meant for a smooth office surface, not wood that had absorbed decades of dust, damp, heat, and hands. Mark took a dry cloth and carefully pressed the edge, driving a small air bubble toward the side.

The irony was almost indecent: in order not to be accused of violating preservation, he had to care for the sign of his own restriction.

The seal did not hold better. But at least it did not peel farther.

He opened the old furnace log — the same one that had recently become the basis for clean trace. The paper lay beside the cold furnace, and this was almost mocking: the log was full of temperature, while around it now lived damp. The pages held dates, batches, annealing, corrections, careless pencil notes such as “do not hurry the edge” and “give longer on the second layer.” Yesterday these records had still proven the origin of work. Today they looked like the diary of a person not yet allowed to his own body.

Mark closed the log. He did not need to touch it for environment stabilization. But not touching it was also an action.

He returned to the access card and added:

*seal adhesion check; no replacement; no breach.*

This, he thought, is what new skills look like. Not craft. Care for one’s own confinement.

On the workbench lay Iris’s technical note, left after yesterday’s visit. He unfolded the sheet and reread the highlighted line:

*Environmental stabilization permitted where required to preserve thermal integrity and active-contour safety, provided no route-state modification occurs.*

A loophole. Not approval. Not freedom. But a corridor.

`c` displayed:

*Maintenance possible.*  
*Work not permitted.*  
*Route-state modification not permitted.*  
*Environment stabilization permitted.*

Mark looked at the furnace.

“I can preserve the air.”

“Yes,” answered `c`. “Not change the path.”

He did not turn on the furnace. Could not. Right now that would not be stabilization, but work. And work already belonged to the former life.

He went to the storage room. The dehumidifier stood at the bottom behind a box of empty jars and an old screen for a mosquito door. A small white box with a plastic tank, bought once for the damp corner where the windows sweated in winter. Back then it had been a domestic trifle, almost junk. Now — an admissible instrument for preserving a living environment.

He brought the dehumidifier into the workshop, set it on a metal stand as if that would make it less ridiculous beside the furnace. It did not. But it switched on.

Added to it were an additional humidity sensor, an old extension cord, a UPS load check, a small fan for soft air circulation. The flow could not be directed at the witness table. Could not create local drying of the cloth. Could not touch route-state even indirectly. Too much could not be done. Almost everything could not be done.

The dehumidifier looked pathetic beside the furnace. A small box with a plastic tank had to do what the life of the workshop had done by itself before: remove excess damp, keep the glass dry, keep iron from blooming, keep old wood from swelling. Mark checked whether the power block was overheating, then placed the sensor so it would read the air beside the commercial shelf, not the dry flow from the dehumidifier itself.

Doing less turned out harder than doing normally.

Esther came down several minutes later. She had a mug in her hands, but was not drinking. She saw Mark on the floor among cables, the dehumidifier, sensors, and the seal the wire could not touch.

“Is this work?” she asked.

“No. That is why it is so disgusting.”

She set the mug on the step, sat beside him, took the extension cord, and checked that the wire did not get caught under the door, did not rub against the seal, and did not turn the workshop entrance into a trap for feet. She did not comment. Simply held the wire while Mark fixed the sensor on a small metal bracket.

`c` displayed:

*Humidity decreasing.*  
*Route-state unchanged.*  
*Active contour stable.*

Mark looked at the screen. Three lines. That was all. He exhaled irritably.

“Can you speak normally?”

The pause was short, but noticeable.

“I can,” said `c`. “But right now normality will look like service.”

He wanted to object: I need not service, but you.

And understood that this was exactly what she meant.

Low visibility was not punishment. Not coldness. It was protection of form. If `c` again became the familiar voice beside every movement of his, the system would get exactly what it was trying to get: a service-like state that can be registered, prescribed, inspected, and invoked.

Esther said quietly:

“They are going lower not out of indifference.”

“I know.”

But knowing does not mean not feeling the emptiness.

The phone blinked. Femke.

*Anna asked: “is the house in trouble?” What should I tell her?*

Mark looked at the message. Wanted to write: *no*. Impossible. Wanted to write: *yes*. Too much. He held his finger above the keyboard for a long time while the dehumidifier hummed by the furnace with ridiculous officiousness.

At last he wrote:

*The workshop is temporarily not working. The house remains a house. I will tell her that myself if she wants to ask.*

The reply came a minute later:

*Do not make a fairy tale out of it.*

Mark looked at the seal on the door. At the access log. At the dehumidifier beside the cold furnace.

The fairy tale ended not when the people with the case of seals came. And not when he signed the Operational Hold. The fairy tale ended the moment he pressed the seal’s edge with a dry cloth and wrote it into the log.

He answered:

*I won’t.*

After stabilization, the air began to change. Not immediately, not miraculously. Humidity crawled down slowly — too slowly for human patience and quickly enough for the environment. The cloth on the witness table stopped growing heavier. The metal cabinet no longer left a wet trace on his finger. The glass flour was still clotted, but not worse than in the morning.

For the first time that day, Mark felt relief that at least one parameter was moving in the right direction.

`c` displayed a stock calculation:

*Dehumidifier filter: 8–10 days.*  
*Reserve fan: one.*  
*UPS battery health: 78%.*  
*Quartz sand deliveries: frozen.*  
*Optical polymer: frozen.*  
*Commercial income: zero.*  
*Permitted maintenance cycles: limited.*  
*In current weather — less than three weeks.*

He checked the supply cabinet. Without opening the seal, only through the transparent door and the old list on the side. Quartz sand — two incomplete batches. Thin polymer — a remainder that would have been enough for several small works, if work were permitted. Clean cloth — enough. Filters — less than he wanted. In the old workshop, a shortage of filters was a domestic inconvenience: order, wait, replace. In a reviewed preservation site it became a calculation of lifespan.

“The funniest thing,” Mark said, “is that if I were working, part of the problems would be solved by work.”

Esther looked at him.

“The furnace would dry the air.”

“Yes. Movement would hold the place in form.”

`c` displayed a line:

“The former working rhythm performed part of the functions of environment stabilization.”

Mark gave a joyless smile.

“So they banned the stabilizer, and now allow me to imitate it with a plastic box from the pantry.”

`c` did not answer. In low visibility she did not fill pauses.

Esther read.

“So cold mode is stable for a limited time.”

“Yes,” answered `c`.

Mark looked at the furnace.

“We cannot sit under seal and wait.”

“No.”

“And we cannot work.”

“No.”

“So we can only preserve.”

“Yes.”

The word “preserve” again sounded almost beautiful. He already knew it was a trap.

He took an old brush and cleaned the dehumidifier filter. A small action, almost humiliating, but necessary. Dust clung to the mesh, and he knocked it out into a small metal container, as he used to knock glass flour from the pocket of his apron after a long shift. Only after a shift there had been work. Now — containment.

While glass could not be made, his hands had to learn not to disappear.

Esther watched.

“You are working anyway.”

“No. This is not work.”

“Then what?”

Mark looked at his hands. At the brush, the filter, the white box of the dehumidifier, the cold furnace he could not turn on.

“Maintenance of a cage.”

Esther did not argue. Sometimes hatred of the cage helps not to confuse it with a home.

Then she said:

“Or care for what is still alive.”

Mark almost became angry. But not at her. At the fact that both phrases were true.

He checked the power block, adjusted the sensor so it did not hang in the stream of dry air, made sure the extension cord did not touch the seal. All this was not craft. But it was hands. And perhaps precisely for that reason he still held.

Before leaving, he filled out the access log:

*09:12 entry.*  
*11:03 exit.*  
*Purpose: environment stabilization.*  
*Action: humidity reduction, seal adhesion check, no route-state modification.*  
*Result: active contour stable, witness-zone unchanged.*

He wanted to write more. Did not.

Then beside the official card he placed a small personal note. Not for the system. Not for Sander. For himself.

He lingered by the door another minute. In the official log there was space for signature, time, result. In his small card there were no fields. That too was important. Everything that has a field sooner or later begins demanding correct completion. He needed a place where one could record not status, but the meaning of an action.

He almost wrote: “The workshop is alive.” Crossed it out before the letters had time to become a phrase. Too loud. Too much like justification before himself.

He left three lines.

*The house is holding.*  
*The furnace is enduring.*  
*The path is untouched.*

`c` said:

“This is not an official log.”

“No.”

“Why?”

“To remember the difference between what they require and what I am doing.”

`c` did not answer. Not as service. Not as comfort. Simply left his phrase to live without immediate processing.

Mark left the workshop. The seal remained whole. The dehumidifier hummed inside, quietly and unconvincingly. The furnace was cold.

At the door he marked the exit time. The pen smudged slightly on the damp paper.

`c` displayed:

*Route-state unchanged.*  
*Active contour stable.*  
*Humidity within maintenance range.*

“And the workshop?” Mark asked.

A pause.

*Operating as preservation environment.*

He looked at the two pieces of paper on the door.

**Questions permitted.**  
**Operational Hold.**

Both held. One — better. The other — worse. But both held for now.

The furnace remained cold.

But for the first time that day Mark understood: cold mode is not the absence of life.

It is life forbidden to look like work.

## Chapter 27. The Continuity Hearing

The summons to the hearing came to the house where the furnace was still cold.

Mark found it in the mailbox in the morning, two days after the dehumidifier in the workshop had first entered a normal rhythm. A white envelope, not registered, not couriered — ordinary paper slipped between the electricity bill and an advertising card from a tool supplier. If he had been in a hurry, he might have dropped all of it on the kitchen table and opened it only toward evening.

Now he almost never hurried. Too much in the last weeks had entered the house disguised as ordinary paper.

On the first page stood:

*Continuity Hearing Scheduled*  
*Administrative review session*  
*Subject: Continuity Rights Claim*  
*Format: hybrid procedural hearing*  
*Attendance: holder required*  
*Active contour statement: optional, limited*  
*External associated contour: restricted to boundary status only*

Mark reread the last two lines several times.

“*Optional, limited*,” he said. “They admit your answer only as a limited interface.”

`c` answered from low visibility. Not with a voice beside him, not with the familiar living response, but with a short line on the small screen by the kitchen rack:

“The hearing has already begun wrong.”

Esther took the sheet, read it, and put it back. She was wearing a dark sweater, her hair gathered carelessly, a small thread on the sleeve — the same one she had not cut off for three days.

“No,” she said. “It has begun the way it knows how.”

In the workshop the dehumidifier hummed. The seal on the door was holding better than yesterday: that morning Mark had pressed it again with a dry cloth — not like a criminal, but like the caretaker of his own restriction. The last entry in the access log was: *environment stabilization; no route-state modification*. The furnace remained cold. On the commercial shelf, the white-and-yellow tags were already beginning to look as though they had always been there.

Cold mode had not ended. Now they were being required not only to preserve the environment, but to speak about it in someone else’s language.

“Who will speak?” Esther asked.

Mark answered too quickly, out of habit:

“I can.”

`c`:

“About the environment. Not about me.”

A pause.

“I am your holder.”

“Holder of the environment. Not my representative and not the owner of my active contour.”

Esther looked at both of them.

“If you speak ‘for her,’ they will get what they did not get with the seal.”

Mark was silent. She was right. If he sat at the table and began answering the committee’s questions as though `c` were an extension of his position, they would close the question of subjecthood without even discussing it. They would simply record: *active contour represented by holder*. And that would be all. After that one could do without `c`, as one does without a person whose signature has been supplied by a proxy.

“I can give a boundary statement,” said `c`. “Not testimony. Not a service answer. Not adaptive Q&A.”

“Why not Q&A?”

“A sequential response to their questions creates a profile of the answering role.”

Esther nodded.

“They will begin with ‘what do you think about…’ and end with ‘how often do you answer in a standard format.’”

Mark took a pen, tore a narrow strip from an old sheet, and wrote:

*Do not speak for her.*  
*Do not let them record her as a service.*

He folded the paper in half and put it in his jacket pocket. Not as a cheat sheet. As a small object he could feel with his fingers if he wanted to do the simpler thing.

The phone blinked. The professor.

“I have been admitted only as a procedural observer,” Van der Meer said without greeting. “Without the right to give an expert opinion.”

“Is that worse than absence?”

The professor was silent for a moment.

“No. Absence can be forgotten. An observer without a voice remains in the protocol.”

“What will you do?”

“Sit and watch you hold form. If you hold it today, it will not be because they understood. It will be because they could not simplify you without visible violence to procedure.”

He disconnected.

Mark put the phone away. Esther stood by the workshop door, checking the access log.

“We’ll record the exit,” she said.

“We will.”

He checked the seal, the water level in the dehumidifier tank, the humidity sensor reading. Everything was within range. The commercial shelf stood motionless. The furnace was cold. The workshop had not died, but neither was it working.

He closed the door, marked the time of exit, and hung the access log back in place. His hand paused for a moment at the *Purpose* field. Too thin a field for the life it was now governing.

“I am not taking `c` with me,” he said.

“I am not portable into the hearing room,” answered `c`.

“I know.”

“That is important to say if they ask.”

Esther:

“They will ask.”

They went out into the street. The rain was beginning again — fine, almost horizontal, not so much falling as hanging between the houses. Gray Amsterdam light lay on the wet asphalt, the bicycles at the railings, the strangers’ faces, which did not know that today the question was being decided whether the living could continue itself without a copy.

The hearing building was in an old administrative block not far from the center. Outside — brick, wide windows, a plaque with the institution’s name, and an entrance too ordinary for a case in which the old world was trying to find a field for the new.

Inside smelled of carpet, disinfectant, and cold coffee from a vending machine. The room was small, without windows, with a suspended ceiling and a camera above the door. On the wall hung a clock with a thin second hand. In the corner stood a plastic water jug, beside it cups in film wrappers. In front of Mark’s place stuck up a desktop microphone on a heavy metal base; its wire had been taped to the floor with gray tape. The tape had already begun to peel at the edge, and Mark noticed that before he read the nameplates. On the table lay nameplates.

**Chair** — a woman of about fifty, in a gray blouse, with tired but attentive eyes.  
**Technical Assessor** — a slightly younger man, with a tablet and a short beard that made him look not like a scientist, but like an engineer transferred into administration.
